DANGEROUS THROAT TROUBLES.

Peps Your Safeguard In All

Weathers.

The throat is very susceptible to changing temperatures, and becomes an easy prey for in- fectious germs. The best safeguard against these perils is to dissolve one or two Pepg tablets in the mouth at intervals during the day, and particularly before exposure to inclement weather.

The fumes given off by the dissolving Peps are breathed in and bathe every part of the throat with an antiseptic vapour which destroys and repels any germ attack. Coughs, colds and sore throat are infectious, and particularly dangerous in households when diphtheria, influenza, or other epidemic disease is about. Regular use should be made of these germi- cidal Peps every day. They are a real throat disinfectant, and more effective than German- owned tablets. Moreover, Peps embody the new principle of conveying ,a healing and protective medicine to the lungs direct.

THE MEDICAL OFFICER.

Guardians' Chairman Gives Casting Vote For Dr. R. B. Sephton.

The appointment of a successor to the late Dr. R. Sephtons who was the 'medical officer of No. 3 District of the Warrington Union, and public vaccinator of the Rixton District, was the subject of a long discussion at a meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians on Wednes- day.

Three applications had been considered by the Finance Committee, and these were from Dr. R. B. Sephton (Cukheth), Dr. J. Bennett (Pad- gate), and Dr. W.J. Peacocke- (Warrington). At Wednesday's meeting- the committee recom- mended that Dr. R. B. Sephton should be ap- pointed. Mr. P. Longton suggested that before the Board appointed another doctor,. alterations should be made in the districts, and he pro- posed to that effect. Miss Broadbent seconded. The Rev. A. M. Mitchell: I myself have always taken exception to the fact that the Finance Committee makes these appointments. The Chairman (Mr. A. J. Jolley): Excuse me. The matter is referred to the Finance Com- mittee, and then is submitted to the Board for approval. The Rev. A. M. Mitchell: I maintain that we ought to have a special committee for these ap- pointments. I think it would be well for the Board to consider the advisability of making special arrangements. The doctor who is most accessible ought to be the man for the appoint- ment. Mr. Mitchell went on to say that he did not think the committee's recommendation was for the best. Dr. Sephton seemed to have his hands full with what he had to do already, and so far as his information went, Dr. Bennett was more accessible for the people. The Chairman pointed out that the Finarice Committee had not arrived at this decision without considering every point, including pub- lic interest and accessibility. Mr. Longton's amendment was then put -and declared lost. Mr. T. Dale moved that Dr. Bennett be ap- pointed. The Rev. A. 31. Mitchell seconded, and after a short discussion, the motion was put. Six voted for and six against, .satd the Chairman gave his casting vote in favour of the com- mittee's recommendation.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0016.txt | included=true | included =====
OPTICAL ILLUSIONS.

Rev. A. M. Mitchell On The Union's Bread And Butter.

Bread and butter are always interesting sub- jects, and therefore the Rev. A. M. Mitchell will be pardoned for raising them at the Warring- ton Guardians' meeting on Wednesday morning. He said that recently he happened to be in the Union Hospital when tea was being served, and he thought it was only fair to the Guard- ians and to the public to say that the bread and butter was most satisfactory, and the patients to whom he spoke expressed their satisfaction with it.

Mr. G. Mather: There is nothing new about that; it has been satisfactory for years and years.

The Chairman (Mr. A. J. Jolley) made a similar remark, which caused the Rev. A. M. Mitchell to comment that at times people suffered from optical illusions.

GUARDIANS AND THE LOCAL GOVERNMENT BOARD.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0017.txt | included=true | included =====
Suggestions For Cutting Down Labour

And Expenses.

A Special Committee of the Warrington Guardians have been considering a number of suggestions the Local Government Board had made with the object of cutting down expenses. At the Board meeting on Wednesday the corn- mittee reported that they made several recom- mendations with regard to decreasing the routine work of the Guardians, but they recom- mended that the Board should continue to meet fortnightly instead of monthly, as suggested by the Local Government Board.

Mr. A. J. Jolley, who was presiding, said the committee felt strongly that if the meetings were held monthly the work would accumulate, and it would mean longer meetings. There was also the danger that members would lose touch with the Board, and that the administration would fall on to the officials.

The course suggested by the Special Com- mittee was adopted.

ULCER AS BIG AS A TEA-CUP.

Only Zam-Buk Could Heal The

Terrible Sore.

From a Yorkshire home comes further striking proof of the value of Zana-Buk: ". Four years ago," said Mrs. M. Huggins, of -131, Hudders- field-road* Dewsbury, to a reporter, I felt an itching, burning sensation on my left leg..

"There seemed nothing to account for it, but in spite of different cooling lotions which I applied, it grew worse. A red angry looking patch then appeared and soon the skin broke. In a short time there was an ulcer on my leg nearly as big as a teacup, and the agony was something to remember. The wound was one mass of corruption and the discharge scarcely ever-ceased. It bled freely, too., but it was dark bluish unwholesome-looking blood.

' "-Doctors said it was a bad calie`of sulcerated leg, but in spite of constant care and attention the wound grew deeper, and deeper.' I took a long holiday, for the doctors told me absolute rest was necessary, but it was no use. "My leg was bad like this for three. years and I was in despair. Then I read of a splendid cure by Zam-Buk in a case very similar to inine and I got a box of till's wonderful balm. The first few dressings enabled me to get a decent night's rest, and as I kept on using Zam-Buk the discharge gradually cleared away and the inflammation went down.

"When Zam-Buk had quite healed the wound it formed new healthy skin and in time. there was' no sign whatever of the ulcer which 'had caused me .so much suffering and anxiety." Nothing but Zam-Buk with its -remarkable healing power could have made an impression on her bad case.

WIREDRAWERS REPUDIATE

TRADES eouNeIL.

Notable Secession Follows in Wake of Betrayal of Trust.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
FUTURE OPPORTUNITIES FOR LABOUR,

Resentment at the Trades and Labour hostility towards the Government's Military Service Bill occasioned the crowding of the Druids'- Hall, Sankey-street, on Saturday even- ing, by members of the Warrington District of the Amalgamated Wiredrawers' Society, who indignantly denounced the Council and resolved to 'sever connection with it. - This was done on the recommendation of the committee who had considered the question and called the meeting. To dissuade them, Mr. Charles Dukes, the secretary of the. Council, sent a letter, which, instead of assuaging their annoyance, inflamed it, and there were loud cries for it to be laid on the table or put on the fire. The Secretary (Mr. A. Siddall) followed the former course. The letter was as follows :- "Dear Sir and Brother,—' ment's Compulsion Bill.' "The Council's position on the above Bill is being misrepresented by people who do not understand the Trade Unionist position. There is a danger of difficulties arising by opponents of Trade Unions using the present crisis to split the movement. Trade Union differences should be settled inside the councils of our own party, and too much emphasis should, not. be attached to outside influence. We suggest that any branch not in agreement with the Council should, permit an Executive Committee member to attend a branch meeting and place before them the Council's position. "Now, above all time, is the time for unity. "Hoping'- your branch will accept this tug- gestion. On behalf of the Connell, "You for Labour, " CHARLES DUKES!'

.` Re the. Govern-

Not one spoke in favour of the Trades Coun- cil's attitude, though two or three thought they should not go so far as to withdraw from the Coune.l. They were weak voices, however, and, on the resolution being put, only five hands signified disagreement with severance.

black people. I consider our cause is just as sacred as theirs. (Hear, hear.) We .are fight- ing for the liberty of the people of Europe, especially for the liberty of small States. 'lhe have been trodden down and ground in the dust; they have been,treated far worse than any blacks :were, ever treated—and by•'a nation which calls itself a cultured race—and they use their culture to manufacture the most diabolical agencies of war. These agencies have been used to obliterate small nations. We are fighting on behalf of these nations, and the men who are opposing us ought to consider these things. I think it would have done some of them, good if they had been in Belgium and Serbia when the Germans went through—(applause)—if they had been treated in the same barbarous. manner. as the Belgians and Serbians have been treated: (Applause.) It is surprising to me that..any men can speak as they have spoken. It is absurd to think that they can carry on as they do, when the country is engaged in a death struggle with a despicable foe. (Applause.)

The taxes they would levy would crush us for a time; and how such a man as Dukes_, who reckons he is a learned man, can advocate such schemes, I'do not know. It behoves every trade unionist to do his utmost and Rut his back to the wall. If we do not win we shall not have the privilege of meeting here and discussing our wages and conditions, but we shall be subdued." In his opinion, the recommendation of the com- mittee should be supported tooth and nail. (Ap- plause.)

===== 0002601_19160129_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
Suppose We Do Not Win?

You know that we are not going on very well. We are simply holding the Germans, and we require every ounce of strength we have got and all the men we have got to win this war. (Applause.) What does it mean if we do not win? You know what the Germans are; they are unfair both in war and trade. You know that Trade Unions have a great deal at stake. With other trades, we have suffered long from the unfair competition of Germany. (Ap- plause.) They have got hold of our .trade marks, made inferior articles, and flooded the world with them, and sold them as being of the same quality as our own, and they have done their best to make our trade go to the dogs. These are considerations to make us all united and determined that we will win. (Loud cheers). In any case, it is better to die fighting in a good cause than to live a life of slavery, which•we shall do if the Germans win. There is no doubt about that, because they would make such terrible terms that we should never look up again. We have got to beat them, and it is going to be a terrible job, but if we do not do,it we shall suffer, and suffer very badly. Nearly, all of us have got either, sons or relatives fighting, and if we do not help them I consider we are traitors to lien' (Hear, hear.) If we allow them to go on fight- ing and fail to send them reinfoicements enough to fight through to a victorious end. I say we shall be betraying them and sending them to an untimely death. (Loud applause.) A Strong Feeling.

Mr. Baker rose a second time to say that there was a very strong feeling regarding the Trades Council, and he ,thought they would be justified in severing their connection with it.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0027.txt | included=true | included =====
AIR. W. HAIGH'S BEST INVEST- •MENT. Biblical Teaching.

Mr. Wilkinson Haigh delivered a characteris- tically trenchant and entertaining speech. , He began by remarking that he supported the com- mittee for the simple reason that it was to their ' benefit to secede. For a good number of years the Wiredrawers' Union had been one of the beet investments he had ever made. (Hear, hear.) If he had treated singly with employers he could not have got the favourable terms he had succeeded in getting; but, through being combined with fellow trade unionists, he had been able to maintain his own ground, and able to get suitable reward for his labour. That applied to all of them. (Applause.) "` Regard- ing the. Trades Council," he went on, "I might tell the meeting that one of our representatives on the Council would not face the music to- night. Ile is not here, and, maybe, a good job Ile is not. (Laughter anfl applause.) He has been taken to task in the shop at the White- cross Works. His name is John Haley—you will know that—(laughter)—and he reckons to excuse himself because the Old Book says that we should not kill. I will give him another pas- sage from the Old Book. It says, Thou shalt not covet.' (Laughter and applause.) Those folk only quote what suits them. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19160129_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
The Snub to the Recruiting Committee.

"Some time ago a difficulty arose through the recruiting officer in Warrington giving an invi- tation to the Trades Council to help them, and I am sorry to say that they ignored it with two or three lines. It was a kind of snub and slur to the recruiting officer. It struck us trade unionists as such, and we had a meeting about it, but things were smoothed over .for a time. They told us they were neutral at that time— neutral in all things. Well, we are here to- night to differ from them to a very great extent. (Applause.) The Red Flag.

"They are neutral on the one side, but as soon as there conies a meeting of the Trades Congress they send an individual of the name of Dukes with a full determination of voting against the Government—and of making a name for himself—(laughter)—but he found there were others there with the same object, and'he hadn't a chance-to speak—(laughter)—and all he could do was to join in singing 'The Red Flag.' (More laughter.) I gather that 'The Red Flag'_ was sung with gusto—if you will allow the term: I don't know whether it is right or not—(laughter) —but, to my reading, it seemed to satisfy him for his journey out. It does not satisfy us. (Applause.) He was never elected to the post to represent us as trade unionists, and never will be. He might have done a certain amount of good work in his own sphere for the Gas Workers and Labourers' Union, but he exceeded that 'when he tried to represent us, and boasted that he represented ten thousand trade union- ists of Warrington. I will bet a sovereign to a penny that he does not represent ten hundred. (Applause.) It is life or death with us as a society now, and if we do not secede from the Trades Council to-night- somebody will hear something about it." (Laughter and applause.)

(Cheers.) ===== 0002601_19160129_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CHAIRMAN AND DISLOYALTY. Trades And Labour Council's . Betrayal Of Trust.

The speeches throughout rang with patriotic feeling. The Chairman (Mr. F. A. Baker) led off with a speech which was at once, a lucid resume of the situation, an eloclunt exposition of their own case, and a strong denunciation of the Council. He said: As a representative body of wiredrawers we have met to discuss our attitude towards the Trades and Labour Council. We have been connected with that body for a con- siderable number of years. They have been use- ful in many respects, and -we have always given them credit for anything -they have done of a useful character. When they have done things that we disagreed with, we have tried to look over them and make every, allowance for indis- cretions, but we think that we can go on no longer, especially after their most recent expres- sion of disloyalty. They do not seem to realise our aims and aspirations, and we feel that it is altogether a betrayal of the trust we put in them, for they have come out on the side of what is detrimental to the country's interests, , (Hear, hear.) A Prior Offence.

Some time ago we had cause to complain of the way they treated Lord Derby's Recruiting Committee. They were asked to help recruiting and they absolutely refused to do anything of the sort. Owing to their action, we, as wire- drawers, are not represented on the Advisory Committee, and this is a great shame, for there will be questions before it from \ time to time when it would have been well if we had had a voice in the way they were dealt with. (Hear, hear.) We think that the Trades Council have not acted in a patriotic spirit in any sense. They have gone the wrong way about every- thing. They said they were neutral as regards the Lord Derby recruiting scheme. That is as good as saying "You and your recruiting com- mittee can go hang for all we care. We will have nothing to do with it." They are the only body in the town which has acted in such a dis- loyal manner. Every one else is anxious to do everything he can to help. We are going through a great crisis, probably one which history will never again record, and yet we have a body of men who do not seem to realise what the war.nieans. In this time of crisis we consider that Englishmen ought to .lc all they can in order to help on to victory. (Hear, hear.) Not Neutrai Now.

- - - Now that the Military Service Bill has been introduced, they are not neutral. They take up .direct opposition .to it. They are -ping to oppose it in every way they can. They did not wait to see whether it would be satisfactory and\ whether certain arrangements satisfactory to Trade Unionism would be made, but im- mediately said "We -will oppose it in every form." I contend that that is no reasonable attitude. If they had taken up •a different attitude to that they would have found that the Bill was quite satisfactory. for we have 'been assured by our Government that it does not mean we are going to have conscription for ever. It is only for -the duration of the war. The stand which the Trades Council have taken is altogether wrong. (Applause.) Trades Council Helping To Prussianise Us. Who is to blame for this Military Service Bill? It is men like those of the Trades Coun- cil. If they had helped in the recruiting schemes there would have been no necessity, for the Military Service Bill. They are the men who have brought it about, and they are doing all the complaining. If they had helped all along the line there would have been no occasion for it Mr. Asquith is not one to bring about conscription because he likes it, but because he sees that we cannot avoid it. None of us like. conscription—(apPlause)—but there are many worse things than a mild measure of compulsion of this sort. (Hear, hear.) I do not think we need worry about that. Englishmen are not going to be Prussianised so easily--(eheers)—but if mern'hold hack and wait for Germany. to beat us we shall be Prussianised. •There is no doubt about that. (Hear, hear.) By- their ..standing aloof over the recruiting scheme, and by not helping in any way to persuade men to join the Colours, the Trades and Labour Council are helping to Prussianise us, only they cannot see it. (Hear, hear.) Labour's Future Scope.

Now, there will be a great scope after this war for working men -to take a greater part in the working of this country. They are making great sacrifices now, and certainly we shall be entitled to a grealer voice in shaping the destinies Of the Empire. From what I can learn from every available source all recognise: that that will be so, but the working men will have to prove that they are woithy of it. (Applause.) These men who are opposing measures which make for bringing the war to a successful conclusion will find that their actions will recoil on their own heads, because they will be "spotted," and will never be trusted to do any prominent wqrk for tiae country. They must know, as we ila that if we cannot trust them in a twitter rf this sort, we cannot trust them in a matter of national importance like the government of the country. (A Voice: "Never!" and applause.) For the Liberation of Europe.

In the American Civil War the North Americans fought the Southern States in order to abolish slavery. The Northerners fought for several years for a noble object, not for any material advantage, but for the upraising of a

===== 0002601_19160129_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
„NO , ROOM FOR ” ORGANISERS "

AT PRESENT.

Mr. Joseph Bennion said that Mr. Dukes was an organiser, and as the Government were now doing the organising, he was out of a job. Some one else had got it, and the best thing he could do was to don khaki. "He ought to have been in the first group," said Mr. Bennion, amid laughter and applause. _

MR. NATHAN COOK AND . SOCIALISTS. Mr. Nathan Cook said that at one time the , Trades Council was one of the greatest powers , in Warrington, with representatives to whom they could look up and who treated the mem- bers with respect. The Council were now put- ting too much emphasis on "the workers," and practically neglecting the Trade Unionists, who, inaugurated the Trades and Labour Council. From a personal standpoint 'he thought it was time he severed his connection with the War- rington Trades Council—for several reasons. Some time ago there were three elections in Warrington. He did not object ter them putting up three men, but they knew the calibre of the imen who stood as candidates. Then they came to the later period of the recruiting stage. Then they knew the feeling of the Council. Now when they came to the compulsory question the Trades Council said what they .had done was perfectly right and perfeetly logical. He was going to challenge their attitude. (Applause.) The 10,000 Myth.. He took the "Warrington Examiner's for his authority that the representative appointed by The Warrington Trades' Council went to the conference at London and pledged his vote for 10,000 men. Ile denied that in toto. The only men that.the delegate represented were the men who sat on the Trades Council. "What right had he," asked the speaker, "what right has any man to take the freedom of my vote?o Ilts vote should have counted for 25 men and not 10,000." (Applause.) He (Mr. Cook) was of the opinion that the wiredrawers had a perfect right to sever their connection with the Trades Council , because this body had become too Socialistic. (Loud applause.) He was no Socialist—he was a British man, born and bred. If they took a vote of the whole of the men represented on the Trades and Labour Council, „he guaranteed that instead of representing 1ff,040 on this question they would not have 600 • men to vote for. (Hear, hear.) It was a complete myth, and there was no ground for the resolution of the Trades and Labour Council and the assertions it contained. That afternoon he (the speaker) had to get a .dictionary to see what Mr. Dukes had been talking about in the resolution. (Laughter.) There was an old adage which said "What- ever thy hand finds to do, do it with all thy might." So far as conscription was concerned, they were placed in this position: the Germans had got conscription, and England would have to use the same methods to -smash them. (Hear, hear.) Conscription was only for the duration of the war, and no man could object to that. In normal times he did not believe in conscription, but he believed that now it was necessary for the purpose they had in view—i.e., the winning of the war. ( Annlause.) How could they do anything less? On all sides, they heard of lads getting their, arms and legs blown off. They were sorry, tout it could not be helped in war time. As old as he was, he had been and offered himself, if only to go and mind a bridge —(applause)—and every man ought to do some- thing. What did the attitude of the Trades Council mean? It meant that they, were looking after their skins and looking at -the ouestion from a selfish point of view. He hoped that the meeting would make a determined protest and say they would not stand this hoodwinking any longer. (Applause.)

PRIVILEGES IVITiIOUT RESPONSI- BILITY.

"This inciNnt," said Mr. Haigh, "demonstrates , their inconsistency from beginning to end." (Hear, hear.)

REPRESENTATION OF LABOUR.

"Medium Course" Suggested.

Mr. Ezra Eden said that when he read the report of the Trades Council meeting he was filled with indignation as much as anybody, but he had made inquiries and found that the wire- drawers had had only one representative at the meeting. The Socialists attended to 'their busi- ness very ninch—and. they could not be blamed for doingso, but the wiredrawers did not. They only had one delegate out of .four present: The Military- Sertiee'Bill was absolutely necessary for this country to bring about victory. But if they were to sever their connection with the Trades Council they would 'completely cut themselves off from representatkon on any body, because the Trade. Council .was the only 'official body to reice the views of labour. ("Oh.').' Failing that, the Society: should secede from the Council. • "Don't let our hearts rum away with our heads, because labour has got to he represented in the future," and that is a medium course I should advocate," concluded the speaker. COUNCIL HELD, IN CONTEMPT.

Mr. Thomas Seed declared that the Trades and Labour Council was a hotbed of Socialism, and it was known to be held in contempt. He was astounded to read that at the conference the

delegates sang The Red Flag" so lustily. It ought to have been the, National Anthem. (Ap- plause.) For .some time past they had_ had Labour represented in the Government. There was a Labour man in the Cabinet and two others held minor positions. If "The Red Flag" Jmen had their way, these men would not be .there, and, then Labour wopld not be repre- sented. It was most important that Labour should be represented directly, and he only hoped the negotiations now going on would be successinl in keeping the three Labour .men in the Govprn- ment. At the' present time,' it was up to the wiredrawers to hold aloof from the Trades Coun- cil. It was held in contempt by all the public bodies of the town, and had been for some years. (Applause.)

DOES NOT REPRESENT THE

WIREDRAWERS.

Mr. William Broadhurst said that he was very pleased to see that other trade unionists were taking the step that was now suggested— especially in Yorkshire. When he read Mr. Charles Dukes' statement that he represented 10,000 workers, he said, to 'himself "Well, Charlie, thete, is one Aere—a trade unionist of about 44 years' standing—that you don't represent. (Hear, hear.) And I know of others of the same way of thinking as my- self." (Applause.) Because they could see through them. (Ap- plause.) PLEA FOR THE TRADES COUNCIL.

An explanation as to why some of the wire- drawers' delegates to the Trades Council did not altogether attend to their business came from Mr. F. Mills. He said he was unable to attend the Council's meetings because one week he was on night work and the other on day work. But he hardly agreed with the committee's recom- mendation with regard to severing their con- nection with the Trades Council. He knew that the latter,had•done a lot of good. At that meet- ing there had been a lot of criticism which should not have been levelled against the Coun- cil but against Mr. Haley. He was the man who had not properly represented the views of the society. If. he (the speaker) had been at the Council meeting he would have put the true, position of the wiredrawers before the meeting, and, consequently the Council would not have been unanimous in its action. The good work that the Trades Council had done was great. Spade work was done quietly, and nobody knew about it. Through them Labour was represented on public bodies. They were represented on the Education Committee by Mr. Plinston., who had done a good deal of work in seeing that the chil- dren were being educated in a proper manner. The Trades Council bad pulled many a Union out of the fire. Because he differed with them on one question it did not follow that he would differ with them on others. The unity of to-day was for the success of to morrow. It was tor these reasons why he was going to put his hand up against the resolution. BACK FROM THE FRONT.

Don't Run Away:

Mr. Harold Potter, who has been wounded at the Front, said there were a good many young men, who, before the war, studied things and were inclined to take Socialistic views. He was not ashamed to say that he was one of them. But a good many of them studied other things when they went to the Front, and were sudying things at the present time. When the Germans invaded Belgium, did the British run away from them? "Now that Charlie Dukes is over-running the Trades Council, don't run away from him, but stick to the Council. lam inclined to think that you are doing a wrong thing' in showing the white feather," was the advice of Mr. Potter.

Mr. John Ireland, chairman of the Manchester Branch of the Society, and representative of the wiredrawers on the Manchester Trades Council, expressed the opinion that where the Trades Councils had made the mistake was that they had not instructed the delegates to hold Union meetings. That had been a big mistake, be- cause he gathered from that meeting, if that course had been adopted the attitude \of the Council would have been different. They as Trade Unionists ought to help the fellows who had gone to the Front, for if they did not, who was going to do it? (Applause.) They ought to think about them—if they did not, what would the then think when the war was over? They as trade unionists were doing their best and had relaxed their rules. He contended that any' man who was not doing his duty by the Government to-day ought to be put against the wall and shot. (Cheers.)

It was carried amid applause, jiith only five dissentients.

SMALLER NEWSPAPERS IN PROSPECT.

The Control 01 Shipping.

If, as seems highly probable, the Government ' withdraw ships from carrying goods which are not essential for national purposes, one of the earliest effects will be to 'reduce the size of the newspapers, says "The Times." Both pulp and paper are brought to this country from America and the Continent, and ' if the number of vessels used for this purpose were restricted to any,considerable extent there' would soon be insufficient material in the country to print newspapers of the present size. As yet the war has not, bad a ,very serious effect as far as the size of the English newspapers is concerned. In the Allied countries the size was reduced immediately on the outbreak of hostili- ties, but in this country, after the first momen- tary anxiety when a scarcity of paper was threatened, the supply which continued to come in was sufficient to justify the publication of newspapers of normal size. • One of the first effects of any change would doubtless be an agreement amongst the pub- lishers of newspapers to curtail a good deal of expenditure which could be obviated, but which the Stress of competition has hitherto made in- evitable, One such saving would doubtless be the, abolition, of "returns," or the practice of accepting unsold copies In exchange for new ones. The percentage of' " returns Wiaries from 9 per cent. to 30 per cent., and iris believed that, if the practice were abolished, the general paper bill could be reduced 400 per cent. Another, 20 per cent. might be saved by re- ducing the size of the papers and cutting off supplies to distag parts of the country. Hitherto there has been keen competition in circulating newspapers to remote 'parts, but in a time "of.stress•the expenditure would scarcely be justified. In fact, there is a strong feeling that Government action ,on the lines indicated would be welcomed by many newspaper pro- prietors as affording an opportunity for a general agreement which would result in an im- mediate saving on the paper bill.

RAILWAY THEFTS.

L. & N.-W. Railway Shunters Heavily Fined.

EXAMPLE TO OTHERS.

The Warrington Police Court was crowded with interested listeners on Tuesday morning, and the solicitors' bench was full of a variety of articles, whisky , and brandy bottles, blouses, brushes, serge, soap and a host of other things, all due to four shunters of the London and North-Western Railway Company being charged with theft from the Arpley Sidings. At the same time a signalman was charged with receiving a portion of the stolen goods. Councillor Henry Roberts occupied the. chair The prisoners were:— Thomas William Crozier, of 24, Catherine- street, Warrington, who was charged with stealing within the past twelve months one quilt, three blouses, two clothes brushes, one tin of coffee, one pillow-case, one pair of boy's knickers, a quantity of wool, two boxes of soap, a quantity of loose soap, a bottle of champagne and a bottle of brandy, valued at £3 75., the property of the Railway company., James Spencer Sparkes, 58, Norris-street, who was charged with having, on or about December 21st, stolen 48 bottles of brandy and two bottles of whisky, to the value of £l4 17s. John James Smith, 155, Knutaford-road, and Benjamin Armitage, 43: Wakefield-str!et, who were charged with stealing about 60 yards of blue serge cloth, valued at £l3 10s., on Decem- ber 9th, and Frederick Summers, a signalman, of 10, Sureey-street, Latchford, who was charged with having received a piece of serge, well knowing it to have been stolen. Mr. Muller, of Euston, conducted the prosecu- tion, and Mr. T. S. Steel represented Sparkes, Armitage and Summers. The, other, two prisoners were unrepresented, Sand formally pleaded guilty. On behalf of Sparkes, Mr. Steel said he did not admit taking the 48 bottles of brandy, but admitted taking two bottles of wh'sky an:! tale of brandy. He (Mr. Steel) understood a con- signment of 48 bottles was missing. an t it had been assumed that because he had taken one he had taken the lot. Mr. Steel pleaded guilty on behalf of hiq other hit) clients. _ _

===== 0002601_19160129_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
Serge For Ireland.

prosecuting, Mr. Muller said all the prisoners were employes of the Railway Company. With regard to the 60 yards of blue serge, the Lanca- shire and Yorkshire Railway Company had con- signed the material to Holyhead' and then to be transhipped to Ireland. It was handed ,over to the London and North-Western Railway Co. and reached Arpley Sidings. It was in good condition and was wrapped in brown canvas When the consignment arrived at Holyhead it was found that the cloth was missing and only the canvas remained. Inquiries were made by detectives and, in consequence of these, they paid a visit to the lodgiigs of Smith and there told the landlady that they were making in- quiries respecting some blue serge cloth. She at once said Smith had given her daughter some cloth. She was asked if Smith was in and she replied "No." Detectives kept observation on the house and later saw Mrs. O'Neil's (the landlady's) daughter go to the door, look round, and then go back. At the back door the officers , saw another daughter come out with a brown paper parcel. They stopped her and she said that the parcel contained cloth. The officers went into the house with her and there they found Smith. They questioned him with regard to the cloth and he said he had bought it at Lewis's, Manchester, but he subsequently admitted taking it from the sidings. - Later, Armitage was seen at the Railway ' Station, and at first denied all knowledge of the cloth. But his house was searched and several pieces of cloth were found. A costume and dress which had been made up out of the blue cloth were also found. Armitage said his wife had bought the costume in the Market, but the wife said her husband had brought the cloth home about a month ago and told her he had got it from the Arpley Sidings. It appeared that Frank Summers, who was charged with receiving a piece of cloth, had 'taken the Material to a Stockton Heath tailor and had had a suit made from it. • Spirits That Went Astray.

With regard to the charge of stealing the whisky on December 18th, Messrs. Harrison, of Liverpool, consigned 25 cases of brandy to Broadheath, and it was in good order and condition when it arrived at Arpley. On the consignment reaching Broadheath it was found that 25 bottles were missing. Sparkes was seen by Detective Hewitt, and he admitted taking one bottle of brandy and two bottles of whisky. The officers proceeded to his house and there recovered the bottles. Concerning the charge against Crozier, when questioned, he at once admitted his guilt. He commenced to take these things about six months ago. Belated Anxiety For Wives And Families. Asked to make a plea, Crozier said he was very sorry. He added that he had a wife and four children, and asked for leniency. Smith expressed his sorrow and offered to pay for the cloth, if the company would give him time.

Sneaking on behalf of his clients, Mr. tite2l said Sparkes was a married man with two chil- dren. He was a highly respected man mil hitd been associated with a certain place of wpihhip in the town. He took the brandy during Chrt•A- mas week. A most remarkable fact was thit he was practically a teetotaler, and his wife and father-in-law—who lived with him—were Recha- bites, so the stuff was of no use to them what- ever. _ _ .

The Chairman: Were the bottles tampered with? - Mr. Steel: No. Continuing, Mr. Steel said that Armitage Wag 45 years of age, married, and had four, children. He found the parcel of cloth one night and took it home. What he should have done was to have taken it to the Railway Company and told them he had found it. Summers was a' signalman in the Froghall box, and where he made a mistake was in not making inquiries when the cloth was given to/ him'. He had at- tested under Lord Derby's scheme and was most anxious that his wife and children should not suffer. If he were given an opportunity, though in group 43, he was willing to join the Army at once. Mr. Arthur Browne intervened at this stage and said Mrs. O'Neil wished him to inform the Bench that she had made a mistake. The Chairman: She made a very serious mis- take and she might have found herself in a very different position if the authorities had cared to take action. Prisoners' Good Wages.

Mr. Muller again addressed the Bench. He said Summate had not given the authorities any information concerning the suit he had had made. Extensive robberies had been going on from the company during the past 12 months, casting suspicion on a number of innocent men. Summers' ‘average wages were 0 ,2s. a week, Crozier's 0 6s: 9d., Smith's £2 2s. 2d., Armi- tage's £2 18s. 9d., and Spark; 0 9s. 7d. Police And Magisterial Warning.

The Chief Constable informed the Bench that there was nn record against any of the prisoners. He felt it to be his duty to emphasise the fact that extensive robberies had been going on for sometime, and that the Warrington police had been tailed upon to assist the railway police,, these inquiries having taken up a great deal of •their time. After the Bench had retired for a few minutes, the Chairman said all the prisoners were men of adult, age with considerable ex- perience and knowledge. There was, therefore, absolutely no excuse for their conduct. The magistrate p had carefully considered the ques- tion whether they should be sent to prison with- out the option of a fine. A merciful view, how- ever, had been taken to ,enable th,e men to redeem their characters, and a fine would be imposed in each case. Crozier, Sparkes, Smith and Armitage would be fined £lO each, or three months' imprisonment in default; and Summers £5, or a month's imprisonment. "I Sincerely' hope," the Chairman said in con- -elusion—" and I am speaking for my colleagues' on the Bench—that what we have done this morning will, at any rate, act as a deterrent against any further robberies taking place. If any servant of the company has any knowledge of robberies going on he should disclose the fact and thus assist to bring the offenders to justice. This is a duty railway servants owe to them- selves, to the public and to their employers."

SUCCESS IN THE POULTRY YARD.

The Best Foods And How To Use Them.

By E. T. BROWN.

There is no better all-round grain for poultry than wheat. At its present price, of course, it cannot be used at all extensively, but in normal times it is the best grain that there is for feed- ing to fowls. It is suitable at all seasons of the year, and under nearly all conditions. Fowls are particularly fond of it, eating it in prefer— ence to any other grain. Barley possesses excel- lent feeding properties, but its hard husk makes it difficult to digest, and -there is sometimes a certain amount of difficulty in persuading fowls to eat it at all readily. Containing a fairly large share of carbo-hydrates, it is really of more value during the winter months. Care is required in purchasino- it since many inferior samples are offered for sale. Oats are extremely well-balanced, -containing a fair percentage of the three essential elements. A good deal of difficulty is often experienced in persuading the' fowls to •eat _them, owing, doubtless, to their hard husks. Only well-filled oats are of any service to poultry, since the small, thin grains, consist almost entirely of husk. It is an excel- lent plant-to steep the grains in water for a few hours previous to feeding, as in this manner they are rendered more palatable and easier of digestion. There is no grain used so extensively for poultry as maize, but generally speaking it is a food to be avoided. When fed in large quanti- ties it impairs the health, reduces the vitality, and causes the birds to become very fat. There are only two occasions when I recommend the use of maize for' poultry, namely, to sitting' hens and during a spell of very cold weather. Maize is extremely strong in the heat-forming element, carbo-hydrates, and thus goes to the production of fat rather, than eggs or flesh. The appearance of s a maize-fed bird is probabllT familiar to many readers; the carcase is entirely coated with a layer of oily, greasy fat, useless as a food, and harmful to the bird. Cooked maize. is a suitable food, but in a raw state it requires. to be employed very carefully indeed. The Small Grains.

The smaller grains are usually only of service. in a dry chicken mixture, and are of little use for adulbstock. Canary seed, hemp seed, buck- wheat, millet and dari are the ones most commonly employed. Canary seed is rather ex- pensive, and is therefore only used during the. first month of a chicken's life; after this time less ,expensive grains are substituted. Buck- wheat enjoys great popularity among the. poultry-keepers on the Continent, and there is• no doubt but that it is a capital grain for all. kinds of poultry, especially turkeys. It iIS not very easy to obtain in this country, but when a good sample can be procured cheaply its use, is recommended. Rice is extensively used by the " duckers " in the counties of Buckingham- shire and Bedfordshire, where it is regarded as the finest food there is for fattening purposes. It is, however, almost useless as a food in a raw state, since it is very indigestible and badly balanced. For cooking purposes, the common chicken or Burrnah rice, should be used, a small quantity is advocated in the formation of a dry chick mixture in order to balance some of the richer grains.

The Best Meals. Middlings is a very useful meal for poultry,. and is largely used. It is also known as top-- pings, seconds, thirds, pollards and sharps. It is rather deficient in carbo-hydrates—the element in which barley meal is strong—and hence the two mixed together form a well- balanced ration. When used alone it is more suitable for summer than for winter feeding.. Although barley meal is a favourite food with many poultry-keepers its use is by no means sa extensive as it might advantageously be, for taking its feeding value as well its price into . consideration, it is an economical food. What was said regarding maize applies equally ta maize meal, and its use is not recommended save when the weather is cold. For fattening purposes ground oats is un- rivalled, producing an abundance of fine quality flesh of an &xcellent colour and flavour. All the best Surrey and Sussex chickens are fed on ground oats mixed with sour skim milk, as the fetters consider no other food can equal it for the purpose. In small quantities it is useful for feeding to young stock, one feed a day assisting very materially in the growth and develop- ment. It is a very finely ground meal, almost resembling flour or its consistecy. The husks are ground as well as the kernels, specially pre- pared millstones being employed. Oatmeal must not ' be confounded With the preceding meal, as the two are totally different ,Oatmeal consists of the kernels without the husks. Either raw or cooked.it is very valuable for young chickens, but is rather too expensive for common use. Bean and pea meals are useful for egg produc- tion, but must be used in small quantities only. One-fifth nart by bulk to the other meals ie. ample. It is useful, too, for young stock, assisting in the formation of flesh and bone. Bran is a very bulky food, containing a large percentage of fibre or indigestible matter. Its feeding value is not high, notwithstanding the fact that it contains a goodly proportion of the three essential elements. It is most useful for adding bulk to the food when a concentrated meal is being employed: during the summer too. it is useful for preventing the birds becoming too fat. ,

===== 0002601_19160129_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
TESTING THE EGGS, 4

. The eggs should be tested in a dark rosin, with the help of a testing lamp, on the even- ings of the sixth and seventh days. With a large machine it is best to make two teetings oft (16iitafttive.eVenils or the drawer may be kept open too long an the eggs become unduly chilled. Each egg put back into the drawer as fertile should be specially marked. The staler eggs .nay. need re-testing on the evening of the fourteenth day, but this should not be neces- sary if all the eggs be of the same, or nearly the .same, degree of :freshness when first put in. As far as possible, the room in which the machine is running should be kept at an even temperature, but free ventilation should be given at all times. Fresh air is a necessity if the chicks are to hatch out strong. Chicks hatched in December, January and February grow faster and attain a larger size than those hatched in earlier or later months, but if the weather be very severe they may need a little care for the first three weeks. After the first few days, however, the inner part of the brooder should not be kept too warm. Chicks hatched in an incubator can be satisfactorily reared by a hen, but in this case care should ;be taken that (1) the hen is thoroughly broody; ;(2) all chicks put under one hen are given to her on the same ,evening, late; (3) the chicks are not chilled: when carried out. The hen should be, fed before the chicks are put under ,her, and may then be left .without food or water for 36 hours. The hen should be fed upon maize for a few days, and the chicks upon food suitable .for them.. Finely . broken flint grit should be given te:chicks when .a day old and every -day afterwards. If water is provided, newly-hatched chickens should al- wnys be offered food before they are allowed to drink. The water should be. quite pure and . . fresh.

CO-OPERATORS' PROFITS.

Reply To The Private Traders' Complaint To M.P.'s.

Tc the Editor of "The Examiner."

see from a Press notice on Saturday, that the Secretary of a certain Private Traders' 'Organisation in Warrington has been in .corn- lmunication with you regarding Co-operativt -Societies and the income tax question, together 'with another matter.

In view of the statements made in this notice, .perhaps You will forgive me for pointing out that there are two sides to this Question, and the other side may not have been put before your notice.

In the first Place the Co-operative Societies are formed of individuals, each.one of which is haute to income tax providing that he comes within the limits prescribed by law, to the very same extent that every other person in the com- munity does. All members of such Societies are liable as above stated, to as,iessirnent .for income tax oil any interest that they may receive on money invested in their Society. The Society 'itself is exempt by law under Schedules C and D from income tax, providing certain conditions are fulfilled.

For many years an agitation has been on foot to remove this exentption, by a certain section of the community, and to tax Co-operative Societies as if they were .private organisations run for individual ,profit, an.d.the matter has gone so far as a decision of the House of Lordil on the matter, which certainly should be flan:. The position simply is that the -members of Co-operative Societies have banded themselves together to purchase wholesale such goods as they require, and .distribute amongst them- selves. For the sake of convenience, retail prices are °hawed, and when the-expenses ofi distribution .are ascertained, whatever overi charges have 'been .made are returned to the', Persons who are members, in proportion to their. purchase..

It has been cid, .and rightly, held, that such', returns are of the nature of discounts or savings' on incokne, and not in any sense an addition to income, and .awe thereforelnot liable to taxation.

Co-operative Societies have always been, and are still, liable to be taxed on any profit made out of trade with non-members, but the Govern- ment has always recognised that to impose this tax would an that 'the claims for its refund- ing would •co nt more limn t the I tax • is worth.

Co-operators as such have- not been beilina any other section in the community in doing their test to assist the country in the present crisis, but nevertheless the recent action of the Chancellor of flue Exchequer in including them in the Finance Act of 1915, for excess profit duty, is one that zannot be.allowed to pass withost protest; solely on the tgrounds: that if no profits exist there can be no,g.uestion-of an excess, but this is not a matter which is intended to argue out here.

Regarding the information conveyed to youi that the SO'drty has ik-sued,at oircular inviting! members to invest spare money veith it, this,has been done simply with the intention of asSist-. ing the Goekernmentiby gettiim the ordinary working classes who can ,not and will not tie up their money for a period of ftve years, even', though the rate of interest is "5 per cent., -to; invest it at a, slightly lower rate of interest' where it can be got in time of need. We enclose for your perusal a copy of the circular, where you win see for yourself the in- tentions regarding the matter. The local Society has -already directly invested; 22,000 in the Government War Loan, aniq Societies throughout the kingdom are respon-, sible for a sum well over the Million, and anyi, further amount that it is posgible from time to I time to loan to the Government 'either directly or through our wholesale organisation will un doubtedly be placed at the 'Government's dis— posal.

Believe me, Yours respeetfUlly, J. j'A.73.IAN, 'Secretary. P.S.—A copy of this letter has been seat ti,

BURNING TRAGEDIES.

Fatal Accidents To Two Young

Children.

There has been a number of burning fatalities in Warrington, and two more have occurred just recently. Inquiries into the circumstances were made by Mr. S. Brighouse at the Warrington Police Station on Tuesday afternoon. The first inquest was on the 'body of Annie Parsonage, the two-year-old child of Lance-Cor- poral Parsonage, of the Royal Engineers (who is serving abroad), and Mrs. 'Parsonage, of 100, Gorsey-lane. _ _

Minnie Parsonage, aged 16 years, stated that on the 11th inst. her mother went out leaving Annie in her care. Witness started ironing, and it was necessary to take the fireguard away from the front of the fire. About 4.30 she had occasion to go into the yaid. At that time her sister was playing with a piece of ribbon on the hearth. She had been away-only a few seconds when she heard a scream. She at once returned and found that Annie's elCAlies* were on fire. Rugs were wrapped round her and she was taken to the Infirmary, where she died on Mon- day last.

The jury returned a verdict of "Accidental death."

The Coroner commented that he Could very well understand the necesSity of having to re- move the fireguard, and he expressed his,sym- pathy with Mrs. Parsonage.'

===== 0002601_19160129_art0046.txt | included=true | included =====
REACHING TO THE MANTEL SHELF.

The second inquiry was on the body of Elsie Shaw, aged eight years, the daughter of Mr. and Mrs. Shaw, of 37, Pierpoint-street, War- Tington.

Mrs. Shaw deposed that on 'Monday at about 4.40, Elsie came home from sehoOl, and then played in the streets for a few minutes. The witness had to go to her mother's house, but 't was not many minutes before someone came and told her that her house was on fire. She went home and there found that 'Elsie had been burned. She was removed to the Infirmary, but Med the same day. Before she died Elsie told witness that she was in the act.of putting some money on the mantelshelf, When her clothes caught fire. A verdict of "Accidental death" was re- turned.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, JANUARY 29, 1916.

STORMING THE TRIBUNE.

VIOLENT SUNDAY AFTERNOON SCENE.

Threatening Situation Saved By Chief Constable's

Intervention.

Instead of being content with an oratorical bombardment of the speakers announced for the ne-compulsion meeting in the Co-operative Hall, on Sunday afternoon, part of the " audience " engaged in hand-to-hand skirmishes with - the •stewards, and the battle resulted in the storm- ing and successful occupation of the platform by the dissentients. , But there were no casualties.

The meeting—promoted by the No-Conscrip- tion Fellowship, Union of Democratic Control, Independent Labour Party, and the Trades and Labour Council—was timed to begin at 3.30, but half an hour before it was evident that the majority of the audience were intent on dis- turbance. Far along Sankey-street the strains of "Rule, Britannia," were , heard belching through the windows a the hall—situated nearer heaven than any other public meeting- place in the town and district. On entering, one saw scores of men singing lustily and- puff- ing their pipes between the verses. Over a dozen soldiers also sang. the song of the sea. The music having ebbed, an excited group, persuaded Councillor Wilson to stand on a 'form and declare:

"We are going to fight these men who are against us—these men who ought to be in 'khaki doing their duty. My sons and sons.:in:law have gone, and some of them have been wounded once, twice, and three times; and are we going to allow men to speak who are again-St us?" A host of voices shouted, "Never!" while, others cried "Traltorsl"

" No " continued the Councillor, " we are not; going to let them speak. I am sincerely sorry that this hall should have been let Tor the pur- pose. When the time comes I hope these men will be compelled to shoulder the gun. (Cheers.)' I sincerely hope that as soon as they appear on; the platform and they show that they are; against the Government in any shape or form; you won'egive them a hearing. (Cheers.) Now, boys, are we down-hearted?"

Councillor Wilson's question was answered with the refrain: Not likely, ‘• While we've Jack upon the sea And Tommy on the land, We need not fret; It's a long, long way to Tipperary, But we're not down-hearted yet.

They certainly were not. Not once, 'butt' thrice they sang their reply, following it up with "Rule, Britannia,' and then "Steady, Boys, Steady'!" And always between the verses they puffed their pipes. Every time a soldier or armleteer came in they cheered. Those who did not know the words of the "Marseillaise" hummed the tune. Most of them hummed.

" No-Compulsion " Speakers Denied A Hearing At Warrington.

Towering above all on the dais, excepting Mr. Ashton, he took command. His first onslaught was directed to those near the ante-room door. fie gave them a sample of trench language, and went on, "I've fowt for you, you wicked old sinners, you -, and yet you won't help us to get more men. Ugh!" }or a moment he hesitated and then changed his derision againSt the promoters of the meet- ing into encouragement for the disturbers.

Three chee; for the English boys!" he cried, and led them himself, staking his hat off, and bellowing loudly. "I belong to the 'Ter- riers,' ' Ike Unbreakable Fourth,' " he cried. "I'm one of the Saturday night soldiers, I am. And aren't you proud of us? (Chorus: "Rather.!") r have five brothers in the trenches. These others are dirty dogs. Won't help. the Army.!"- Ile looked to the right threateningly, and there were challenges to fight from' one or two. peace people. Nothing came of them, how- ever, and Muck turned his attention to the audience in general by asking satirically, *".Areetithey a nice-lot? Come on, boys (he was now coaxing)! Come on! It's the greatest game I've been in! It beats fighting at the Front. These others are a lot of criminals; that's what they are. Now, 'lads, you of the Unbreakable Fourth,' .come on!"

There were no more of his regiment to come. But there were others. Boys of the Bantam breed from Orford Barracks fell in at the double. One little fellow—said to have been only 15 years old—was almost persuaded to stand, en the table, but he threw his hat on to it in- stead, and holding up his swagger cane menac- ingly, shouted to the anti-compulsionists, "Oh! ton dirty .dogs." He repeated the accpsation, became speechless, and Bluck relieved him of embarrassment by shouting "Three cheers for the Bantams!" The response was terrific.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0048.txt | included=true | included =====
PLATFORM RUSHED.

Half an hour of this, and the like of it, and just as the speakers were emerging from the ante-room, mores in the audience rose :as one man and rushed for the platform. The stewards tried- in vain to push them back; they were at- tacking in close column order. Mr. Walter Ash- ton was first on the platform. He took it :at a jump, but caught the glass lumaloler ornt :the table in slipping his legs. over the rail, and it, fell with a clatter; the receptacle was smashed to atoms, and theewater (like the King's wine) undrunk. Councillor Wilson was not so facile. He tried to get under the rail, but a steward pulled him back by the coat tails, and having struggled free; the Councillor then dodged an- other, rushed off to the side, and Scampered up the steps on to the platform, waving ins hat triumphantly. Others quickly rallied round him. Soldiers as well—some getting there via the staircase, .others under the rail (greatly to the danger of the flower pots), and yet others over the rail.

In possession of the platform they must -needs sing "God save the King." They then placed the chairs in front of the stairs near the ante-room to prevent the speakers approaching. It was an effective bar- icade. But it Was not a frivolous demonstration. Every man looked tremendously in earnest. "Their brood's up !" whispered a quiet one in the area to another.

At first it was a pandemonium—those for the Government and those " agin the Government" hurling epithet A at each other with mhxim gun- like rapidity. It was a Futuristic presentation of sound, as vigorous as Souza's band in action, s varied as the voices round the Tower of Babel.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0049.txt | included=true | included =====
SOLDIERS JOIN IN.

Councillor Wilson strove hard to gain quiet, but for several minutes his • words were com- pletely drowned. So he talked into space and into the ears of, the Pressmen within a yard of him. "We are going to fight this war to a finish," he yelled (one could almost hear his throat tear with the strain). "We are all bound together independent of party politics, and we are going to win. We are not going to allow the Germans, to dictate' to us. . . ." The. Councillor's persistency was gradually being recognised, and Order ! Order! Order!" from many voices produced reasonable quiet. "Those who want to speak against the Gov- ernment are nothing but the enemies of Eng- land," he declared, amidst cheers. "We are going to stop here till five o'clock or twelve o'clock to-night. No one else is going to take possession of this platform." (Loud cheers.) It was then that the soldiers began to roll up to reinforce the position. As they went forward at the double someone shouted "Boys of the bulldog breed." Private Billy .Bluck was amongst them.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0050.txt | included=true | included =====
CONFIDENCE IN THE GOVERNMENT.

Councillor Wilson, trying to make himself heard above the uproar, said he moved: "That this mass meeting of the people of Wallington has absolute confidence in the Government— (hear, hear)—and we—"

There were more cheers, and a young lance- corporal, waving his stick, and shouting at the top of his voice, said: "I ask these young men a question. Are they eligible to enlist, and if so, why should we, who have already been out eighteen months, go out again, while they stop at home filling our jobs and making our 'money?"

" Come on, all who wish to enlist," cried Bluck ; "come and play in the biggest game in the world Vs

Someone, suggested that there were men on the platform who were eligible and had not en- listed. The lance-corporal made hurried in- quiries, and announced in stentorian voice that there was not an eligible man among those who had taken up their stand on the platform who had not either enlisted or attested. "You are a lot of criminals, that's what you are," shouted a soldier from the platform. "I only wish the Germans would come here and give you a bombing. That would cure some of you! Never mind these snuffy noses," he added, "come along, lads, and we cannot lose!" Roll up, my boys! Good old South Lanes!"

Again liluckT intervened. "I have five brothers in the trenches," he said. "Some of you have relatives there, too. Why cannot you all rally to them?" Pointing to the fifteen-year- old soldier ,rhe.:Aasked the no-compulsionists whether the sight of the boy did not make them feel ashamed of themselves. (Cheers.)

===== 0002601_19160129_art0051.txt | included=true | included =====
"NO MEETING."

At this point the Chief Constable (Mr. Mar- tin Nicholls) was seen in conference with Coun- cillor Wilson,

Councillor Wilson, connikig to the front of the platform, was able for the first time to secure sufficient quietness to permit of his voice being heard all over the room. He then said quietly and deliberately: "We have achieved our object. (Cheers.) We came here to-day to prevent this meeting taking place—for the good and- the best interests of the country. We are, I believe, a majority of twenty to one, or more, and we are in favour of the Government Bill. (Applause.) I have much pleuure in moving: , "'That this mass meeting of Warring- tenians have absolute confidence in the Gov- ernment at the present time and their Con- scription Bill to get all the help necessary .1-o win this great war.' (Cheers.)

"We don't want it to go forth from Warring- ton that we are against the Government or that we are in favour of peace. ('Never.') We are going to fight to the bitter end. (Cheers.) We are going to make every personal sacrifice— (hear, hear)—and we are going to see this suc- cessfully through, so that such a war will never again take place in the history of our children or children's children. (Applause.) I believe it is the honest wish of every man and woman that there should be no war, but when yolk have a men—"

A Man on the platform: "A pro-German." • Councillor Wilson: We want to do no damage here to-day.- (Hear, hear.) What we want to do is to carry our, resolution of unabated confi- dence in the Government; and I move that we have every confidence in the Government and the way they are conducting the war. (Cheers.)

Mr. Clarke, seconding, said he trusted we should fight out the war to the, bitter end, and get the matter settled as soon as possible. (Ap: plause.) The resolution was then put and carried with only two dissentients, and the National Anthem was sung.

"DEPLORABLE."

In the meantime the speakers (or, rather, those who had been announced to speak) got away through a fire escape exit, and proceeded to the Socialist Hall, Bridge Foot, where they discussed the position. To a representative of "The Examiner," Mr. Tom Richardson, M.P., said it was deplorable that at this time of day free speech was denied to those who were not with the crowd.

Mr. Richardson had made a special journey from London and arrived at the time the meet- ing was to have commenced. He returned to London on the five o'clock express. Someone remarked that he hoped he had enjoyed his half-day excursion. The other speakers announced were: Messrs. E. Plinston (president of the Trades and Labour Council), Chas. Mr. S. Wright, J.P., of Church-street Post Office, asks us to state that the rumour which has gained currency in the town that he was at the no-conscription meeting on Sunday after- noon is altogether unfounded. He had nothing to do with the meeting nor with the letting of the hall for the purpose of the meeting.

THREE OUT OF FIVE.

Suggestion That Cottage Homes' Men Might Work On Munitions.

Are the Padgate Cottage Homes, under the care of the Warrington Union, over-staffed with men? Mr. George Mather declared that in view of the general shortage of labour in the country they are. At the Warringtbn Guardians' meeting on Wednesday Mr. Mather said they could see from the papers that there was a shortage of labour in the country. The nation was short of sol- diers and short of anunition workers. He had been wondering if the Board were patriotic enough to give three out of the five men at the Cottage Homes the opportunity of going on munition work, or.of doing anything else that would assist the. country at the present time. They had 120 children there and five men,.which was out of proportion when compared with the needs of the country. He was confident that the Board could spare three men until the war was over, and thereby assist the Empire in what it had to perform. The Chairman (Mr. A. J. Jolley): That is a matter for the committee.

The Rev. A. M. Mitchell: What a pity Mr. Mather does not attend the committee meetings to ventilate these matters! I quite agree that the Board ought to do everything possible to release workers at this time, but it is rather a mistake to bring the matter up here before it ; has been ventilated in comnutitee.

Mr. Mather replied that he had mentioned the matter to Mr. Mitchell, and wanted to know why he had not brought it before the committee.

It was understood that the Cottage Homes Committee would look into the matter.

Guardians Have Difficulty In GeMu( The Beverage.

Housekeepers know only too well that the prices of commodities are going up, but it is no consolation to them to know the Board-ol Guard- ians are experiencing the same difficulty in securing goods at something like the old prices. Recently Mr. Arthur Bottomley (clerk to the Warrington Union) wrote to 40 firms asking for samples of tea at is. Bd. and is. 4d. per lb., and in every case the reply was to the effect that tea could not be bought for is. Bd. a lb. Only two firms could send samples of tea at is. 10d., but the remaining 38 said they could not even supply it at this figure! Mr. .Bottoniley reported these facts to the Warrington Guardians on Wednesday at their fortnightly meeting, and during a discussion Mr. George Mather jokingly remarked that the Union would have. to go back to Nature and drink pure water. The Rev. A. M. : It is a fine thing; nothing better. .

Ultimately Mr. Bottomley was instructed to ask for samples of tea at 2s. and 2s. 2d. per

The nearest.approach to happiness that some people ever acquire is not to be as miserable as they were yesterday.

Look Up!

Most people walk with their eyes to the ground. At intervals they look up and are sur- prised at the height of the blue skies, the quiet of the tree tops, the cheeriness of the sunshine, or the beauty of the stars. This is not meant as epigram, but there is a similitude which lends itself to application. Look up!

FARM AND GARDEN.

I . HOUR-SAVING MACHINERY.

"To Meet Labour Shortage.

- At a meeting of the Farmers' Club, held at the Whitehall Rooms, Hotel Metropole, London, on Monday, Mr. W. J. Malden read a paper on "Labour Saving Farm Implements and Machinery." Mr. Maiden's paper is of particular import- ance in the present crisis. This year, in a greater degree than ever, arable farming in this country will be dependent on mechanical con- trivances. The present was looked upon as a fitting opportunity for considering the economic merits of various implements of the newest patterns, not only by comparison with older designs, but also in relation tq their fitness to displace animal and manual labour. The Big Farm.

"A far greater age of machinery on the land is just ahead," said Mr. Malden. "It is im- perative, because there will not be so full a supply of manual labour. Moreover, this short- ness of labour will bring the British farm.:. more into line with those farming in new countries, where labour has• always .been in short supply, and where reliance has maully more into line with those farming in new countries are always on the alert to acquit.) Un- proved machinery, and it behoves th. J3ritih farmer to lead- and not to follow oth.)rs can leave the minor crops to the enthuiast of the spade and the toy holding ;- there is room for some minor industries on the land and under glass; but I do not admit that--,-except those which are chiefly devoted to .the production of luxuries, as apart from national necessities— these minor crops are better produced by those working on a small scale than by those working with good organisation on a large scale, where they naturally can afford to command a more effective and more varied outfit, and can work to better advantage. We cannot progress back- ward to the spade, and Adam—when he consti- tuted the-whole farming community, and com- prised the only Farmers' Club—with Eve (who organised the first apple-eating association—re- cently revived) as the energetic secretary. Can- didly, I do not see the large farm quickly dis- appearing, except through ignorant or party-in- terested political developments; consequently, with-the temptations that there are, and will be for men to work at good wages in other industries, we must look forward—and a very little way for- ward—to a great extension of the use of machinery on the land." Viewing the question from the particular cir- cumstances of the approaching months it can be said that success in overtaking the important spring calendar will be determined largely by the extent to which the work can be done by mechanical contrivances. This fact has been recognised .by societies and individual farmers, and as a result of prudent foresight compound machinery will play a part never before ap- proached in field operations in England; and one which would have been still more pronounced but for the inability of makers to. produce sufficient to meet the demands.

THE FATE OF SMALL HOLDINGS.

General Booth has executed a deed terminat- ing the Salvation Army's small holding experi- ment at Boxted. The scheme was founded by the late General Booth in eonjanction with the late Mr. George Herring, but the measure of success was not considered sufficient to warrant its continuance, and the difficulties, owing to the war, during the last year became such as to leave General Booth no option.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0059.txt | included=true | included =====
AGRICULTURAL IMPORTS IN 1915.

The total value of the principal articles of agricultural produce imported into the United Kingdom in 1915 was £276,803,000, as against only £211,591,000 in 1914, £213,923,000 in 1913, and an average of £186,276,000 in the ten years 1903-12. The increase of 1915 as compared with 1914 is no less than 65 million pounds—figures that may well give rise to thought as to our dependence on foreign food, and our helplessness against foreign prices in a time of emergency.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0060.txt | included=true | included =====
MONTHLY AGRICULTURAL REPORT.

The crop reporters of the Board of Agricul- ture,- in reporting on the crops and agricultural conditions on the Ist January, state that the wet and often stormy weather very largeli pre- vented work in the fields during December. The early-sown wheat, although backward, is gener- ally .a good plant, but late sowings have been very slow, in germinating, and where ,they are up they are not So satisfactory as the early sown, more especially on wet lands. As very little could be done during the months, the acreage now under wheat is only about three- fourths of the area intended for this crop, and, as 'compared with the end of 1914 (when prob- ably. over 80, per cent. had been got in), the acreage actually sown is about six or seven per cent less. Winter oats and beans, altfiough also backward, are generally satisfactory. Turnips and swedes, although small, are generally of good quality, but some damage has been done by frosts in the northern districts. Shortage of labour has interfered with pulling in many parts, and more will probably be fed off than usual.

EFFECTS OF QUICK AND SLOW MILKING.

In the course of a paper read by Dr. C. Crow- ther, before the British Association, data were furnished in support of the view that, in addi- tion to removing milk previously formed, the handling of the teats may impart a stimulus to further vigorous secretion during the period ot milking. The milk from the " quarter "of the udder milked first had a tendency to steadily increasing fat content as the quarter was emptied, a tendency which was much less pro- nounced with the subsequent " quarters " milked, although in the case of all four quarters there was a rapid rise in the percentage of fat towards the close of milking. When the quar- ters were milked in pairs the result from the first pair resembled those from the first quarter, and those from the second pair those from the last quarter; when the four quarters were milked simultaneously the result from each resembled those from a "first quarter." The milk from the quarter milked first was almost invariably the richest in fat, and that from the last qtarter the poorest. The foregoing observa- tions led to the conclusion that the time-factor must be of considerable importance in milking. This was confirmed by a comparison of very quick and very slow milking by ordinary methods, which showed a difference of 10 per cent. of milk-yield and 40 per cent. of fat-yield in favour of the quick milking. A further comparison was made of ordinary milking, taking the teats in pairs, and simul- taneous milking, by. two milkers, of all four quarters. A difference of 2 per cent, in milk- yield and 6 per cent. in fat-yield in favour of the latter method was indicated, despite the occa- sional disturbance of the cow inevitable with this mode of milking. Further tests with the milking-machine are proposed.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0062.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE GARDEN.

LAWNS.

Many lawns at this season, more especially those used for croquet and lawn tennis, are in poor condition, the surface being bare and un- inviting. If there is a good bottom of grass thus doing away with the necessity of sowing grass seeds, the following treatment will go far to improve the appearance of the lawn. Pro- cure some fibrous loam and add to this some well-rotted manure, and the residue from the burning of garden rubbish. Some bone meal may be added with beneficial results. Mix all this together and pass it through a fine sieve to rid it of stones and other rubbish, and spread it evenly over the lawn. Let it lie for a few weeks so that it may be well broken down, then brush it in with a stiff birch broom, first length- wise and then across, so as to work the whole well into the roots of the grass. In April a dressing of -sulphate of ammonia or nitrate of soda at the rate of 21b. to the square rod will help greatly.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0063.txt | included=true | included =====
IMPROVING HEAVY LAND.

This is a slow and very often difficult business, but the labour and expense are not thrown away. Much may be done by working heavy land at the proper time, giving when this is being done a heavy dressing of strawy stable manure in preference to cow manure. Strawy manure can- not be dug in so easily as that which is quite rotten, but in the case of heavy soil the aerating influence of the strawy litter is clear. If a good wide trench is kept open there need not be any difficulty in burying a quantity of it, this serv- ing to keep the soil open and porous. As much as possible should be mixed with the bottom soil of the trench, and if this is repeated say every second year, such soil may with advantage be brought to the surface. Leaf soil, burnt garden refuse, ashes, burnt clay, road scrapings from iron-tarred roads, the scourings of ditches and water courses all come in useful in the opening up of a heavy clay soil. Quicklime too is most useful, this both improving heavy land and rendering soil that has been over-manured fertile. The lime should be put out in small heaps all over the quarter to be treated, covered over with soil, and when staked spread over the surface, forking it in in the spring at the rate of a bushel to every three yards.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0064.txt | included=true | included =====
BULBS IN POTS.

An important point in the case of such as hyacinths and tulips in pots, or in the case of the former when grown in glasses, is to make sure that each has plenty of roots before taking the bulbs indoors. Too often is it the case that the bulbs directly they are potted or placed in glasses are taken into the greenhouse, or, if such i 4 not available, stood in the windows, with the result that the flower spikes fail to develop. To guard against this the bulbs when potted should be plunged in the open air in ashes or leaf-soil, leaving them in this until it is seen that the tops are showing through this covering. this proving that the roots are working freely in the soil. When this happens the sbulbs may with every hope of a successful flowering be taken indoors. In the case of hyacinths grown in glasses these should always be stood in a dark cupboard or in a cellar until it is seen that the roots have freely permeated the water in which they are growing, when they. too, may safely be stood in the window.—Wick, in "Farm and Home."
