THE EXAMINER,

SATURDAY, JANUARY 29, 1010.

KNOCK-DOWN BLOWS FOR TRADES COUNCIL.

We congratulate the trade unionists of

Warrington upon having ranged themselves

in advance, with the trade unionists of the

rest of the country, in the stand taken at the National Labour Conference at bristol this week. We especially congratulate the Wire- drawers of the town who, in special meeting assembled last Saturday night, made it quite clear to all whom it May concern that they will tolerate no tampering with' their honour or trafficking with their patriotism. Mr. Charles Dukes did. not attend the meeting. He sent a letter, in which he did not attempt to meet the charge of misrepresenting the Unions. He merely alleged that the Trades Council position was being misrepresented by people who did not understand the trade union ,position, and that its opponents were trying to split the movement by the aid of the present crisis, and he hoped the meeting would accept his suggestion that this was the time for unity. They did accept the sugges- tion. All the speeches that were made urged the necessity for complete unity, but it was not of the sort of unity Mr. Dukes had in mind. His unity meant their agreement with his distorted idea of what is right; their unity meant closing up the ranks, supporting the Government, and getting the Germans on their knees at the first possible moment. Perhaps Mr. Dukes will tell the Wiredrawers now that they are seeking to split their own organisation. Of course, he knows better than they do what are their most pressing 'needs, and how best to serve their interests. Ho is willing to vote for them without asking them how they would like him to go. Voting is his concern, not theirs—and they will be obligingly careful to be united by agreeing to what h,e is pleased to do.

But assuming that the Wiredrawers do not understand the trade unionist movement as ; Mr. Dukes does, what about the National Conference at which Mr. Dukes has been pres- ent this week to vote for the thousands of trade unionists of Warrington whom he repre- sents? That conference has laid it down, clearly and emphatically, that trade unionists will have none of this Independent Labour party tomfoolery at the present juncture. On the first day they pledged themselves by a majority of nearly a million of votes—we pre- sume Mr. Dukes' votes for Warrington went in the minority—to assist the Government as far as possible in the successful prosecution of the war, notwithstanding Mr. • Ramsay Macdonald's opposition. By a majority of over a million and a half they approved the p4rticipation of the Labour party in the vol- untary recruiting campaign, notwithstanding the opposition of Mr. Philip Snowden. It was • upon this voluntary recruiting campaign that Mr. Dukes and his " executive " forced the working men of Warrington to be neutral, as an organised body. But doubtless-Mk: Mikes will say the National Conference, like the rest Of us, want to split themselves up. However, the conference went further and decided that there is to be no agitation for the repeal of the Compulsion Bill, which has passed the House of Lords and immediately becomes law. True, the conference condemned compulsion; but we all do that, from Mr. Asquith down- ward. The Wiredrawers condemned it on Saturday night, and, as Mr. F. A. Baker, the enlightefidd chairman of that , enlightened gathering, said, we are not going to have con- scription for ever. With the war out of the way conscription will vanish, or the trade unionists will know the reason why..

Yesterday the conference endorsed by a majority of 1,405,000 the entry of Labour into the Coalition Government; and decided, by a majority of 1,127,000 votes that the best in- ' tei.ests of the nation would be secured by Labour continuing to be represented in the Government, while a resolution efiticising ouY methods of foreign policy, a resolution which was said to savour of "an arrangement be- tween the I.L.P. and the Union of Democratic Control," was rejected by nearly two votes to one.

As genuine trade unionists are coming to see where their true interests lie, they are becoming determined to manage their own affairs more to their own liking. One of the speakers on Saturday night urged the Wire- drawers' Union to remain in the Council, and said it was necessary for them to have a cen- tral body upon which their interests could be represented. So it is. But it is better not to be represented at all than to be misrepre- sented so glaringly as the Warrington-trade unionists have 'been misrepresented for some time past. Mr. Baker hinted at getting properly represented through another body which will be set up loally. We hope this plan will come to fruition. In the meantime Warrington can congratulate itself upon hav- ing brought %itself into line with the sane Labour thought of the country before The holding of this week's great conference. Other- wise we might have been branded as out of harmony with trade unionism and as being given to toying with dialoisity.ofjhe basest._

kind—a brand that would have been cruel be- yond conception, considering what the town and its sons, mostly trade unionists, have

done for their country in its hour of need.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0072.txt | included=true | included =====
THE " ABANDONED " MEETING.

Regrettable scenes were witnessed at the Co-operative Hall, Sankey-street, on Sunday afternoon. We are strongly opposed to the ' opinions of those who organised this gather- ing. The welfare of our country, the interest of civilisation, the future of humanity, de- mand that we should put forth our whole strength in this conflict, and we need the help of all the manhood of the country to ensure rittory. Defeat would be so disastrous that every legitimate means must be adopted to avert it. But while our own convictions are strong, we must recognise that those who oppose the Compulsory Bill have as much right to. hold and to express their opinion as ft& -e have. Milton says, "Give me liberty to know, to utter, and to argue freely according to conscience, above all liberties." Freedom of thought; the right to exercise and express private judgment should be protected at all hazards. Liberty is meaningless where the right to utter one's thoughts and opinions cease to exist. ' True, there are limits even to the freedom of speech ;a man may not slander his neighbour under the cloak of this freedom; but in the case in question those who organ- ised this gathering are perfectly within their constitutional rights.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0073.txt | included=true | included =====
THE DEFECTS Oe FORCE.

The unfortunate result of preventing by force the exercise of such rights is that it defeats its object. In this case it gives sup- port to the contention of those who are opposed to this Bill. They tell us that the compulsion, which we deem to be necessary to save the country, will be extended and used in other spheres, and thit the rights and liberties, which have been won by years, and maybe centuries, of effort, will be imperilled.

It enables them to say, "You say you are fighting for humanity's right against Ger- many's might, but by your action you employ your might against our right." The forceful suppression of the expression of legitimate opinion is ever a mistake. Those who pre- vented at this meeting the free expression of opinion would, we think, have acted more wisely if they had listened to what the speakers had to say and Voted down any reso- lution proposed which, in their opinion, was against the national interest.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0074.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PROVOCATION WAS GREAT.

While we stand for the freedom of speech, and consider the suppression of the gathering wrong in principle and mistaken in tactics, it is only right Jo say that the provocation was great. This little band of dissentients using the name of the traders and workers of War- rington, had, without authority, challenged by public meeting. the determination of a vast majority of the citizen workers of all classes to make every necessary sacrifice to win a war-. The sacrifices made are in very many cases very real and costly. There are few families in Warrington who have not one or more of the members exposed to danger in. the fight- ing line. There are many families in which death has already made gaps. These are facts" which stir the deepest of human feelings. The known opinions of the organisers of this gathering were considered, rightly or wrongly, an outrage, because it appeared to disparage the sacrifice and belittle the patriotism of those who were giving of their best for the life of the nation, while these men were stand- ing aside in safety under the cloak of an. ignoble neutrality.

The meeting was called by a circular,- the only local names upon which were those of the President and the Secretary of the Trades and Labour Council. There is ample evi- dence to Show that this Council is not in accord with the 'opinion of the great majority of the members' it claims to represent. The mem- bers of the Council were, we believe, urging their own ideas, instead of interpreting the opinion of their constituents. If this be so the meeting was held under false pretences, and no doubt this was one of the factors which created a condition .of mind which re- sulted in the unfortunate- disturbance. What we !lave. said can fairly be urged iu palliation of the action taken by the opposition at the meeting, although does not justify it. We trust for the future the citizens of Warrington will so act as to maintain the character they have proudly held in the past for toleration and fairness, for the freedom of opinion, and for its undisturbed constitutional expression in public meeting.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, JANUARY 29, 1916.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0078.txt | included=true | included =====
GOSSIP.

It being a detail of etiquette to take off one's hat when addressing a meeting, it was becoming that some of the Wiredrawers at their gathering in the Druids' Hall on Satur- day should have called upon delinquents, in this respect.

One man who got up, however, refused to comply with the requests of the audience. "It's no use you talking to me like that," he said from under his bowler hat, " I'm not taking mine off. I have a bump on my head which was put there by a German !"

It appeared that the speaker was a wounded soldier who had returned to his trade. The audience forgave him.

Heedlos of the weather. some speakers when hording forth in the, open, always bare their heads out of respect for the audience— one presumes.

Mr. Gladstone was particularly insistent in this practice, no matter what the weather was like. On one occasion, I recollect, he was speaking in Hawarden Park, when it started to rain sharply. Mrs. Gladstone held out his hat to him, but he declined, and when she held her umbrella over him he looked posi- tively annoyed. But she maintained her ground and probably saved him from taking cold.

At the Wiredrawers' meeting, Mr. Wilkin- son Haigh hinted that Mr. John Haley, their delegate to the Trades Council, had resigned because he did not want to face the music.

The incident reminded me of John Graham, the pork-packer, in "Letters from a self-made merchant to his son," who, in writing of a subtle youth, said: "I got his resignation one day just five minutes before the one whieh I was having written out for. him was ready; for I will do Simpkins the justice to say that there was nothing slow about him."

We are to have more light in Warringtom

Complaints having been received in ccinnec-, tion with the. restrictions on the lighting of our main streets, and the Town Council hav- ing advised that there is no legal Order in force restricting the lighting, the Electricity and, Tramways Committee have resolved that the engineer shall confer with the Chief Con- stable with a view to an improvement.

I think those who frequent the town will regard that as good news. Badly-lighted towns look despondent. No wonder we have pessimists!

Naturally, licensees in Warrington are con- cerued about the new restriction order. On Saturday the, "Manchester Guardian" was the only newspaper which succeeded in pub- lishing a forecast of the order, and I under- stand that not a copy could be purchased any- where in the town after lloo'clock. Licensees had bought them all.

In speaking of the needs of our Territorials in France, their Chaplain (the Rev. W. Bracecamp) at St. Paul's Church. on Sunday evening, mentioned that there was a great scarcity of writing paper, and he had known men scribble their letters for home on cigar- ette paper.

When one wants to write one always finds a Means. Mr. Chesterton was Once in a ecinntry railway station_ waiting-room and bethought himself of his weekly article for a contempo- rary. He had not. any paper with him, nor had. he decided on • a subject for his column. So he plucked a sheaf of green excursion bills from a nail in the wall, and, placing them on his knee, wrote eloquently on—cabbages. The colour of the bills and the fact that he was in the country' suggested the subject. •

That there should have been no Haggis at the Burns' celebration by the Warrington Caledonian Association on Tuesday evening was a tremendous disappointment to the diners. The Haas was despatched from Scotland on Monday, and arrangements were carefully made for its reception. But it went astray.

In announcing the fact to the gathering the President (Mr. J. L. Davenport) was mournful, but philosophical.

"How it has allowed itself to get lost, I cannot imagine," he said. " Any self-respect- ng Haggis which had an appointment with Scotsmen would have taken the job into its own, hands and come through on its own'!"

Captain Mathams remarked that in future if he were anywhere where there was a great omission he would not say, "It is like play- ing Hamlet without the Prince of Denmark," but "It is like a Burns' night without Hag gis "—a remark hailed with laugnter and cheers.

But, really it was a case for tears.

At the same gathering, Dr. Naden, a guest, was a little Critical in a literary, . way. He drew attention to the menu card, which had a 'quotation from Wordsworth in praise of Robbie.Burns,.and.added that from..his know- ledge of the two he thought the English poet was not a great admirer of the Secittish bard; in fact, the upshot of his criticism was, "What a fine man Burns would have been if he had not been what he was!"

Thus, Dr. Naden showed that 'Englishmen can be shrewd critics, as well as Scotsmen.

The meetings of the three principal public bodies in Warrington have all been of short duration this month.

Had the Town Council, Insurance Com- mittee, and Education Committee been all held together, they would not have lasted more than a quarter of an hour.

At the Education Committee on Monday, members were still arriving when the business had been concluded. Four minutes only were occupied. It was a case of 'taking off one's coat to put it on again. VIGILANT.

Attested Men In Leather Trade.

The Warrington correspondent of the "Leather Trades Review " says:—

" A stroft trade was -moving all through the week, with. a full inquiry for all classes; there- fore leather was "moving quickly,.and output was large, and only physiCal difficulties prevented it 'being considerably larger. The chief of these are the shortage of labour and the inability of the railways to handle anything near the weight of goods tendered to them. Bends of Army substances were in a very strong demand; all supplies in sight both large and small were soon taken up, and generally at an advanced figure. Medium weights ,in all selec- tions were in almost as strong re- quest, and, supplies being altogether in- adequate, buyers were forced to be content to cover their requirements in a partial faithion. Lights were also in much request, but again brought out very few offers. Prices are all on the up grade, and,•with the demand running so strongly as it does, progress is likely to be rapid. Internal conditions are more settled, as it now seems clear that our attested men will not be called up for service, but the minor difficulty remains that it is well-nigh impossible to engage the extra labour which is so badly wanted.",

TRADE UNIONIST REVOLT

Against The Pretensions Of

The Trades Council.

FURTHER ACTION THREATENED.

On another page we give a full report of the meeting of the Wiredrawers' Society, at which they decided to secede from the Warrington Trades and Labour Council. Many—we might almost say all—the Trade Unions affiliated with the Council are considering its recent actions with a view to deciding upon the best course to be adopted in the circumstances.

ENGINEERS MAY MOVE.

A Union IVhich•Has Already Seceded

Twice.

_ We learn from MT. W. Oxley, secretary of the Warrington Branch of the Amalgamated Society of Engineers, that a suggestion has been made that a special meeting shall be held to consider the resolution of the Trades Coun- cil, and it is quite possible tliat this take place. From his own observation and the reports of members, Mr. Oxley estimated that the majority are against the Trades Council's de- cision, and, he mentioned, as one reason for this, the fact that 70 out of a membership of over 450 are serving with the Forces. If the society dissociates itself from the Trades Council, this will not be the first time it has done so. On two occasions it has seceded from the Council, owing' to differences of opinion,' and has only been affiliated with them this last time for six months.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0081.txt | included=true | included =====
THE POSTMEN

Want Unpatriotic Resolution Rescinded.

The Warrington Branch of the Postmen's Federation have asked the Trades Council to rescind their resolution against the Govern- ment Act, and upon the reply depends what they will do With regard to remaining affiliated.

A Voice From The Trenches.

A feature of the meeting, which was held at the Post Office on Wednesday evtning, was the condemnation of the Trades Council by Lance-Corporal Hewitt, an es-postman, who was on furlough. At great personal inconvenience he attended the meeting solely from• a sense of duty to protest against the Council's un- patriotic Act. The first resolution carried (with only one dissentient) was proposed by Mr. J. Davies and seconded by Mr. C. Gallaway: "That this. branch meeting of the Postmen's Federa- tion condemns the action of the Warrington Trades and Labour Council in passing a reso- lution on such a vital question as the Military Service Bill as not voicing the true feelings of the trade unions of Warrington." Lance-Corporal Hewitt proposed; and Mr. J. Davies seconded, a resolution that the branch , should secede from the Trades Council, but the following amendment was carried: "That the Trades Council be asked to withdraw the reso- lution condemning the Military Service Bill until such time as the trade unionists of War- rington have voted on it, and that no further, action be taken by the branch until we know , of the result." . •

Mr. T. Walsh- presided over the meeting, which, we understand, was the most rpplesen- tative that has been held during the last eighteen months.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0082.txt | included=true | included =====
BAKERS • AND CONFECTIONERS

Say Council's Action Is Against Their

Interest.

The Warrington Branch of Abe Amalgamated Union of Operative Bakers and Confectioners also protested against the action Of the Trades Council.

When they met on Saturday they passed un- animously a resolution declaring that by their attitude of hostility the Trades and Labour Council were overstepping the bounds of the principle of trade unionism, and that their action- was against the interests of trade unionists.

THE TRADES AND LABOUR COUNCIL AND THE WAR.

To the Editor of "The Examiner."

Sir,—The bold stand you took in so ably challenging the "Truth of the Syndicate" of the so-called Council, in your issue of the 15th, is most creditable and worthy of the reward so well given by "A Worker" and the Wiredrawers' and Iron and? Steel Workers' Unions., • _ „ _

Well may the question be asked: "For whom do the Trades Council speak?" Surely I should be justified in asking how long will my fellow- toilers submit to the bellowings of the few— what shall they be termed? Not men, for their past actions are those of cowards. These blatant individuals continually set themselves up, select/the music, and the affili- ated members paying the costs each and every time. Having. had an experience as delegate, like many others, you soon learn what the atmosphere was like if you. were not inside the fold. _ _

Without doubt our genuine Trade Unionism is shelved and misrepresented by the Syndi- cate of Independent Labour Party, British Socialist party, Town Hall-seeking party, &c., pure and simple. The dilemma they find themselves in to-day has been flowly but Surely developing by their unpatriotic spirit throughout. The true spirit is budding into flower that was advocated in the "Labour Gazette," July, 1914, of the standard to "Be a Man," and reprinted in your columns about that time. In closing, let us hone our "Borough." which we are proud of, shall not be. blighted ,by the doings of these few; but .rathei, like Oldham and other well-organised Trides. Councils, complimented for doing our bit in 'answer to our country's call.—Yours, .&c.,

ONE WHO HAS SECEDED.

MR. HAROLD SMITH, M.P., AND

THE PERSIA.

Questioned by Mr. H. Smith (U—Warrington) in the House of Commons on Tuesday, -Mr. Runciman (President of the Board of Trade) said: A formal investigation has been +ordered into the circumstances attending the loss of the s.s. Persia, and the question whether the statutory rules for life-saving appliances ' .applicable to a ship of this class' ;were duly ,complied with, and whether due heed was given in the 'case of ,this ship • to the .warnings which have been issued by the" Board-of Trade in re- gard to the additional risks to ships arising out of the,,present war, will be dealt with in the course of the investigation. While the in;,,esti- gation is an investigation into the loss of this particular ship and the loss of life ensuing therefrom and is not in regard to ocean-going steamers generally, any recommendations or observ,Ations that the Court holding the investi- gation may think fit to make will be carefully considered. A general investigation on general matters will necessitate another inquiry. Mr. Runciman further informed Mr. Smith that in October last the Board of Trade issued a notice to owners and masters of passenger steamers suggesting that all ports and scuttles open in the dining saloons of such steamers should be closed when travelling in danger zones. , Mr. Smith: Are they permitted to Wive ports and scuttles open in danger zones? Mr. Runciman replied that the Board of Trade had suggested to 'owners an masters that under certain circumstances the ports and scut- tles should be shut.

Absence from School and Medical

Certificates.

The School Management Committee of the Warrington Education Committee have been un- able to accede to a request by the Warrington Division of the British Medical Association, who wrote asking that the committee should modify their demands for medical certificates in respect of children absent from school. After careful consideration the committee stated it was resolved that the Association be informed that, in all cases where sickness is the alleged reason for absence from school, the onus of proof rests upon the parent or guardian, and the com- mittee regret, therefore, that they are unable to suggest any alternative to the present practice with respect to the necessity for medical cer- tificates..

Fire Brigade's Annual Dinner.

• The annual dinner of the -Warrington • Fire Brigade was held at the Fire Station on Wednes- day evening. An excellent repast was provided• by the officers' wives, and was iollowed by a smoking concert. The Superintendent, Mr. F. 1.1. Turner, presided, and was supported by the Deputy-Superintendent, Mr. Hy. Cook., and ex- Superintendent, Mr. Frank Turner, sen, Engi- neers Wakefield and Vernon, and all the mem- bers of the brigade. The loyal toasts were sub- mitted by the Chairthan. Sergt. Clegg proposed a vote of thanks to "The Subscribers." It gave him very great pleasure on behalf of the auxili- ary, or part paid, firemen of the brigade to do this. It was a time-honoured custom, and he was in- formed by their-collector that he had no trouble in raising the money, in spite of the many calls that were made upon the subscribers, thus show- ing that the services of the auxiliary firemen were giving satisfaction. The proposal was seconded by Engineer Wakefield, and carried with cheers. :The health of "The Superintend- ent and Officers" was given. The Superintend- ent, in reply, said they had had a very quiet year, with no serious fires except one, when they were assisted by the Wilderspool Brewery motor engine and brigade in fine work. There were 26 calls during the year. The damage was about the same. Three men of the brigade were with the Forces, and he. hoped they would have a , safe return. Deputy-Superintendent Hy. Cook and Mr. F. Turner, sen., also replied. Songs were given by Firemen Vernon, R. Cook and W. Berrisford, and Mr. J. Carter anil Mr. Kenney, who also acted as accompanist.

Death of Mr. E. E.. Pester.

We regret to record the death of Mr. Edwin Elijah Pester, which occurred suddenly at his residence, Orford Green Farm, on Sunday. Mr. Pester, who was 48 years of age, was at busi- ness us usual at the "Guardian Office on Saturday, and rose on Sunday apparently in his normal health. During the morning, however, he complained of feeling unwell', and died from heart failure' shortly before noon. To mourn his loss, Mr. Pester leaves a wiabw, two daugh- ters and six sons. Two of the last-named are serving at the Front, one in the R.F.A. and another in an ammunition column. Mr. Pester was well-known in Warrington, being a trustee of the Buckley-street Methodist Free Church and treasurer of the " Garibaldi " Lodge of the National Order of Oddfellows.

Amateur Photographers' Exhibition.

The evening dev—oteli to the "Amateur Photographer" prize slides, is always looked forward to as one of the events of the winter session in connection with the Warrington

Photographic Society, and the large 'umber of members present on Tuesday night suffered no disappointment. The winning six were a set of yachting and other sea studies of charming quality and tone. The portrait and figure section was especially good, deriving most of its attraction from pictures of children at play. One study, "The Kid,— was particularly' admired for its rendering of a little girl's hair and laughing face. Six pictures recording tlw• bombardment of the "fortified town" of Scar- borough were the winners in the "Record " section, and the comparatively new colour pro- cess introduced by Messrs. Paget won unstinted praise for the prize slide "Among the Flowers," and another "Tired Out," where the colours were delightfully rendered, both of boy's face and of the straw amongst which he was lying.

Death Of Well-known Gentleman At Advanced Age.

It is with regret that we record the death of Mr. Wililam Gill, at his residence, 20, 'Win- marleigh-street, on Thursday night: Mr. Gill, who was 77 years old, had been un- well for some time, and his many friends deeply sympathised with him. Mr. Gill retired from business about 25 years ago.

S'COCKTON

HEATH GARDEN

SOCIETY MEETING.

The Stockton Heath Garden Society is one of those societies that have been hit—in the financial sense—by the war, but taking every- thing into consideration it is in a strong position. Chiefly as a result of the war and file bad weather of August Bank Holiday, the end of the year found • their bank balance £2l less than it was at the beginning. But the Society has £54 in the bank.

The annual general meeting was held in the Church Room, Stockton Heath, last night, Sir Gilbert Glreenall presiding. The Chairman said lie thought the Society, under the circumstances, had done better than they had anticipated, and they could congratu- late themselves on the way in which they had come through the year.

Lectures on Eastern Life.

Those who perused the reports •of 'Mr. A. Mounfield's lectures on Eastern Lite will be glad to learn, from our advertisement columns, that a second series of addresses will be commenced on Friday next, in Friars' Green Schoolroom. During this session Mr. Mounfield will deal ex- clusively with Eastern life in the days of Jesus and the apostles, and the lectures will contain descriptions of social and political conditions in Galilee and Jerusalem. Among the subjects to be introduced will be Jewish beliefs and preju- dices, the Jewish sects, life in the Synagogues and Temple, and the surroundings of the early . Church. The subject has been chosen in view, of the fact that the Sunday school- lessons for 1916 deal with the Early Church and the Book of Acts. Admission is free, and all students and teachers are invited.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0107.txt | included=true | included =====
BURNS THE PATRIOT.

Memory Honoured By Local Caledonians

===== 0002601_19160129_art0108.txt | included=true | included =====
WITH SPEECH, SONG AND GOOD CHEER.

It is war-time, but Scots the world over met on Tuesday to honour with speech, music and. good cheer the immortal memory of their national bard, Robert Burns. The members of the Warrington Caledonian •Association had a concentration march (in singles or. couples, of course) on the Lion Hotel, where they had an enjoyable supper, discounted, however, by the fact that the Haggis, which had been despatched from Scot- land on the previous night, failed to present itself in time for consumption. So the hanqueteers went Haggis-less. Mr. J. L. Davenport (the president) easily justified the celebration, first remarking

"We're met again tae celebrate This Janwar twenty-five, And I wither what his thochts wad be Had Rabbie been alive? • Ms pen had framed an epic. On the horrors o' the day, And Britain's sons been heartened Wi anither 'Scots wha hae."

"There never lived a greater patriot than Burns," he added, "and his poetry has been an inspiration to our Scottish soldiers in What- ever clime they have fought." Sympathetic reference was made to the fact that three, of their members had fallen in the war.

And if he had their vices, God gave him them in order• that he might understand human nature the better. Let us thank God that in Robert Burns there mingled the high and the low, the good and the bad, in order that he might read the problem of life as a book and tell is all about it.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
BURNS THE SOUL OF SCOTLAND.

It was fitting that the proposing of the prime toast of the evening—" The Immortal Memory "—should have been allotted to a soldier—Captain Mathams—who eloquently dis- coursed on the patriotism of the poet. First, however, he spoke of Burns in a general way. "I do not think we always realise what that man has meant for Scotland,". he said. "and what he has done for Scotland, but to me I think that in reading into the soul of Robert Burns we are reading into the soul of Scot- land, its greatness and its weakness, but its abiding and lasting strength. And so long as Scotsmen- live and gather together on such nights like this to think on Robert Burns, on 'what he Sang and on what he talked, Scotsmen will always be worthy of the heritage of their race. (Cheers.) We scarcely stop .to think of what he has done for us, what purpose ani- mated him, andlwhat love of Scotland he had. Here we have a man who sprang from the soil and declared that so far as he was concerned, '-hodden gray' was the thing for him and also the homespun habit.' He did a great service to our race. When all the great thinkers, and great minds of Scotland gifted—thanks to the foresight of our forebears—with a finer educa- tion than our English brethren possessed;. when they, with the calculation of which only 'Scotsmen are capable, said, 'We have brains and knowledge, we May not have much else, but we have a message. to give to the world,' .they decided to give that message not in the, hamely 'Scots',' but in• the language which they had the foresight to see would reach the widest circle. They saw that if Scotsmen— cultured and learned,.--were to triumph it would' -he through the English language. Our Rob- ertsons, Humes, and Adam Smiths spoke in the English tongue not because they thought little of their own, but because they realised that the brains of Scotland -were worthy of reaching to the uttermost parts of the earth. :(Cheers.) That was a noble attitude—an atti- tude of sacrifice for the Empire. Scotsmen First; Britons Next.

"But Robert Burns came along, and, in his simple ploughman way, realised that what; had made Scotsmen great was not the learning of its big thinkers and the scholarship of its Universities, but the simple life of our people —simple religion, simple ideals, and a true love of the beautiful that, God had laid before them. It was in the braed Scots' that he appealed to us and let flow the music of his soul in order that man might be a great force for the betterment of humanity and for the furtherance of God's good will. That is what Robert Burns did for us in choosing to use the Scottish tongue, and we owe it to him to keep alive that Scottish tongue as long as possible, .and after we have lost it—if we do lose it—we must keep it on our minds if we cannot keep it on our lips. (Cheers.) We must, be Scots- men first and Britons afterwards. We shall be better Britons if we are Scotsmen first." (Cheers.)

Continuing, the Captain said that critics often filmed their bitter criticism and satire upon Burns, telling us he was a drunkard, and much worse. They told us that he was a man who sang songs, but whose life was a discredit to .the country which begot him. "To these critics I would say this," added the speaker: " Robert Burns was no accident, not a chance ploughman born in a chance cottage. He was a man whom God in His wisdom sent into the •world for the good of Scotland and succeeding generations. Therefore, in Robert Burns—in his soul, aye, and even in his body—you had accumulated all that went to make up Scot- land: the spirit of the martyrs, the spirit of the scholars, and the spirit of the peasants.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0110.txt | included=true | included =====
BIG WHIST DRIVE.

450 Guests Play In Parr Hall.

On Thursday evening there took place in the Parr Hall a whist drive and dance, Organised by the workiis of the Thelwail Heys Military Hospital, in aid of the upkeep of the estab- lishment. .

The affair was a 'brilliant success, for fully 450 sat down at the tables to participate in this popular card game. Twenty-four hands were played. The hall was tastefully decor- ated, the festoons and flags adding additional colour to many charming gowns worn by the ladies. Naturally there was a predominance of ladies, and many of the in-patients of the hos- pital, in their light grey suits, participated in the entertainment. Many nurses also were not deprived of the chance of winning any of the handsome prizes for successful competitors; although, curiously, none of the staff- was in winning vein. __

A Patriot From Boyho.d.

"We cannot, in a time like this, forget that, above all things, Robert Burns was a patriot. When he was a.boy and wondering what he was going to be,, he always had one thought in his mind, and that was the service of his country. . . . - Turn to him and study the motives which animated him. Remember that Burns was just a Scot like ourselves, and that we, thank God, are Scots like Burns. And, when it comes to the stress and strain of war, again let us turn to Burns. He wrote a letter once describing the first two books he had ever read ----one was "-The Life of Hannibal,' and the other The Life of William • Wallace.' He said of the first that it gave him such a turn that he strutted up and down with a drum and bagpipe waiting until he was tall enough to be a soldier. of the life of Wallace he said it had stirred him so much that his blood would boil until the curtain Of life dropped into everlasting rest. There you had the patriot, the man who had the country's past and dreamed of his country's future; one who would always battle for freedom and against tyranny. (Cheers.) It is no use to speculate on what would happen if Burns were alive to-day, but if he were here in the flesh and not merely in the spirit you may depend upon it that he would be in the forefront of the battle. He would be where the danger was ,greatest; not because he loved fighting,. but ' for pair auld Scotland's sakei-" (Cheers.) Captain Mathams conceived an admirable finish to his address with the following quota- tion, from the poetry of the man he had been honouring :

" Wha for Scotland's Kirig and law, Freedom's sword will strongly draw: Free-man stand, or Free-man ía'? Let him follow me!

By Oppression's woes a-nd pains! By your sons in servilo chains! We will drain our dearest veins, But they shall be free!

Lay the proud usurpers low! Tyrants fall in every foe! Liberty's in every blow ! Let us do or die ! ! ! "

THE MAYOR SAYS WE HAVE FEW SLACKERS.

Another toast which was honoured with en- thusiasm was that of ." The Town We Live Mr. J. Rymer Young proposed it in a racy speech, and The Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock), in replying, said he was more than .gratified by Warrington's response to the call to the colours. Warrington was not noted for slack- ers, but there were a few. With regard to our. industries We were about the most fortunate town in England. Warrington was quite all right to live in, provided you made friends and went the straight way in all your dealings. (Applause.) It was true that we had to pay high rates, but, to some extent, we could blame our forebears for that. In tke same way, he supposed, our successors would blame us, but we had a good excuse, for the war had neces- sarily put U.S off ,things. Since. he had been Mayor he had had a very happy time working with colleagues who had devoted their energies for the good of the town. (Applause.) Hia only object in life at present was to see the war at an end. He was afraid it would not be in the next month or two, but he hoped that soon we should smash the- Germans. "Then I shall be the happiest man in Warrington," be concluded amid cheers.

Mr. George Bowman proposed "The Visitors" toast, and Dr. Naden responded. Mr. James McDermid proposed the toast of " The President" (Mr. Davenport), remarking that they had been exceptionally fortunate in their presidents, and never were they more fortunate than in the present one. The toast was accepted with Highland hon- ours, the company mounting the chairs and placing one foot on the 'table, their glasses meanwhile held aloft. "He's a jolly good fellow.' rang out glowingly. Several members contributed songs of their nationality, Mr. Joseph Hunter singing "There was a lad was born in Kyle." For about 20 years in succession Mr. Hunter has made this his selection. Mr. .J. Robertson and Mr. Geo. Marshall also sang Scottish lyrics, and Mr. Arthur Pearce, Mr. H. M. Ashton, and Mrs. E. A. Sanders were among the other artistes.

Supporting the President at the head table were the Mayor, Captain Mathams, Mr. J. Rymer Young, Dr. Manson, Dr. , Naden. and Mr. W. Russell. Mr. J. Tennant, honorary secretary of the association, had charge of the arrangements.

LANCE-CORPORAL J. WHITTAKER

One Of A Soldier-Family Over On Sick Leave.

Lance-Corporal James Whittaker, of the Warrington Territorials, who resides at 4, Church-street, has arrived home on sick leave. He has now almost recovered, and shortly ex- pects orders to return to duty. When first brought to England he was, for a time, a patient in a. Sheffield hospital. Lance-Corporal Whittaker, who is 24 years of age, has been in the Territorials fifteen months, and has been at the Front about six months. There he has been acting as a bomb- thrower. He fearned his stripe while at the Front. He was educated at St. Mary's School, Buttermarket-street, and before enlisting was employed in the Telegraph Department of the G.P.O. His father and his only brother are also in the Army, the former at Salonika, and the latter—who is a Regular—is stationed in England.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0119.txt | included=true | included =====
THE COLONEL,

" My Honour Is Due To My

Lads."

REV. W. BRACECAMP'S NEWS. Being on leave from France last week-end, the Rev. W. Bracecamp tendered some interesting news of "The Unbreakable Fourth" at Sunday evening's service in St. Paul's Church. He mentioned that the other night he had the privilege of taking a paper to Colonel Brereton Fairclough and congratulating him upon the honour of C.M.G. just conferred on him, and the reply was: "It is kind of you to congratu- late me, but the honour is due to my lads. The honour is given to me, but it is really for the lads I represent." "The Unbreakable Fourth !" the Chaplain went on, I could tell you of deeds of valour, how the men fight under all difficul- ties. They are made of stuff we ought to be proud of, and to 'every infantry soldier that passes you, should raise your hat in humble recognition of the men who are willing to die for liberty and justice. Don't be down-hearted. We are not. We are as bright and as cheerful as we can be in the circumstances. We 'still joke with each other. We don't pull long faces. We still enjoy ourselves at concerts. We stiri live as if we were human beings, and we are quite confident that in the end we shall pre- vail. Greater courage will be given to us if you at home will only do your duty by crying and pleading unto the Lo,rd that peace may be restored, and we may come home. Message From The Boys.

When he (the Chaplain) left France, the mes- sage that "The Unbreakable Fourth" gave to him was, "Tell them that we are all right. Tell them that we are quite cheerful. Tell them that we are not downhearted, and tell them that we are prepared to do our duty, even if it costs us our lives." It was a fact that they had sent some of the bravest fellows to the Front, fellows who were full of courage and energy, and were patriots tn the very core, ready to sacrifice their lives at any moment for King, country, and God. So far, so good! But were they doing their duty at home by pray- ing unto God. "I don't know what you are doing at St. Paul's," he added. "I don't know; whether you are better than anyone else, but this church should be crowded from end to end every time there is an intercession service, because a lot depends upon it. We out there don't want to be away from our loved ones longer than we can help, as it is so lonely, so cruel. But there we have to struggle, there we have to live. The peace of a country depends upon the Christian people at home. "I have been in London for the past three or four days, but I did not see any difference. There is the same rush for the theatres and the restaurants, so far as I could judge. I cannot see any mark at home—l don't mean St. Paul's congregation—that you are feeling the pangs of war at all. And it is a war we are up against, and you have little idea as to what it means It means that your lads are standing six, seven, eight, nine and ten days in mud, almost up to their armpits. They cannot lie down, they can- not sleep, and there they are watching for' the enemy, protecting your homes, the homes of your wives and children, doing this for liberty, for honour, and yet we are not 'Crying unto the Lord.'" There was no need for pessimism, for we were going to win if we put every ounce of strength, both military and industrially, into our cause. "Never mind your trade unionism," he declared. "Let that drop for the moment. Put in every ounce of strength you have got, and I promise you that 'sooner than you think we will be privileged to, rejoice that peace has been proclaimed, because we as a nation have done our duty and given of our best." •

"We went into the trenches! last Saturday

night, and, as usual, we were in a place where there were no dug-outs, and had to sleep on the fire-step. Owing to my being on guard, I did not get much sleep. pn the Sunday the Huns started shelling us, and, while I was digging in the morning we were shelled like fury. Well, we had the usual rest from fatigues in the. afternoon, and so I began to I write a letter to Jim, but, unluckily, I did not finish it, for a, shell came which I did not hear and I found myself absolutely •buried, and people digging me out. I was not con- scious for long, aad I suppose I was buried for about an hour. When 1 found myself again I was lying in aniither part Of the trench with a stretcher-bearer. I was.taken on the stretcher to the first-aid station and there I stayed the night. I am in a rest station now, but I don't sappose I will stay here long." . Private Fellows, who is 21 years of age, was eniployed in the laboratory at the Technical School for three years .and also in Crosfield's laboratory for a like number .of years. He was also+ a lieutenant in the Free Church Boys' Brigade, belonging to Thewlis-street, where he was in the Bible Class. He enlisted at Whit- suntide, and has been at the Front for three months.

Battery-Sergeant-Major William Joseph Price, of the Royal Field Artillery, whose mother lives at 30, Alexandra-street, Warrington, has been awarded the D.C.M., as reported in our last issue, for services rendered while serving "some- where in France." He is 42 years of age, and a native of Aldershot, although he has resided in Warrington for quite a number of years. Four of his brothers are also in His Majesty's service, one of whom, Samuel James Price, the well- known assistant master of St. Mary's School, became Lieut.-Quartermaster in the local Ter- ritorials in November.

MEMORIAL SERVICE TO PRIVATE RANDLES.

A large congregation assembled at the Work- ing Men's Mission Church, on Sunday, to pay a tribute of respect to the late Private T. Randles, who died a„fortnight ago, falling a victim to an attack of dysentery. The service was conducted by Mr. J. Harrison, of Liverpool, who also preached an eloquent sermon. During the even, ing a memorial 'address was given by Mr. R. Jones. Special hymns were sung, and the "Last Post" was sounded by 'members of the Boy Scouts, who attended the service.

HOME THOUGHTS.

Soldier's Sympathy For Those Not In

The Trenches.

Often in the trench I think Of the poor chaps at home, Of the perils that surround them Wherever they may roam:

The. train and the tram collisions, The Juggernaut -motor car, Bacteria in cow's milk, And Zeppelins from afar.

How awful it must be at night To lie in a feather bed; To find for breakfast when you rise That there's butter on your bread.

With all those shocking worries A man's life must be sad, And to think I'm missing all of them Makes me exceeding glad. -

Now out here things are different, And our life is fancy free; We have no butter on our bread, Nor cow's milk in our tea;

There are no tram collisions, Nor feather beds at night, And Zeppelins never trouble us— They keep well out of sight.

And all we have to worry us Are bullets, bombs and shells, With bully beef and biscuits, And nasty horrid smells..

To the chaps of good old Warrington I send my sympathy, . And ask them for their safety To come out here with me.

Private, T. J. Taylor, Warwickshire Regiment, in France—" a Wartingtonian bred and born."-

THREE BROTHERS SERVING.

Mr. J. A. L. Delaney Obtains Commis- sion In 10th Royal Lanes.

By Mr. James Alfred Leo Delaney having obtained a commission in the 10th Royal Lancasters (4th King's Own), three sons of Mr. Peter Delaney, of 19, Museum-street, Warring- ton, are now serving in the Army. The elder brothers, Peter. and Richard, are in the 2nd South Lancs. Regiment, in training in the South of England. Nineteen years of age, the new officer made a name for himself among his schoolfellows. At the Boteler Grammar School he held the swim- ming championship for three years, and was also eminent in other branches of sport. When at St. Francis Xavier's College, Liverpool, he showed his athletic prowess by winning the senior boxing competition. Leaving the college he entered Liverpool University as a medical student, and five months ago joined the Officers' Training Corps. As well as a keen soldier, in Mr. Delaney his regiment will have a sportsman of whom they may be proud. Mention should be made of his skill as a footballer, and that offers for his assistance have been made by several first-class teams.

CLEVER CAPTURE.

Private T. Fernehough Prevents German From Escaping.

Mrs. Thomas rernehough, 43, Scott-street, Warrington, has this week received a letter from her husband, Private Fernehough, who is at present .guarding German prisoners in an in- ternmpnt camp in the isle of Man. From the particulars in his letter it is evident that he has been instrumental in stopping a German prisoner from escaping, for he writes : lam in the best of health. You ,will be glad to know that I was successful in capturing a German who tried to escape, so much so that _ .

_ I have been highly commended by the Adjutant, who gave me a monetary reward. He sent for me on Sunday morning and told me I had done a very good piece of work. I said I considered 1 had only done my duty as a soldier. They say it is a clever capture, so I am glad I have done a little bit, as he meant getting away. He had some cardboard boxes under his arm when I pulled him up, I expect with a rig-out if he had got past." Private ,Fernehough joined His Majesty's Forces upon the outbreak of war, but was re- jected for foreign servieo, so eventually he was sent to the Isle of Man for the purpose of guard- ing the captured German soldiers stationed there. Previous to enlistment he was employed at Messrs. J. Crosfield and• Sons, Ltd., on their oil plant, and his regiment is the 2/4th South La ncashires.

AATarrington Consignment: Donations

Towards Cost.

• The Voluntary Organisation of Workers for providing comforts for soldiers, working under the direction of the War Office, with head- quarters at the Town Hall, received a requisi- tion last Saturday for eight bed-jackets, 25 pairs of bed socks, eight dressing gowns, 2: helpless case bed-jackets, 50 pairs of slio9ers, 12 pairs of operating stockings. These will be dispatched to France Pn Mon- day. Some have been made by the lady workers, and the remainder have been pur- chased, and money is urgently required to pay for them. Donations towards this cost will be gladly received by the hon. treasurer, Mr. John Fairhurst, borough treasurer. The Mayor is president, Mr. J. H. Smethurst chairman, and 'Mr. Henry Collin hon.'secretary of this praise- worthy organisation.

DRINK RESTRIeTIONS.

Week-day Hours In Public Houses Like Sunday.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0128.txt | included=true | included =====
THE WHOLE OF LANCASHIRE AND CHESHIRE INVOLVED.

This district has been " scheduled " in ex- tended areas, which are being proclaimed in various parts of the country for the limitation of the sale of intoxicating liquor by the Control Board. '

--6;crers in Council were published in a Supplement to the "London Gazette," issued on Thursday night, extending the areas in which the sale and supply of intoxicating liquor shall be controlled by the State under the Defence of the Realm (Liquor Control) Regulations, 1915, "on the grounds that war material is being made, loaded, and unloaded and dealt with in transit therein." In the case of the first area scheduled the words "and that men belonging to His Maj- esty's military forces are assembled therein" are added to the grounds for State control. This area is entitled the Lancashire and Che- shire Area and is defined as being:—

The area comprising the Counties of Lancas- ter, Chester, and Flint, including all cities and county boroughs within the geographical limits thereof (excepting the county borough of Barrow-in-Furness and the Petty Sessional Divisions of Hawkshead and North Lonsdale, in the county of Lancaster, and the Petty Ses- sional Division of Overton, and the Parish of Merford and Hoseley, in the county of Flint), and the Borough of Glossop, and the Petty Sessional Division of Glossop, in the county of Derby. The existing Liverpool and Mersey District Area, which was defined in the Order in Coun- cil of July 6th last, will form part of the new area, which is remarkably comprehensive, in- cluding as it does Manchester and other big cities and boroughs in the counties named.

and to clubs, and clubs will be put in just thet same position as public-houses. Area.

It will apply not only to Manchester, but to every part of Lancaghire and Cheshire, and also to parts of Flintakire, Hours.

The hours during which intoxicating liquor may be sold or supplied for consumption on the premises will be:— Week-days. Sundays. 12 0-2 30 p.m. 1.2 30-2 30 p.m. 30-9 30 p.m. , 0-9 0 p.m. The hours for sale for consumption off the premises will be the same except that the off- sale will close an hour earlier in the evening. The 'Sunday hours refer only to the English parts of the area; in the Welsh parts Sunday closing will still obtain under the general law. There will be certain exceptions The two most important are that the Order will not be deemed to prohibit where the same is other- wise lawful—(a) the consumption of intoxicat- ing liquor by any person in any licensed pre- mises or club WHERE HE IS RESIDING; or (b) the sale or supply of spirits to any person produc- ing a certificate in writing signed by a duly qualified medical practitioner that the spirits are immediately required for medicinal pur- poses. Hours For Non-Intoxicants.

For the supply of food and non-intoxicating liquor licensed premises and refreshment- houses may still be kept open during the hours permitted by the general Licensing Acts, wad for this purpose licensed premises may be opened at half-past five in the morning, which is half an hour earlier than these Acts permit. Additional Restrictions For 'Spirits. In addition to the general restrictions already stated, there will be special restrictions on the sale of spirits for consumption off the pre- mises:—

(1) No orders for spirits to be consumed off the premises are to be given or accepted from any person actually present in any licensed

REPRESENTATIONS FROM THE LICENSING TRADE.

It is further announced that it is the inten- tion of the Board of Control to make an Order for the purposes of the control of the sale and supply of intoxicating liquor in the area thus defined. Before, however, the exact terms of the Order are settled, the Board are willing to

The opportunity of expressing their views will be welcomed by the Trade, who have con- tended all alnng that they, were entitled to be heard on a matter which concerns them so :vitally, but they will be disappointed that the 'concession was not made before the promul- gation of the schedule, as the area to be in- cluded in the Order was one of the points which they wanted to raise. This means that the sale of spirits for "off" consumption will be, prohibited absolutely on Saturdays and Sun- days, and restricted to two and a half hours on all the other days of the week. (2) Spirits for off consumption cannot be sup- plied from any licensed premises or club in any less quantity than one reputed quart; they cannot be supplied in any open vessel; and the bottle or other vessel in which they are con- tained must bear a label showing the name and situation of the premises from which they are supplied. (3) The sale of spirits for off consumption will be absolutely prohibited at all times in railway refreshment rooms. - Conditions As To Distribution.

PROBABLE CONDITIONS OF THE ORDER. The "Manchester Guardian," which a week ago published, in outline, the regulations which had been finally settled, said yesterday: "The Trade' will be allowed, it appears, to make further representations as to points of detail in the regulations, but there is not the least likelihood that the Manchester Order will differ in any substantial particular from those many others which are already in force." The "Manchester Guardian" London corre- spondent wroth a week ago:— As I have several times indicated, the Order will be exactly the same as the London Order, , and may be summed up as something like Sun- day hours for every day.

There will be several stringent provisions designed to check the hawking of liquor, and these 'will impose new responsibilities on the "off" trade. No liquor is to be delivered from any vehicle or receptacle except in pursuance of an order, the quantity, description, and price of which, together with the name and address of the person to whom it is to be sup- plied have been entered, before despatch, in a delivery-book or invoice and also in a day- book. The delivery-book or invoice must be carried by the distributor and the day-book must be kept on the premises from which the liquor is despatched. The distributor is fur- ther prohibited from receiving payment. Taken in conjunction with -the general pro- hibition of credit, this means that the money must be paid at the time the order is given, and before despatch.

"Treating" Prohibited. There will be several provisions against eva- sions, such as lending or agreeing to lend the money for the drinks. An exception is made for diners-out and such like—if the drink is supplied with a. meal and is consumed at the meal, the person who pays for the food may also pay for the drink. No "Trust."

All liquor, whether intended for on or off consumption must be paid for before or at the time when it is supplied or despatched or taken away.

"Long Pull." Prohibited.

"Bona-fide Travellers." Abolished. Weaker Spirits. The number of degrees under proof in which spirits may be sold without offending against the Food and Drugs Acts will be reduced by ten; whisky, brandy, and rum may be diluted by water up to 35 degrees under proof and gin up to 45 degrees. This will probably benefit the publican, though one presumes that the Board have other benefits in mind. Punishment.

The maximum penalty for a breach of any provision of the Ord4r will be six months' im- prisonment, with hard labour, and a fine of

===== 0002601_19160129_art0130.txt | included=true | included =====
"Two Houses" Daily.

The hours during which licensed houses will be open for the sale of intoxicating liquors will be 12 noon to 2.30 p.m. and 6.30 p.m. to 9.30 p.m. There will be—as in London—half an hour's grace for the consumption of liquor ordered with a meal during the licensed hours. Thus,if you order a bottle of beer or wine with lunch at 2.30 p.m. you are allowed to daily over it till three o'clock, but it must be ordered before 130. The regulations will apply to clubs as well as to public-houses.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0131.txt | included=true | included =====
No Treating; No Credit.

Treating will be prohibited. "Standing drinks" will disappear as a social duty or pleasure, as it has disappeared in London. Credit will be abolished, even for off-sales. Any liquor ordered to be sent home, whether from a 'public-house or an off-licence shop, will have to be paid for beforehand. It will not be permitted to buy a noggin at a public- house to take home. The smallest quantity pf spirits that can be purchased will be a full bottle. It is confidently expected that the new Order in Lancashire will have the same beneficial effects as it has had ,elsewhere. Even the former opponents of the new regulations ack- nowledge that at least up to the present these Orders have made a great difference to the amount of drunkenness and offences due to drunkenness. For my information and prom a comparison of the general provisions which have invariably appeared in other Orders, I think the follow- ing may be safely taken as the effect of the new Order :

Scope. The Order will apply to all licensed premises

DISTRICT NEWS

local Police „Constables Attest.—All the .local _police in the section were attested under Lord Derby's scheme yesterday afternoon, sanction ,having been obtained from the Standing Joint Committee last -Saturday.

Recruiting.—Since the reopening ,of 'the re- cruiting station under the group system of Lord Derby's scheme, many of the unattested men have, within the last few days, come forward, which is proving'highly satisfactory to the•local agent.

lrlam Labourer"Sent to Gaol—At the Lymm Police Station on-Wednesday, before "Messrs. S. W. 'Gould and S. 'Davies, Albert Ernest Cox. 'labourer, Tramway-road, Irlam, was sentenced to 28 days' imprisonment for an indecent offence against a girl of nine years old.

'Local 'Tribunal.—The first meeting of the 'Tribunal in connection with appeals for exemp- tion under Lord 'Derby's scheme was held on Friday night of last week, in , the Council 'Chambers. Altogether nine cases were before the 'Tribunal, and four were successful in respect to postponement. No total exemptions were granted.

Cheshire Volunteer Regiment, Army Reserve Class B.—The members of the Lymm Platoon, Cheshire Volunteer Regiment, cordially invite all men of the Army Reserve, Class B, to at- tend.drills, which take place in the Drill Hall every Tuesday and Thursday night at 8.30. If a sufficient number of men respond to the in- vitation a special section will be formed •to enable them to drill together until they become efficient.

'Corporal B. Elliott.—Mrs. Benjamin Elliott, Canterbury Cottages, ,has received news this week that her son, Corporal B. Elliott, at pre- sent an inmate of a Bristol hospital, has undergone an operation. As already published, Corporal Elliott has since his wounds been suffering severe pains in the head. A few days ago it was decided that he should have all his teeth out, and it transpires that the drum of his left ear Is broken. However, he is now pro- gressing satisfactorily, so much so, that the doctor in charge has written Mrs. Elliott to say that within the next few weeks he expects her son to be well enough to pay a visit home.

'Y.M.C.A. Hut For Soldiers.—With reference to the movement on foot organised in the village for assisting in the noble work of, the Y.M.C.A. for providing huts for wounded soldiers, a meeting was held in the Council Chambers on Thursday evening, when a com- mittee of ladies and gentlemen was formed. It was decided that a thorough canvass of the dis- trict should be made to provide funds. The meeting. urfortunately, was not well attended by the public. However, a sale of work was fixed for February 10th at the Drill Hall, when it is hoped a goodly number will aSsemble. The treasurer. Mr. T. Dixon, announced that a good start had already been made with respect to sub- scriptions.

Scalding Fatality.—An inquest was held at the Police Station on Wednesday, before Mr. H. C. Yates, who was acting temporarily during the illness of Deputy Coroner A. 0. White, touch- ing upon the death of John Sturton, aged one year, of Cross Brow, Statham, who sustained fatal injuries through the upsetting of a pot of tea on Friday evening of last week. The evidence went to show that the mother of the child had just finished brewing the tea, placing the pot on the table whilst she went into the back kitchen. During her absence the boy pulled the teacloth, with the result that the teapot and its contents fell over the head and neck of the child, badly scalding it. Dr. Thorp was immediately called in, prior to which a neigh- bour, Mrs. Appleton, applied a lotion and dress- ing. The little one, however, gradually grew worse and death ensued the following day. The verdict was "Death from shock, accelerated by accidental scalding."

ILYMM

PRIVATE LESTER STURTON.

In Military Hospital Suffering From

Septic Poisoning.

Mr. S. Moston, of the Anchor Inn, yesterday received the news that Private Lester Sturton, of Higher-lane, Lymm, is at present an inmate of the Military Hospital, Wye, Kent. He writes as follows:

"Dear Pal,—Just a. line or two to let you know in what district I am at present. • Tin.. fortunately, I have got septic poisoning in my kidneys. The doctor says I have been drinking out of something dirty. So this little lot will teach me to be careful in the future. I can tell yotrvl have gone through some awful pain; in fact, I was bedfast for five days. They are treating us very decently here, but my diet is not a varied one. I am not allowed to have anything but custard and cream, with one very thin piece of bread. "However, I am progressing very nicely just now, and when I get up I intend to apply for sick leave, and, if successful, will visit the old place." Private Sturton is attached to the 2nd Loyal North Lancashire Regiment. He is also a relative of John Sturton, the child who was fatally scalded, as announced in to-day's issue.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0137.txt | included=true | included =====
SEAMY TRADING.

To the Editor of "The Examiner."

Sir,—An .attack upon Sunday trading was made by a Lymm resident, in a local paper a week or so ago.; but after careful consideration I find that the advantages greatly overweigh the disadvantages. One great advantage is that by being able to obtain non-alcoholic refreshment at a reason- able .cost, the youths do not feel the tempta- tion of the public-houses. A few years ago the experiment of closing the shops was made, with the result that the youths of Lymm had no other place .to obtain their refreshments other than the public-houses, with the result that in- stead of suffering the inconvenience of passing a 'few youths in the village on a Sunday night we had 'to put up with rowdy scenes after clos- ing time, when every one was about to retire. So I think I can safely say that Sunday trading has enabled the youths of Lymm to follow the "King's examPle" by drinking non-alcoholic drinks.

Secondly, by•confidential chats to their soldier friends, many youths have resolved to follow their friends' example and join the Colours; thus Sunday trailing has helped to do a little to the -benefit of our country. If all of us would only try to find out what we can do towards bringing this war to an end, I think we shall find enough to do without bothering our heads over such small things as Sunday trading. Yours truly. A WELL-WISHER. -

===== 0002601_19160129_art0138.txt | included=true | included =====
B ITTIST 'PASTOR LECTURES ON

MRS. BROYVNING.

On Wednesday evening the Pastor (the Rev. Edward E.,Lovell), at the Baptist Church, Higher4ane, gave a lecture on "Elizabeth Barrett Browning" 'to an appreciative audi- ence.

Mr. Lovell's lectures are always interesting and instructive, and the subject on Wednesday was a very popular one, the lecture being much enjoyed. In dealing with Mrs. Browning's fife and works, the Lecturer caught and infused into his hearers the true spirit and understand- ing of the subject in hand, emphasising the power for good her life had been, and the. charm and sincerity of the poems written often in great physical pain and weariness. The famous writer was more or less an invalid, but the power and influence of her life and works will live in the hearts of her readers so long as they themselves live.

Miss Mary Brundrett was, the soloist, the songs chosen for the occasion being "The Better Land," "My Task." and "Child, What Sings the Morning?" Miss Helen Brundrett accompanying.

PATRIOTIC FAMILY.

Father And Two Sons Serving With

The Colours.

There is more than the usual romance of war in the fact that the husband and two sons. of Mrs. James Taylor, of West Hyde, Newfields, are serving with the Colours.

Private James Taylor, sen., our representative learned, has altogether seen upwards of thirty years' service on behalf of the „Empire. There are very few lands he has not 'fravelled in, but most of his life has been spent in China, India, South Africa, Malta and Spain. Fifty-two years of age, he is at present on home service with the 10th South Lancashire Regiment, being stationed in Salop.

- - - Although an old Army pensioner,, immediately on the commencement of the present hostilities he, with pride, offered his services and was duly accepted, thereby setting a noble example to the younger members of his family. His genial wife has been married twice, and at the present moment the whole of the family are in some way or other helping their King and country to annihilate German militarism. In civilian life Private James Taylor, sen., was employed as a fustian cutter at the works of Mr. Henry Moston, of Newfields.

In The Boer War.

Private James Taylor, sen., for some consid- erable time prior to the Boer War, was on garrison duty, stationed at Gibraltar, but when war was declared was drafted to South Africa, where he went through several stirring engage- ments. When peace was declared he stayed for a further period, and helped to gather up the dead for burial in the new cemetery under the auspices of the Women's Guild. His regi- ment at that time was the 2nd South Lanca- shires. He has has many mementoes of his travels abroad, as his cosy home can testify. One of his sons, Private James Taylor, was the first local " Terrier " to be wounded in the, present war, his injuries consisting of bullet wounds in the muscles of left arm, sustained on March 26th last. Unfortunately, the wounds turned out more serious than was first ex- pected, for a little while later he lost the use of his arm and was subsequently discharged from further service. It is singular to record that his step-brother, Private Henry Brookes, was the first to render aid to him when he was hit. Percy Taylor, the youngest member of this patriotic family, when Lord Derby made the call to arms immediately attested under the group system, but his employers—the Man- chester Ship Canal Company—deemed him in- dispensable, with the result that he has been exempted for the present. He is the most down-in-the-mouth member of this worthy family.

The St. George's Medal.

, The remaining brother, Private Henry 'Brookes, whose achievements we have already announced, is chiefly noted for his success in winning the coveted St.. George's Medal for heroic bravery in ambulance work. He is attached to the local Territorials as a stmtcher- bearer. -Private Brookes—who lives at Heald Green, Cheadle, with his wife—is. 25 years of age, and was born and brought up in Lymm. After leaving school he commenced work as a fustian-cutter, and, later, went to the late Mr. William Long's tanyard, Grappenhall. Here he remained for some years, next going as a gardener to Lymm Hall, and later as ' bee& gardener for Mr. Champ, with whom he after- wards proceeded to Cheadle. Whilst in Lymm he ;had joined the Territorial movement, and had, served one year when the war broke out. After seven months' , training he left for the Front; and we have since had the pleasure of printing many of his interesting letters home.

STOCKTON HEATH.

Adult School Whist Drive.—The whist drive at the Adult School on Saturday evening was a success. Fifteen tables were utilised, and the prizes were won by the following :—Ladies: 1, Miss Shepley; 2, Mrs. Lynch; gentlemen: 1, Miss Dutton (who played as .a gent); 2, Mr. Booth. Policemen Attest.—The Stockton Heath police- men have taken the opportunity granted them by the reopening of the group system, to attest under Lord Derby's scheme. On Wednesday evening close on a dozen of them attended the local recruiting centre and, after passing the medical examination, they, were formally at- tested. Recruiting in Stockton Heath is,still going on, but very few recruits are coming in.

Wesleyans' Tea and Concert.—The annual tea and coacert were held on Tuesday .evening, when over 70 members of the congregation sat down to the tastefully, arranged lea tables. The Rev. Percy meson, pastor, in the course of a short address, appealed for vorunteers to assist him in the visitation of the village, with a view to advertising the services. A good musical programme was submitted. The .choir, under the leadership of Mr. F. E. Penketh (though somewhat depleted of its male members) sang a number of glees in splendid style. Madam Lily Fairhurst was heard to advantage in her well- chosen songs "When You Come Home" and "Until." Miss May Isherwood gave a capital rendering of Tosti's " Good-bye " and "The Nightingale," for which she was deservedly encored. Mr. J. W. Warburton, as baritone, sang finely "There is a Land." He was encored, and afterwards contributed " Lighter- man Tom." As elocutionist, Miss Collin de- lighted the audience by her well-rendered recitals, and was recalled. Mr. C. Whitley, as instrumentalist, contributed two selections on the banjo, and was. loudly encored on each occasion.. Miss Turner, Mrs. G. Gould and Mr. W. H. Rigby were efficient accompanists. The tea was served by the lady workers of the church, and was in every way a success. The ladies in charge of tea were: Mesdames Deakin, Egerton. Whitfield, Asher, Dutton, Ardern, Todd, Webb, Jones and Turner.

THE LATE MRS. RICHARDSON.

Interment At Warrington Cemetery.

By the death of Mrs. Wm. Richardson, the widow of the late Mr. Wm. Richardson, .J.P., the district has lost one of its most respected inhabitants.

Mrs. Richardson, who had not been well for some time, pawed away at her residence, Holly Bank, Walton .Inferior, at 4 a.m. on Tuesday. She was a keen worker on behalf of St. Thomas' Church, and will be greatly missed by all con- nected with the parish. She was 68 years of age, and had been a widow just over two years. The funeral took place on Thursday at War- rington Cemetery. Previous to the interment, however, a service was held in St. Thomas' Church, the Rev. J. Collins (vicar) officiating. The Vicar was accompanied by the Rev. H. V. Pigot and the Rev. E. W. Clarke. The last rites at the graveside were said by the Rev. J. Collins. The private mourners were Messrs. James and Wm. Richardson (sons), Messrs. J. C.Robinson and W. A. McMullen (sons-in-law), Councillor J. G. Craik (brother), Messrs. J. Spencer Craik, John Fairclough and Tom Craik (nephews). Among others present were Messrs. Edward Rose, J. Longland, A. M. Ker and Dr. Hutton.

Voral tributes were sent by "Jim and Vanda," "Bill" "Amy," "Edward and Bessie and family," "George," "John and Annie Craik," "Betty and Harold," "Cousin Mary," "Marjory and Jim:: "Harriet and family," "Fanny and Jessie," "Dot and Tony," "Jack and Gertrude," Mr. and Mrs. Andrew M. Ker, Mr. and Mrs. HOdgkinson, Mr. J. H. Clarke and family, Miss Shaw, Mr. and Mrs. R. Garnett and Miss Brundrett, Mrs. G. Braide, J. Keith and W. GRAPPENHALL.

Death of a Respected Resident.—We regret to announce the death of Mr. John Williams, of Knutsford-road, Grappenhall, who passed away on Monday morning after a long illness, and was buried on Thursday at Grappenhall. He had been a resident in the village for many years. He leaves a widow and six children, and was well respected in the village. He was employed at Robertson's Steel Works, Latchford.

ON MILITARY SERVICE.

Nursing Association 'Without A Nurse.

The; Grappenhall and District Nursing Association are in a peculiar position, for throughout the past six months it has been without a working nurse. Miss S. Violet Callender is the Association's nurse, but six months ago she offered her ser- vices for war work and received an appoint- ment at the Winwick Hospital. The annual meeting of Grappenhall and Dis- trict Nursing Association was held on Wednes- day evening at Birchdale (by kind permission ! of Miss Appleton), Mr. F. A. Frost being in the ! chair. Other ladies and gentlemen present were Miss Appleton (secrefary), Mr. Roger C. Parr (treasurer); Mrs. Frost, Mrs. George Ainsworth, Mrs. Dakin and Mr.. Lister Kaye. Mr. Parr read the nurse's report, which stated that up to July 7th last year, the number of cases had been 38. The number of visits she had paid 'AI as 689. The 38 cases included- 21 medical and 17 surgical. Twenty-one patients were cured, four were convalescent, six had died, and seven remained about the same. Mr. Parr also reported that the Association started the year with £33 7s. 2d. in hand. The money they had collectedomade this sum into £B4 2s. Expenditure had been £63 2s. 3d., leaving a balance of £2O 19s. 9d.

The Officials.

The election of officers resulted as follows : President, Mr. F. A. Frost; hon. secretary, Miss Appleton; and hon. treasurer, Mr. Parr. The following committees were Also elected: General Committee: Miss Appleton, Mrs. Ainsworth, Miss Barrow, Mrs. Elgood, Mrs. Frost, Mrs. Field, Mrs. Hutchings, Mrs. Long, Mrs. Densham, Mrs. Parr, Mrs. Geo. Dakin, Miss Fairclough, Mrs. Kerfoot, Mr. Earwaker, Mr. Frost, Mr. Lister-Kaye, Mrs. Shipman, Miss B. Appleton.

PADGATE.

PROHIBITION.

Padgate Wesley Guild Debates The - Question Thoroughly. ' A very interesting debate on "Is Prohibition Practicable?" was, held in the schoolroom on Monday evening, presided over by Mr. Llewellyn (of Warrington). The case for the affirmative was championed by Mr. W. H. Sherburn, and Mr. Pierce Wil- liams was his opponent. Mr. Sherburn stated that this was the most serious problem which faced any country where drink was in use. Our nation was in a serious position if prohibition were not practicable% He contended, however, that it was, and gaveiPthree ways by which, in his opinion, it could be done: (1) By autocratic command: and in sup- port of this he cited Russia and the vodka evil, and also the American States where prohibition was in force; (2) By constitutional means; and (3) By voluntary personal abstention, such as the example set by the King. In the United States Navy and Army drink has been abolished since 1901, and as a result the number of offences against martial law has been reduced enormously. Restrictions and regulations will neither restrict nor regulate.. If we are helpless, then our doom as a nation is sealed.

Interference With Liberty.

Mr. Pierce Williams said the prohibition of a thing was often determined by its desirability. The aim or object should not be confused by the means of obtaining it. It was of primary importance that prohibition dealt with the traffic in liquois and not with the individual act of drinking. Prohibition would involve inter- ference with the liberty of the individual. If passed by Parliament it would lack public sup- Pert to make it practicable, and if public opinion did not support an Act it became a dead letter. It was to the liberty of the individual that we owed the virility of the English race. (Applause.) Mass action with proper organisa- tion would be effective, but when there was an absence of public sympathy and support, was impracticable. He advocated temperance re- form in education. Take care of education and prohibition would take care of itself, he said. Nfr. J. Seed and Mr. W. E. Plihstona also took part in the debate. along with the Chairman, but owing to the large number of abstentions from voting, neither side could claim any ap- preciable victory, although the Chairman de- clared for, tile affirmative.

‘. Votes ot=thanks to the speakers were proposed by Mr. Pearce and Mr. J. Seed. Owing to the popularity of the subject the

attendance was one of the largest for some time, about 70 being present.
