PENKETH.

South-West Lancashire Volunteer Regiment.— The Penketh section of the above regiment has made a good start. Twenty-five nam'es are now on the roll book, eighteen of whih are ex- pected to turn out to-day (Saturday):. to do their share in defending their township from an invading force. The muster on Tuesday night was exceptionally good, and hopes are entertained that the members' enthusiasm will remain at concert pitch. Sankey are also doing well, although not quite 60 strong as Penketh, a weakness which will be rectified most pro- bably before long, for they intend• to show what men of mettle they are.

FINE RECORD.

Dr. Murray Misses Only Seven Out Of 138 Meetings.

Owing to having been appointed medical' officer of health to the -Warrington Rural Dis- trict Council, Dr. Murray sent in his resigna- tion to the Penketh Parish Council on Friday evening.

Mr. J. G. Withinshaw, who presided, said Dr Murray had been connected with the Council from its formation, and for the past 16 years had been chairman. Out of 138 meetings he had only missed attending seven. (Hear, hear.) It was decided to send a letter to the doctor expressing regret at his resignation. Extortionate Charges.

The Chairman said the gas authoiities had sent their half-yearly account for lamps, and had only deducted £6 6s. 6d. There had -only been eight lamps lit out of 23, and the Corpora- tion were only prepared to allow Bs. sd. per lamp instead of 425. 6d. He thought the charges were extortionate.

.Mr. Woodward observed that the rent of the lamp standards would have to be paid whether they were used Or not. The Chairman moved a resolution that the clerk write to the District Council asking for a full explanation, at the same time protesting against the charges made. This was agreed to. Automatic Lighting.

The Clerk to the Warrington Rural District Council sent a letter stating that the gas engineer had written to them stating that he was willing to arrange a new quotation for the supply of gas in the different townships. ===== 0002601_19160129_art0153.txt | included=true | included =====
FARMER'S SUICIDE.

Fretted Over His Son Being In

Group System.

CORONER AND RECRUITING.

Fear of a son being called up nnder the group system is said to have caused the suicide of a prominent Cheshire farmer, named Samuel Massey Houghton (53), of Swinyard Hall Farm, High Legh.

The facts were told at the inquest held on Tuesday afternoon.

The deceased was found hanging in the garage on Sunday.

Prior to going to High Legh Mr. Houghton resided at Grappenhall, where he was well respected.

The widow said that on Monday of last week a gentleman called on military business in regard to a son Sydney joining the Army. He had attested, and the individual told him that he would have to appeal to the Tribunal if he wanted to be relegated to a later group. This greatly upset Mr. Houghton, who got it into his head that if he appealed to the Tribunal his. son would have no chance, considering the gentlemen who composed the committee. The Coroner (Mr. H. C. Yates) said he was sorry to hear that. Continuing witness said that her husband fretted very much,,., but went about his work as usual. On Monday night they went to bed at nine o'clock, and her husband made the remark, "You will not have me long; this recruiting is killing me." At 10.30 he jumped out of bed, and said he was going downstairs, adding "I am going rout of my mind." She followed him, and he made fires up in two rooms, and walked about the house the whole night. Next night he acted in the same way, and only, slept ,an hour or two on Wednesday night, while on Thursday he never ate anything. "Nearly Finished Him."

The Coroner: Why didn't you get a doctor? Witness: I wish Ihad. I did not think it was serious. That I didn't fetch a doctor is my one regret.

. The Coroner: Still you did your best. Continuing, witness said Mr. Moss, a neigh- bour, came on Thursday and said that his son had been called up to join the Army on Febru- ary 3rd, without' any appeal, and witness added that when her husband heard that it pearly fin- ished him. On Friday morning he went out and did not return until five o'clock, when she found him in the garage, sitting on the shafts of a trap. He said he was going to sleep. At 7.30 she brought him into the house, and they went to bed about nine o'clock. He stayed in bed the whole of the night, and on Saturday he got up to milk the cows as usual. On Sunday, witness got up at five o'clock, but her husband remained in bed—a thing she had not known of him for years. sue never saw him again. In answer to the Coroner, she said it was the recruiting business only which unbalanced his. mind. He had no other trouble, financial or otherwise, and was the best of husbands.

The Coroner': Has he indulged in drink? - Witness: OceaSionally, but he never took any drink from last September until Friday of last week.

One of the jury said he did not think it was correct to say that he indulged in drink. He had never lost any work since he came to High Legh. Sydney Houghton gave similar evidence to his mother about recruiting, adding that on Mon- day he received notice from the Tribunal to the effect that he had been put back ten groups. He mentioned that it was he who discov,ered his father hanging from a beam in the garage. He tried artificial respiration, which was of no avail.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0154.txt | included=true | included =====
Unfortunate View of Duty.

The Coroner remarked it was a pity that Mr. Houghton had taken such a view of life, and of his duty to his country. If everybody took the same view, there would be great difficulty in getting recruits. Sydney had attested, and in all probability he would have had to serve, but, as they heard, he had been put back ten groups. He (the Coroner) was particularly sorry to hear of the names of the gentlemen mentioned. lie knew one, and he was high minded Mid con- scientious, and had the appeal come before him, there was no doubt that justice would have been done.

The jury returned a verdict of "Suicide whilst temporarily insane." Funeral At Grappenhall.

The funeral of the deceased gentleman took place on Wednesday afternoon at the GraPpen- hall Church. There was manifest sorrow on the part of the large gathexing, many of whom had been closely associated with the deceased. Many farmers from the surrounding districts were present to pay tribute to his memory. The late Mr. Houghton, although not occupying any official position at the Agricultural Shows in the district, took more than ordinary intOrest in fat stock shows and sales, &c., and was highly esteemed and respected wherever he went. The hearse was a mass of floral tributes, for- warded by many admirers and relatives.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0155.txt | included=true | included =====
FALL FROM A SWING.

Girl's Death Occurs Six Months Alter:

The death of Alice Taylor (8), the daug"ater of Private Frederick Taylor, of the 4th South Lan- cashire Regiment, and Mrs. Taylor, of 7, Neston- street, Warrington, was the subject of an inquiry held by Mr. S. Brighouse on Tuesday. Mrs. Taylor stated that in July last Alice fell from a swing and cut her head, and since then had frequently complained of severe head- aches. On Saturday, the 22nd inst., she ap- peared to be in her usual state of health, but complained of pains in her legs. She had been running about during the day, and witness thought she was only.. tired. She 'commenced to vomit during the night, and told the witness "there was an, engine in her head and she could not see." Dr. McKee was sent for, but the child died at four o'clock on Monday morning. Dr. McKee said the death was due to menin- gitis. _ .

In answer to the Coroner, the doctor that meningitis could arise in .a natural way without an injury laving been received. The jury returned a verdict of "Death due t•I natural causes."

A Woman's Letters.

Women, it is generally admitted, write bettE.r letters than men. According to a well-known author there is a reason for this superiority, lie says: "The obvious meaning is never the one we should read into a woman's letter. Th3re is always a-veiled meaning. Woman makes pee of a letter just as she employs a glance or a smile, in a way that is carefully thought o7r, and with an eye to effect. And, after all, do"s a woman's hat serve to cover her head ° Does woman's pardsol keep off the sun? Why, then, should a woman's letter serve to convey her real thoughts to the person addressed, just like the letters of some honest grocer, who writes: I send you five pounds of coffee,' because fie really docJi zend yo*z five pour.ds of cofree?"

RUNCORN RURAL DISTRICT COUNCIL.

Suggestion That Cottage Resi- dents May Keep Pigs.

Probably with the intention of increasing our food supply, Mr. Kaye suggested at Tuesday's meeting of the Runcorn Rural District Council that the old system, whereby people resident in cottages were able to keep •pigs, • should be reverted to. .

It was decided that the matter should be discussed at the next meeting. The Clerk of the County Council forwarded a copy of the letter that Lord Derby had circu- lated to the Lord Mayors and Mayors of the country on the recruiting question. The Clerk of the County Council suggested that the Rural Council should assist in the matter.

Mr. Linaker, jun., said there were three recruiting offices allocated in the Council's area —at Frodsham, Runcorn and Stockton Heath— and he did not think the letter would apply to the Council's area. Mr. T. Antwis presided.

RUNCORN GUARDIANS.

Cost Of Keeping Lunatics

Greater Than Hitherto.

Mr. C. E. Linaker, jun., presided at a meeting of the Runcorn Board of Guardians an Tuesday.

A letter was read from the. Clerk to the Upton Asylum stating the rate of maintenance for pauper lunatics for the ensuing quarter had been fixed at 10s. 91d. per head per week. This is an increase of 7d. per head per week.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0159.txt | included=true | included =====
Objection to " Pauper."

During a discussion on a letter from the Prescot Union. which referred to the charge- ability of a pauper, Mr. T. Antwis said that he understood that some two or three years ago objection was taken to the use of the word "pauper." INMATES' ENTERTAINMENT.

The House Committee recommended that the offer of the Ladies' Visiting Committee to pro- vide an entertainnient on February 11th. should be • accepted. The Board approved of this sug- gestion.

The Clerk (Mr. W. E. Hough) said the num- ber of inmates in the House for the third week was: seventy men, forty-five women and four children, the total being 119. The "casuals ' were: forty-nine males and six females. The figures for the corresponding period of last year were: eighty males, forty-four females and ten children. The number of the " casuals " was sixty-five.

The Children's Home Committee reported that they had passed a vote of thanks to Lady liarmood Banner for providing a Christmas tree.

CHESHIRE POLICE FREE TO ATTEST.

The Cheshire Police Committee on Saturday; unanimously agreed to allow eligible .members of the county constabulary to attest. It was stated that 100 members of the force had en- listed, and eight were serving as drill instructors. There were 272 remaining of military age, of whom 208 were married. The force is pow 130 below strength.

FRIENDLY SOCIETIES.

FORESTERS' BURIAL SOCIETY.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0164.txt | included=true | included =====
Strong Financial And Numerical

Position.

Despite the war, the Warrington District Burial Society of the Ancient Order of. .Foresters have just completed a most successful year.

The forty-third annual meeting was held on Tuesday evening at the Roebuck 'Hotel, Bridge- street, Warrington, a good muster of members being present, and Mr. A. Tilling presided, in the enforced absence of the President (his Worship the Mayor). The secretary's report and balance sheet showed a successful year, both financially and numerically, . although the amount .paid .in death was the greatest in the history of the Society, partly due to the un— fortunate number of deaths of members whilst on active service. The increase in - premiums was the greatest on record. The valuation of the Society for the five years ended December 31st, 1914, proved the. SOciety to be worth SI 3s. 6a.... in the -£ , which is a remarkable state of solvency. The Mayor '(Alderman Peter Peacock) was elected president for 1916, Mr. G. Rotherham vice-president. Mr. Harold Perdue Junior auditor, Mr. G. Maybury examiner. The pri.!-,s to collectors were won by Mr. E. Fowler, Mr. F. Clegg .and -Mr. J. W. Perdue. The in•eting closed with votes of thanks to the officers an 1 the visiting members. .

BOON 10 THE DISTRICT.

ELM% 00d-AA ciitte Congregational Church Makes Progress.

It was stated at the annual meeting and social held in connection with 'Elmwood Avenue Con.; gregational Church that, it was a boon to the district.

The reports of the year's work *ere -Presented by the various secretaries, and proved to be _of an eminently Satisfactory and encouraging nature, showing excellent pro- gress in most departments. - Mr. Fred Morrell (church secretary) said that the church and school were proving a boon to the district, and the services had been Well at- tended throughout the year. The church inein bership had been added to by 20 members, mak- ing a total of 80. The average attendance at the evening services was more than 250, and the morning attendance was improving. During the year a " Brotherhood" had been inaugur- ated, with a membership of 83, and the meet- ings op Monday evenings had been socially and spiritually helpful. Christmas gifts were sent to all our soldiers by the church and school.

UNITARIANS %11l BELGIANS.

Former Pastor's Appeal At Cairo Sired.

A former pastor in the person of the Rev. W. IL Drummond visited Cairo-street Unitarian Chapel on Sunday evening, and in the course of his sermon appealed for help for the Belgian Hospital Fund, and the collection realised £lO. The reverend gentleman mentioned that at the beginning of the war Unitarian Churches. throughout the country thought it would be well to take up some particular work of their own that would help to enrich the magnificent traditions of public philanthropy which they had inherited from their forefathers. After the fall of Antwerp there was a great influx of wounded Belgian soldiers to Calais, and it was not possible to rescue any of the Belgian Red Cross Stores from the hands of the enemy. When the Unitarians heard of this they felt it was an opportunity for the special service for which they had been waiting. They made an appeal, and it met , with an immediate and generous response.. It fired the imagination of their people, who liked the taking up of a special branch of service of their own. They had now passed the sum of ..tlO,OOO in money, and were receiving parcels from all parts of the country, clothing and cases of hospital equipment, to the extent of 40 or 50 per week. Hands of help had been reached out to make the wears- period of convalescence so far from relatives and friends a little more cheerful—games; books, and cards, and other things to ease the men's burdens. They had even established a small hospice for the civilian refugees, and three small institu- tions for giving help and shelter to Belgian chil- dren who had lost their parents in the war: it was philanthropy which could not help but appeal to them all.

WORKINGMEN'S MISSION.

Annual Congregational Tea

And Concert.

The annual congregational tea and conceit of the Bank Quay Working Men's Mission were held on Monday evening and 'proved to be a

great success. A hundred and ninety . people sat down to tea, after which the room was cleared for the concert. Mr.. Thomas Dale was in 'the chair, and was supported , by the Vice-President (Councillor George Dale). • The Chairman mentioned that Mr. A. Tilling I the secretary) had received a letter from Mrs: John Crosfield„ in which she expressed her regret at not being able to be present, and sent her kind regards to the members of the Mis- sion.

Mr. A. Tilling's annual report was as fol- lows (in part) : As a very humble branch of 'God's great' Church we have . endeavoured to teach the indispensableness of God, both for the indivi- dual and the nation. We have humbly, yet earnestly, Phdeavoured to teach the possibility of man possessin,, God, of claiming Him as their own, individually and collectively—of allowing God to live in them and through' them—of attaining to that high altitude of spiritual experience attained to by St. Paul when he exclaimed 'For, me to live is Christ.' As to how far success has attended the efforts put forward in the preaching and teaching Of Christ's Gospel we:. cannot say. 'Paul may plant, and Apollos water, but God alone can give the increase.' If, as we believe, the seed of God's eternal truth has been .sown during the past year, we can confidently leave the matter to God, believing that no toil for Him can be in Vain.' Whis we can unhesitatingly

;ay : that grief-striae-n- and anxious souls have, inder the ministrations of God's servants from put- pulpit, learned to rest in the I/Ord,' and Lave received grace to enable them to wait gaiently for Him.'

===== 0002601_19160129_art0173.txt | included=true | included =====
Sunday School And League Of Help

"You, heard, a few .-weeks .ago, from the Sunday School Secretary's report, that the various branches of the Sunday -school were doihg good work, and tbe-.,.4anie may be reported of those branches of the Mission which may

be said to be more closely allied with the chapel than with the school, although the •par- tition between the school and chapel is so very thin that one has a difficulty sometimes in deciding to which section they belong. You see the various sections dovetail into each other, and make the chapel and school in a very real sense one—' members one of another.'

Peel and cut eight medium-sized potatoes into quarters ,or large cubes; wash and place them with .one quart of water and one tablespoonful salt over the fire and cook until done;. then drain off all the water, return the potatoes to the fire for a few minutes to dry. In the mean- time melt one tablespoonful ..of butter and a. tablespoonful of flour, stir and cook two minutes, add one pint of milk, and season with ,half a teaspoonful of salt and a pinch of pepper, stir, and cook to a smooth sauce; put the potatoes into a dish, •pour over the sauce, and sprinkle over one tablespoonful of very finely- chopped parsley. servo with broiled lamb or veal chops or beefsteak.

Casserole Cookery. .All casserole cookery is economical. First, be- cause one May add any number of ingredients to the mainstay of the dish; secondly, because it is cooked so closely covered that every atom of nourishment and taste is kept in the food. Put bluntly, where you take two helpings of roast chicken, you only want one of chicken done in the casserole. Also, every dish that can be done in a closed vessel with long cookery can be cooked in the haybox, which brings down the price .of necessary fuel to that of the heat re- quired to bring it to boiling point. The French are great at casserole cookery. It is very easy indeed. You put a fowl, or some meat, pre- viously browned in butter, into the casserole, with what remains of the butter, some more, many small onions and mushrooms and carrots, turnips, peas—in fact, what you choose; and a half-pint or more of water. Proper Age to Marry. " What is the proper age at which to Marry?" This question has become so chronic in the women's departments of the American "yellow" newspapers that a Statistician has gone to con- siderable trouble to collect data that will enable the questioners to settle the subject for them- selves. These precedents constitute the result of his labours:—

Adam. and Eve, 0; Shakespeare, 18; Ben Jon- son, 21; Franklin, .21,; Mozart, 25; Dante Kepler, Fuller, Johnson, Burke, Scott, 26‘; Tycho Brabe. Byron, Washington, Bonaparte, 27; 'Penn and Sterne, 28; Linnaeus and Nelson, 29 ; Burns, 30; Chaucer, llogarth and Peel, 32; Wordsworth and Davy, 33; Aristotle, 36; Sir William Jones and Wellington, 37; Wilberforce; 38; Luther, 42; Addison, 44; Wesley and Young, 47; Swift, 49; Buffon, 55;- Old Parr,- last time, 120. If Adam and Eve married before they were a year old, and the veteran Parr buckled with a widow at 120, bachelors and spinsters may wed at any age they like, and find shelter under ' great namea for either early. or late marriages.

As A frequent visitor to other countries, and deeply interested, in social work and problems, one thing amazes me constantly. It is the lamentations of our - lawmakers ov:er the mortality of the infant population, renewed with such grave cause recently, owing to the last statistics. Is it possible that these gentlemen fail to realise the main cause .of this mortality among the children of the working classes, and that the remedy 'lies entirely in their own hands? England stands almost alone among civilised • nations without a pure food law, and in Consequence the food of the children is not only lacking in the nutriment necessary to build up their little bodies, but in the case of the , bread, milk, sugar, their principal diet, is adulterated with positively deleterious sub- stances. This has been so repeatedly proved and exposed that it is beyond dispute. The teeth of the children loudly proclaim the fact. Feed the ' children with pure wheat and rye bread, pure milk and sugar at least, and we shall no longer have cause to lament their early death, nor will those who do not survive possess, as they too often do, stunted and diseased bodies. Would not the organising of such . a work be pre- eminently suitable as Women's National Service, backed by a pure food law ?—Constance Eliza- beth Maul, in " The Times."

OM'N 3S WORLD. • • ea

Perfection in Ready-Mades.

That capes will "come „in " with the warmer weather is More than probable, since they have always had a vogue when full skirts were fashionable. And the women who buy liew clothes for the spring, although they may now be wearing narrow dresses, will naturally order the new styles, consequently. fhll skirts will be more generally, worn than at present. The capes are to have hoods lined in some contrasting colour. • Short women, however, should avoid very full, or very short skirts. Styles were never so various, therefore every type of woman can find something to suit her individual re- quirements. Fortunately there is every likeli- hood that the present diversity of fashions will continue for some time to come.. rerhaps some women are inclined—at least those who are the busiest—to take just what fashion-mongers pro- vide, without any particular reference to their personal needs. For these the ready-to-wear suits and dresses are a veritable boon. They are well cut and well made, besides being up-to- (late.. The working- woman should avail herself of the opportunities the sales offer to purchase first-class goods at reasonable prices.

The Vogue of the Blouse-Coat. Women now ; demand that comfort . and economy shall receive consideration, and this probably accounts for the recent appearance of several sensible and economical accessories, among which the 'Meuse or house coat takes a foremost place. Cemparatively an innovation, since it was unknown a few months ago, it has already firmly established itself, and bids fair to rival the blouse, to which, with the sports coat, it owes.its origin. Every week .it appears in some new and attractive form, and as it can be adapted for all , hours .of the day, and is moreover most useful in. rejuvenating a last year's gown, its popularity_ is not a. matter for surprise. In crepe de chine for morning wear, in velvet edged with fur for the afternoon, or in some transparent fabric for the evening, it is equally successful. Embossed velvet makes very handsome coats, and Short lengths bought cheaply in the sales should prove most accept- able for smart wear. A charming model in silver-grey Georgette crepe was relieved with motifs of orange embroidery. A pleated basque to another model fell well over the hips, the

fullness being kept in place by a narrow strip ot. fur, about four inches from the hem.

The Triumphant Knitted Coat. Some little time ago the tailored sports coat. appeared, and there was a probability that it would replace the knitted and Woven garments. Although quite attractive, and made in brightly coloured materials, the tailor-made model ntw occupies an ,inferior positions as compared with the popular and comfortable woven or knitted styles. Probably the threatened rivalry incited the makers of the latter to put forth fresh efforts to maintain their position, with the result that this season's knitted silk and wool' coats are the most varied and beautiful ever seen. The mak- ing of artificial silk has been brought to such a pitch of Perfection that it is difficult to distin- guish it from the real. A beautiful model in a Levet mixture was of silk, and with it went a tweed skirt, neat and trim, and straight in line, although box-pleated. The fullness, however, was so cleverly managed that the skirt was not too heavy, yet it possessed the fashionable flare with the freedom of movement demanded by the sportswoman. The range of colours include all the season's shades of beige and grey and Champagne.

Monograms and Samplers. The monogram is very much in, evidence again. It is seen en umbrellas, bags, bracelets, brooches, hatpins,. handkerchiefs and purses. Another revivatis the sampler, which now takes the forth .of a war Souvenir. The autographs of soldier relatives and friends are inscribed on a piece of fine canvas With a sharp pencil, and •then embroidered generally, in outline stitch. The centre of the sampler, which varies in size according to the number of names inscribed, is filled. in with the various regimental badges of the signatories. The form and colouring of the badges must be correct; "satin" stitch is the best for this purpose. The idea is good, as the names of members of a family who took part in the world war will thus be banded down to future -generations. Sewing in a number of forms has come into fashion again, seeing that occupation has to be found for the evenings that the darkened streets compel so many women to spend at home. Knitting is as industriously 'pursued as it was last winter, and perhaps with more dexterity, for it had become almost a lost 'art.

CHAT and GOSSIP.

By MARJORIE.

An Anti-Waste Campaign,

Every housewife who can compass the visit, should make a point of seeing an interesting exhibition held by the members of the Institute of Hygiene, who have started an Anti-Waste Campaign.. All sorts of contrivances warranted to cut down household work and expenses are shown, as WelLus a wonderfully fitted kitchen. Demonstrations in economising. fuel are given

_ daily. Haybox cooking is especially dealt with, and wonders are performed by: the boxes ex- hibited.. Lectures on the values of foods, and what to choose in wartime are given. Two ex- pert cooks one a French, the other a Scots- woman, have been engaged to give demonstra- tions of the cheap and nourishing, yet easily prepared meals, for which their respective nations \ are celebrated, and a number of chil- dren prove their ability to make \simple pud- dings, after one or two cookery lessons. As an attempt to show that domestic economy can be rendered interesting as well. as profitable, the exhibition is in every way a success. Women Inventors.

According to one of the leading patent firms, women inventors are on the increase, and their inventions are not confined to what is termed women's sphere. They include improvements in hospital and medical devices, and sports and war are represented. A knitted glove for soldiers, which can be worn also as a mitten, is the in- vention of a woman; it has a slit near the thumb that enables the fingers to be ..released, thus forming a mitten. A collapsible bedstead that can be carried in a knapsack, adjustable crutches which can be used by tall or short men, an automatic lighting apparatus for ex- tinguishing street gas lamps, a contrivance for lowering- the boats, of a ship, and a signal ar- rangement for railway junctions, are some of the unusual inventions for which women were responsible during last year.

Potato Turnovers,

Mix about one pint of hot mashed potatoes with a beaten egg. SeasonMth salt and pepper, and roll it well in flour—do not use too much flour. Form into balls, and press .Or roll out rather thin. Put a teaspoonful of meat, minced and seasoned, on one half;' fold over, and press the edges together. Have plenty of smoking hot fat in a deep frying pan, and in it fry the turn- overs on both sides till a pale brown. Creamed Potatoes.

HOW A STRANGE FRIENDSHIP WAS

FORMED.

A group of boys were standing one day by a village pond. They were evidently torwenting something in the water and enjoying themselves very much. Only now and then one less- hardened than the others would exclaim, "Let the poor little wretch be.'' Fortunately for the poor little kitten which was struggling in the water there was more grass than stones at hand or it must have been killed. .

Suddenly a deep-toned bark sounded near at hand. At the first note the bullies dropped the pebbles or grass they held and listened, but when the second bark came nearer still there was a cry from all: "It's Bob" And all the boys took to their heels like the cowards they were. Bob, the squire's bulldog, came bound- ing to ,the scene of action: lie hated boys of any kind, but most of all he hated ragged, naughty boys, and he never saw a. knot of them together without considering it was his duty to disperse them. Ile caught sight of the young- sters at the pond as .he was starling for his morning walk, and he dashed up like a steam engine to see what they were after. The boys meanwhile had scrambled into the various trees and Watched the envies proceed- ings. Bob loOked round with a sneer on his already well-turned-up nose and was, perhaps, reflecting on the cowardice of bullies when he caught sight of something struggling to climb up the edge of the pond. " My!" exclaimed jack Hunter, the boy who had pleaded for mercy, " I wouldn't give much for the' little beggar's life If Bob gets hold of . it." ,

, But Jack was Wrong. Bob could be gentle as he wits strong. lie seized the poor exhausted kitten and trotted gravely home with it in his moutist. Bob, no, we don't want any drowned rats here," said the squire as he met his favourite dog. But 'Bob trotted on till he reached his own kennel; then he dropped the poor kitten on the nice, clean straw and began • licking it all over. Half an hour afterwards Squire Strange; lookiog in, found Bob lying fast asleep with the kitten nestled between his long pars. -

C. That was the beginning of 'the strange friend- ship between the wee kitten and the bin. bull- dog, Where .Bob went there pussy was bound to go the. Sometimes she would ride on his back, sometimes Bob would carry her in his mouth, and sometimes the kitten would leap about by his: side, but wherever one was there you would find the other.

LITTLE MAIDS OF ALL WORK.

Soon after breakfast one Saturday morning, the mother of Emily and Nancy Green was sent for in a hurry to go•to.u: poor old neighbour who

was very ill, and, us the children watched het out of'sight, Emily said to Nancy:— "Now we are going to be maids of all work "Air, yes!" agreed little Nancy. " What can I do, Emily?" "Well, there are the breakfast things to wash up, and that must be my work,'.' answered Emily, thoughtfully; " but you can help to carry them into the cupboard when .they are ready. There are the beds to make, too—the big one must be left for Mother to do, but you can help me to make our two 'small ones. The sweeping is all done; but there is the dusting to do, so you can dust the chairs and stools Nancy, and I.can dust the tables and the other tall things. Then the potatoes must he peeled!" "May I help you do that?" the little sistN' asked eagerly. "'No, dear, your fingers are not quite big , enough" Emily told her kindly; "but you can pick up sticks for the fire to boil them!" "Yes, I will!" cried Nancy, looking very pleased at the.thought. "What else can I do?"

"Well, I think we had better do all we have just talked about before we talk about an3ithing more to do," laughed Emily, "Come along, little woman, and help me to begin." So away they ran together, and soon after- wards there was Nancy outside •feeding birds with the scraps off the plates, and-Emily busy washing up in the kitchen. Emily stood in front of a big bowl of hot water in which was a clean little mop, her sleeves were rolled up 'past her elbows, and a nice dry cloth hung over her left shoulder; the things to be washed up were all on the left side of the bowl, and she first. washed the cups, saucers and spoons, then the plates, and lastly the knives—not forgetting that knife-handles should be kept out of the water, and that a big thing twist not be tilted upon a. little thing. - Emily was -doing her best to work iptickly in •the right way, as _Mother had taught her to do, so that nothing was put down roughly with a loud clatter; and when all the things were clean and dry and bright, they were safely carried into the cupboard with Nancy's help. , Then beds were made and (lusting done, after which little Nancy, with a warm shawl wrapped round her, ran into the orchard to pick up wood for the cooking of the potatoes which her Sister had begun to peel. , They were finished when Nancy got back, and she found Emily busy at the fireplace; and, as the little maid carried the wood to her there, she again asked eagerly : "What else can I do, Emily?"

- "Not tired yet, darling?" and Emily gave her a sweet smile as she took from her dear little arms • the long pieces of wood. "Well, when you have warmed your hands we will sit down together, and I will hem mother's new apron and you can thread needles ready for her to use this afternoon—some with white cotton and some with black."

So when their mother came home and found their little fingers busy with needles and cotton, and nothing dirty or dusty anywhere, she was very pleased with her little maids of all work;' because„ not only. had they • done as much as they were able, they had also done every bit of their. work as nicely as they .could.

Though the land at the rear of the church has been purchased, there is no immediate scheme of extension in view, though £76 has been set aside as a nucleus for this purpose. That the - Presbyterian Church has •.been so successful in recent years is chiefly due to the initiative and untiring energy of its minister, the Rev. William Reid. When he leaves the town he will be greatly missed, not only by his own people, but by the inhabitants of War- rington in general. • He has made many friends and his popularity covers a wide area. The aim of. the writers of this volume is to raise 801110 of the problems involved in the question of settlement, such as nationality and territorial rearrangement, the freedom of the seas, international economic relations, etc., "to set people thinking upon them, and so to assist in building up a publie opinion capable of appreciating and solving them." A Permanent Peace. Mr. G. Lowes Dickinson discusses the "Basis of Permanent Peace," and finds it in the development of the `,` international mind" which would establish international organisa- tion. Mr. C., R. Buxton treats of the prin- ciple of " Nationality" in its relation to both large and small States, and shows the im- mense difficulties which impede the strict ap- plication of the principle. The chapter deal- ing with the "Freedom of the Seas" by Mr. H. Sidebotham is a very searching criticism of British naval policy in relation to the rights of, neutral countries. Mr. Sidebotham con- cludes That "just. as the war on land has shown the excesses of German militarism to be not only crimes but blunders, so, too, the full history of the war will show that our attempts to wage war on German commerce have not materially influenced its progress, but have caused us as ,much loss as we have inflicted." Mr. Hobson contributes an examina- tion of the economic problems underlying in- ternational rivalries, and finds the only hope of a solution of the present differences in the policy of the Open Door. The chapter is one .of the best in the book, and will well repay the most serious consideration. Miss Maude Royden deals with war and the women's move- ment, and shows the ultimate irreconcila- bility between militarism and feminism. Mr. H. N. Brailsford discusses the question of the best method of settling disputes between nations with a view to the avoidance of war, and Mr. Philip Snowden treats of democracky and publicity in foreign affairs. In the con- cluding chapter of the book " Vernon Leo " writes very inspiringly of " The Democratic Principle and International Relations." A Temperate Discussion. One is struck, in reading this book, by the fact that the 'writers seem to treat the belligerent nations as being on the same foot- ing. The immediate cause of ,the war is dis- missed_as irrelevant, and the origin is sought

in. conditions, in the bringing about of which Germany would appear to have played a very secondary part. But surely the facts that have come to light during the war show be- yond doubt that Germany was determined to achieve her aims irrespective of their cost or reasonableness, and by deliberately planned methods which have received the moral repro- bation of the civilised world. In the con- sideration of terms of settlement, therefore, it seems to us that this must be borne in mind, and we believe that it will have great weight with the members of the Quadruple Entente in determining the demands which they will impose on Germany and her Allies on the conclusion of peace: While commending the book, therefore, as a calm and temperate discussion of the prin- ciples on which alone any security for a "last- ing settlement" may be based, we cannot sup- pose that it will be possible to apply all th, proposals submitted therein, when the war is over. no. lines on which a settlement is 'ar- rived at will probably be very different from those suggested by these Writers, but the book will have served a very considerable object if it sets people thinking seriously of these problems.

The Woman at Home, "Curing for the Mother and Child" is the titlo of an important article by Marion Labrosse - in the February issue of "The Woman et Home." This talented enthusiast deals exhaus- tively with one of the greatest problems aristUg out Of the war—one that must evelltuoll3! come under. the serious consideration of the State. From statistics it appears that over 800 mothers and 100,000 babies die each year in England and Wales whose lives could easily be saved. It is an article which every woman should read from beginning to end. Souter introduces us to the Hon. Lily , Montagtes Living Monument—London's largest Club for Girls—a hive of practical in- dustry and a restful home of entertaining and invigorating pastimes, providing for its mem- bers every opportunity to develop their natures physically, mentally and morally. . S. Macnaghtan details the experiences of a second day at a railway station .in Flanders, and brings us into very close contact with the hor- rors of war and its effect upon gallant Belgium. Fiction forms a_powerful feature in this mini- her, and is represented by J. E. Buekrose, Mrs. De Horne Vaizey, Annie S. swan, Walter. Prichard Eaton, and others.

HINTS ON HEALTH.

tseful Information lii Popular Language.

For Health.

Breathe - deeply; eat temperately; chew horoUghly; drink (water) copiously; clean teeth varefully; bathe frequently; eliminate freely; laugh heartily; sleep regularly; work planfully; exercise daily; serve willingly; speak kindly; play a little; read much; think more. The above admirable health prescription was published in the Chicago Department; of Health Bulletin of September 4th, 1915. To Combat Pneumonia.

The main reason why the pneumonia' season has arrived is because the cold weather drives so • Many indoors. That means inhaling more poisonous air, and that furnishes an excellent opPortunity for the pneumonia germs to come to the front. To avoid pneumonia, breathe pure air. Drink pure water, instead of poisonous drinks. Eat clean, wholesome food fresh from the lap of Nature instead of various second- hand foods as flesh, fish and - foil.—" Good Health."

REV. WILLIAM REID

Accepts A "Cal! " To A North London

Church.

While wishing him continued success, his many friends in 'Warrington and district will regret to hear that the W. William Reid, after a seven years' ministry at St. John's Presbyterian Church of England, Warrington, has accepted a "'call", to minister at the Trinity Church, Canonbury, in the Presbytery of London (North). _ _

Mr. Beid's record in Warrington has been one of continual success. , .

When he was appointed to the town by the Manchester ,Presbytery, in June, 1909. the pros- pects of local Presbyterianism were none too bright. The old church in Winwick-street. had been sold and the little band of communicants, numbering about thirty, worshipped at the 'Cairo-street Schools. Mr. Reid immediately started a building scheme, and the fine church on the Wilderspool-causeway was built in 1910, being formally opened by Mrs. Reid in May of

hat year. Later it .was dedicated by the

Very

- Rev. W. M. Macphale, who at the present time is 3loderator-Elect of the Synod. From a. membership of only thirty the Church began to grow in influence. Worship- pers were attracted to it, and at the present time the number of communicants on the •roll is 239. A -Sunday school was started, -along with various other organisations, .and all are in a flourishing .condition. About 120 children attend the school.

The next big movement on behalf of the Church occurred in September, 1914, when the freehold ef the site on which the church stands was purchased. At the end of the same year another forward step was taken, and the freehold of the land at the rear of the church was bought for extension purposes. The pur- chasing of the land cost about .2660, and the erection of the church about .22,000. The debt on the church is being paid off at the rate of £5O a year, and there is still .2275 outstanding, but it is a 'debt which carries no interest.

NEW BOOKS.

Towards a Lasting Settlement. Edited by Charles Roden Buxton. London: George Allen and Unwin, 2s. Gd. •net.

There are some who think that with a .war of the magnitude of the present conflict in progress, it is idle to consider terms and con- ditions of peace; that our whole time and energy should be devoted to the successful prosecution of the aim with which we entered the strife, and that we should leave- to the future the consideration of the principles that must guide us in the settlement of this unpre- cedented upheaval. In this view, while we have aome sympathy with those who support it, we find ourselves unable to concur. For those actually engaged directly or indirectly in the conduct of the war, it is, doubtless, im- possible to occupy themselves with such matters as the terms on which it wl be..de- sirable to conclude peace, but there ate many persons, unable from various causes to par- ticipate in war Work, who should be seriously considering in their own minds the problems involved in the question of peace. It is essential that a nation on whom a demand is made for unlimited sacrifice, as is the case with us in this war, should have a fairly definite idea of the object it has in view and of the terms on which it is prepared to make pee. Definiteness in these matters will not result from a refusal to think them out. It can only proceed from a wide and informed discussion of the many and varied questions involved in the settlement of the present dis- pute. \ And on the wisdom of the conditions of settlement of the present war depends our security against a recurrence of this ghastly horror. If the conditions contain the possi- bility of extension and development there may be hope of an ultimate and permanent settle- ment. But this will largely depend on the extent to which the people demand and insist on having a voice in the framing of the con- ditions.

JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

" Old King Cele." When I saw that the youngsters at a Padget° gathering had been singing this, I wondered if it was Mr. Verney Binns' setting. In that case the composer was an old friend of my own, and, when he published that very popular example of his skill, was leading tenor in the Halifax Parish Church Choir. The mere mention'of the title of a part-song which has had an extra- ordinarily successful run, brought to my mind a flood of happy recollections. Relaxation.

At h social gathering Mr. Binns was a host in himself. 1 well remember how at a smoking concert he introduced a most appropriate and irresistiblo tit-bit in the shape of a nonsense jingle. It represented a hard-up townsman longing for "bit o' bacca," and making this pathetic appeal to a little knot of loungers at a street corner:—

"Has onnyonyeronnyonyer Anyonyeronnyonyer? Annyonyer onnyonyer anyonyeronnyanyer?"

" I Sehmokes Mein Pipe." Naturally enough, one turns from this to the grave warning against the tobacco habit quoted in last week's "Hints on Health." In the reference department of a public library within an hour's run of Warrington is a unique col- lection of books on this subject. I lived there a white, and was fond of browsing among these It wail there I read of the Dutchman Von Kldes, who rejoiced in the title "King of Smokers," and whose right thereto there is still none to dispute. By-the-bye, Mr. W. A: Penn has this note on him in "The Soverane Herb": " His,ilen was a museum Of picotian relics, con- taining specimens of every kind of tobacco smoked in the world and every kind of pipe through which the nations draw inspiration. Here he smoked constantly, ince4santly (his life- consumption was estimated at four •tons of tobacco, or 10 pounds every •week of his sixty smoking years—he died at eighty-one), devoting his whole life to the burning of incense to ' Nicotia. His funeral was in harmony with his life. By his express directions his coffin was lined with the wood of old cigar-boxes. At his feet were placed a bladder of the finest Dutch golden-leaf and a packet of caporal; by his side were laid his china-bowled pipe and a box of matches, and steel, flint, and' tinder. . . .

Ail the mourners smoked daring the funeral service, and at the words Ashes to ashes, dust to dust,' shook the ashes of their pipes on to Ow coffin-lid."

Smoking in Church. The Dutch indulge in this, and the practice exists in some parts of South America. It prevailed in Wales so comparatively recently as 1850, and was common throughout Great Britain to the close of the 16th and in the earlier part of the 17th century. The nearest approach to anything of the kind within my own recol- lection was when the Rev. Mark Guy Pearse, the Wesleyan minister whose inimitable Cornish stories none who has heard any of_ them can ever forget, was wont to invite outdoor labourerg into his schoolroom for the dinner-hour. He invariably said a few suitable words to them from the platform, but always insisted that they should first of all light their pipes, just as if they had dined in the open-air.

Home-grown Tobacco. Assuredly it is more important than ever that we should produce more and more tobacco in these islands. It must be well into the teens of years ago since the Department of Agriculture decided to encourage this, and by way of ex- periment subsidised two or three Irish agricul- turists who were willing to try it on such con- ditions. Satisfactory progress has been mean- while reported. I read that tobacco is essentially a desirable crop from the standpoint of the smallholder, so "Who'll be the next?" as they used to sing at the Moody and Sankey revival meetings in the 'seventies: "Not To Be Taken."

According to last week's citation, Dr. Olsen denounces tobacco as a poison. Well, once more, that reminds me. In an office in which I used to be there was a small boy to whom was handed a bottle, labelled as above and not quite empty, with strict injunctions to throw it forthwith into the boiler fire. • Presently he returned, stiL carrying the bottle, and, an explanation being demanded, he urged with a fine assumption of innocence, "I cannot take it; I've read the label, and it says, Not to be taken!'"

Sales Now On.

What I have read in the front page of recent issues of "The Examiner" somehow reminded me of a story which I met with some time ago, and which I will now venture to repeat:— Blinkers was not a beauty. His calves were too flabby for cavalier costume, his arms were too thin for the garb of ancient Vikings, and his face too reminiscent of a disappointed lemon to admit of any heroic headgear. Every- body wondered, therefore, what he would appear as at. the funnybody's fancy dress ball. The night arrived. Hamlet leaned sadly against a wall, while William the Conqueror threw bitter glances at a crowd at the Other end of the room. "What ho, Bill!" cried Oliver Cromwell, arriving late. "Feeling seedy, old man?" "We all are," growled Bill the First. "Blinkers has done us in the eye after all." "Blinkers! How?" ;

*" He's come as a Bargain, marked down to one-and-eleven-three," croaked the Conqueror, "and every blessed girl in the place is fighting to get near him."

"Wasps In January." • The paragraph under this heading in last reek's " Farm and Garden leads me to observe . that even wasps are by no means indispensable; like rats and bluebottles and some other un- pleasant creatures, they have their uses, though in return for their services they exact more or less heavy toll—as the, birds certainly do. Season by season of late years we have had the Increasingly insistent cry, "Swot that fly," and this wiper has greatly helped in the crusade. It has been well remarked that the fly is a pest breeding in all manner of scattered corners, a guerilla that, can only be crushed in detail, a filthy-footed carrier of disease. Now the wasp has the merit of destroying the fly, and in case he should become a nuisance, there is no diffi- culty in finding where he lives with his ten thousand fellow-brigands, and in putting the witsle tribe painlessly to .death. Their enemies , ere our enemies, however, and it is inconvenient that they should be so fond of our food, for, of course, we want the harvest for ourselves, and' 'begrudge them any, part or rot in that matter. 'nue it is that an advocatus vespea wo,uld have a very good case to argue in a court of equity. k)nt of that 0-eat the way is pointed—to accept the good that wasps do while their nest is grow- ing and to.sincrther tilic evil when they become fruit eaters, jam suatct44:;.4 and the accidental tingers of cbildren and fruit pickers. The tqUggestiou is that this is a better way than such wholesale destruction of queeii wasps as was recorded in last week's issue, though the writer is bound to admit that it does not seem iso simple as on the face of Things it ought to be.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0200.txt | included=true | included =====
THE LATE MR. JOSEPH CLARE.

Warringtonian Who Crossed The Mersey By Four Consecutive Bridges.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0201.txt | included=true | included =====
FIRST RESIDENT TO TAKE CHLOROFORM.

BY ARTHUR

By the death of Mr. Joseph Clare Warring- ton loses one of its oldest and most widely- kaown inhabitants. Failing health had re- moved him from active life during tEe past year, and he passed away peacefully on Monday last at his residence, 59, Marbury- street, at the age of 86. Born on January 14th, 1830, his memory took him back to a town which differed very widely from the Warrington of to-day. Events of eighty years ago were fresh in memory to the last, and he could tell stories of famous Mersey floods, of famous fires, and of events which now seem like ancient history. A year ago in a conversation with me he ventured to surmise that he was the only inhabitant who had crossed the Mersey by four consecutive bridges—the Henry VII. bridge, the tempo- rary wooden bridge, the Victoria bridge, and the present King George structure. The

===== 0002601_19160129_art0202.txt | included=true | included =====
JOSEPH CLARE:

temporary wooden bridge which served during the building of the Victoria Bridge remained. very clearly in his memory, and his residence in Latchford during childhood helped to fasten in memory the 'erection of the familiar stone bridge, which has lately disappeared. Mr. Clare was a bibliophile, and at one period had a very large collection of books. Some of his most valuable volumes were given to the Municipal Library a few years ago. The memory of the suffering which followed the amputation of his leg made him thought- ful for others who have to face similar ordeals, and out of the modest competency earned during his years of business he made a gift of £2OO to the 'Warrington Infirmary for the extension fund in 1908, subject to an annual payment of j 8 to him during his

life time.- A similar gift was made to the St. Mark's Hospital, London, in recogni- tion of kindness shown to him during an operation in 1882. He has been a familiar figure in the streets of Warrington for two generations, and in late years has spent much time in the com- pany of the friends who have assembled in the " Old Men's Parliament " in tit° Park.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0203.txt | included=true | included =====
lIIs LIFE STORY.

It will, perhaps, be welcome and appropriate Ito reproduce an article which appeared in "The Examiner" in 1909. The events were narrated at my request and taken down at his dictation.

I was born in the Old-road, Latchford, on January 14th, 1830, the son of a shoemaker. The families of both my father and mother belonged to Cheshire. I was familiar, from my earliest days, with the keenest poverty. My father's income was about 15s. per week, 2s. of which was in demand for house rent. My Mother was a factory hand, and went at six each morning, rising at twelve o'clock on Sunday night to do her washing before going to the mill at six on Monday morning. As I was the eldest child, it fell to ray duty to attend to the housework, and I performed in addition the duties of cook. Not that the cooking required any special skill, for our breakfast consisted of porridge and bread, our dinner of potatoes and bacon, and our tea of bread and treacle. Nine chil- dren were born to my father and mother, and for this family a quarter of a pound of bacon sufficed for dinner. For Sunday, however, half a pound was purchased, and' we had double allowance. Though our fare was scanty, we enjoyed the best of health, and for 20 years I do not remember a doctor entering our house. It may interest my readers to know that seven of the family survive, the youngest being ,65. Our ages total 'to more than 500 years. and the average age is 74. Not a bad record for a family reared upon simple fare.

My father had theories of his own about the upbringing of children. He wanted them to grow to large stature, and as a means of attaining this end would not allow us to go to either school or work. Ile had his reward in see- ing every one of his children exceed his own stature. My early days were taken up in bathing and games, and the earning of an occasional penny for holding horses. When nine or ten years old I went a little to the National School. I well remember at this period the great flood. The water from the river had reached Reynolds's Tannery as I went to school and, had to be waded through. By the time school was over the streets were impassable and people plied the streets in boats.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0204.txt | included=true | included =====
Unprecedented Operation—Locally.

My real schooling, however, was obtained at a later date and, was an indirect outcome of

the greatest misfortune of my life. In 1850, when I had attained the ago of twenty years, a serious affection of the knee of two years' standing necessitated the ,amputation of my leg. I had heard of chloroform, and told Dr. Hardy, the surgeon, that I must hde some , for the operation. He demurred And warned me of the danger, but I insisted, and for two hours was under the influence of it whilst my leg was removed. his operation gave me the distinction of being theitirst man in my native town to be placed under the sedative. For twelve months I lay in bed and my sufferings were increased by the knowledge that bad trade was bringing my parents almost to the point of starvation. In a little room, unrelieved by even a picture, I lay for months, but the doctor, in his compassion, paid for the loan of books from the Mechoics' Institute. began to read books of travel and obtained quite an education in foreign lore. The doc- tors expressed the opinion that I should never recover in the penned:np cottage in Old-road and I was taken to a cottage opposite the Horse and Jockey, a spot which:was then considered, Emia indeed was, a country place. But the jourhey in a coach shook me 60 severely that I determined not to return in it,

===== 0002601_19160129_art0205.txt | included=true | included =====
Last To Ride hi A Sedan Chair.

A happy notion came to me. I knew that the Heath family had a Sedan chair in which a consumptive son used to be carried to Arpley Meadows for fresh air at the times of the

===== 0002601_19160129_art0206.txt | included=true | included =====
MOUNFIELD.

incoming tide. The loan of this was solicited and I made the journey back in this old- fashioned and stately conveyance. But, alas! the old chair had not been dusted for years, and I arrived in town dignified, but nearly as black as a chimney-sweep. My unfortunate loss conferred indirectly an- other honour. It had given me the distinction of being the first Warringtonian to inhale chloroform; now I gained the glory of being the last native to ride in a Sedan chair.

Standing one day in Bridge-street to shelter from a shower. I was called across the street by Mr. Edward Twiss, who inspired by a. kindly inter Est, offered to pay for two quarters' schooling for me at Brewtnall's school in Newton-street. I gladly accepted, and, though a young man •of 21 or 22, was put in the children's classes aild had to work my way up. This I quickly did.; and was soon at tho top. My half-year's tuition being ended. I applied to the Guardians (Mr. Beamont being chair- man) for a grant of 4(1. per week for. school money. This was given, and, assisted 'by a gift of books from Mr. William Alcard and Mr. Edmund Robinson, I got back to my studies. After nine months I began .work at Blundell's coal yard, at a wage of 4s. per week, which was gradually raised to 7s. 6d. I con- trived to live upon is. 11,1 d. per week and kept •it up for weeks in succession. Though walking four miles a (lay on crutches to my work, I felt no inconveniences from my spare ' diet. Cetainly there was no suffering from indigestion! A coal strike having almost de- stroyed the business, I sought work elsewhere, and took a situation in Widnes at a remunera- tion of 2s. per week and food.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0207.txt | included=true | included =====
Poor Start, But Good Finish.

I was approaching my thirtieth year when I drifted into the buying and selling of fenth. With a capital of :30.i. I °Poked business in the Warrington Market. It wYs sorely against the dignity of a de Clare (for I claim to be descended from Gilbert de Chge, a cousin of Williath the Conqueror) to set up in the rag market, but -I faced the situation as bravely as possible, and, after twelve years spent thus, was able to retire. My wants have been few and my hobbies numerous,. and I have been able to live in comfort. Like Joseph Brether- ton, I could say "my riches consist not in the abundance of my possessions, but in the few- ness of my wants."

The Editor asks for recollection of my early clays. My readers may be surprised to hear that I remember the first Warrington election in 183 t!.. I was then a child two and a half years okl, and my Mother carried me to wit- ness the nomination. The hustings were fixed in the triangular space at the junction of But- termarket-street and Dial-street, and I remem- ber the cries of "Hornby for ever." I well remember the building of Warrington Bridge, though I cannot recall its predecessor. The circumstance is fixed on my. mind by the fact that when the temporary bridge was being demolished I fell into a pool left by the exca- vations and was in danger of drowning.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0208.txt | included=true | included =====
Son Of A Chartist.

My father was a Chartist, and in my early days I used to attend the meetings held in a room in Union-street. Here I heard Fergus O'Connor, Ernest Jones„ and many others of the fiery orators of the Charter. I well re- member Captain Leigh, who has been men- tioned in recent issues of your paper. -I recol- lect seeing bins travel round the town in his coffin (a boat-like thing on wheels), and John Stirrup, of Mersey-street, sitting therein and playing a fiddle as they went. Captain Leigh wore a pig-tail and knee. breeches, and tradi- tion had it that he had chosen a certain river bay called Knotts Hole as his watery grave. I enjoyed the friendship of the late Lord Win- marleigh, who often sought me out in the Market. We engaged in happy and animated conversations, for I was then a zealous Radical, and I well remember our discussion of the Reform Bill when it was before the, country, and our frequent debates upon matters of home and foreign politics.

I remember Peter Phillips well, and his wife Hannah, and brother Joshua. I was on the best terms also with Phillip Carpenter, men- tioned in your last issue. They might well call him "Water Rat," for he had a great love of bathing. He went out with his friends to the canal to swim and gave them degrees according to merit. I was one of his happy followers and attained the distinction of an M.A. degree. I was a member, too, of his total abstinence society, and remember that the rules were 60 rigid that when,l had my operation and was ordered some wine, I had to have my name taken temporarily off the roll.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0209.txt | included=true | included =====
THE FUNLII ME-

The funeral took place on Thursday afternoon at Hill Cliffe Burial Ground. The body was carried to the chapel, where a service was con- ducted by Mr. Albert Wright. In the course of a brief address Mr. Wright stated that it had been the expressed wish of their late friend that nothing •should be said of him after his death, and in deference to that wish he would make no reference to his life and character. He would like, however, to say publicly how much he appreciated the kindness of the two nurses who had cared for him during his last illness. Their care of him and the attention they had bestowed was most praiseworthy. Among the mourners were Mr. John Clare and Mr. James S. Clare (brothers), Mrs. Ellen White (sister), Mrs. Shaw (niece), Mrs. Barnes (niece), and Mrs. Birchall. A number of friends and neighbours assembled at the graveyard, among Whom were Mr. R. Harding, Mr. Peter Dalton, Mr. John Grounds, Mr. A. Mounfield, and Mrs. Morland. Messrs. J. Mounfield and Sons carried out the funeral arrangements.

POSTMASTER-GENERAL.

Mr. J. A. Pease Succeeds Mr. Herbert

Samuel.

The King has been pleased to approve the appointment of the Right ion. J. A. Pease, M.P., to be Postmaster-General in succession to Mr. Samuel.

It is' understood that Mv. Pease will not have a scat in the Cabinet. Mr. -Pike Pease, Assistant Postmaster-General, a cousin of the new Minister, will retain his post. Mr. Pease, who was 56 last Monday, is a member of the well-known Yorkshire family and a director of Pease and Partners (Limited). He began his political career as private secre- tary to Mr. John Morley, and after serving ag Junior Whip and then as Chief Whip, he entered the Cabinet as Chancellor of the Duchy in 1910. In the following year he became Edu- cation Minister, but room was not found for him in the coalition Government. Last -June he was granted a political pension of £1,200 a year, and in July, at the request of the' Army Council, he undertook the post of civil member (unpaid) on the Claims Commission in France. In consequence he resigned his position on the Cabinet Committee for the PreVention and Relief of Distress, and the chairmanship of the Professional Classes Sub-conunittee.

LABOUR'S GUARANTEE,

" This is A War Bill, And A War Bill Only." A leaflet has been issued by the Parliamentary Recruiting Committee to meet the allegations by which it is being sought to excite trade unionists against the Bill. The chief fears which the anti-conscriptionlsts have tried to t arouse are that the Bill is an agency for intro- ducing permanent compulsion and that it will in the meantime produce a virtual system of industrial conscription. The pledges which Ministers have given upon both heads are of the most explicit character, and the leaflet usefully - presents them in textual form. Premier's Pledge. The Prime Minister received a Labour depu- tation on January 12th, and in a speech, which was not reported in the Morning papers, said he would be no party to the continuation of compulsion beyond the period of the war. "This is a War Bill, and a War Bill only." With the same clearness Mr. Asquith repu- diated aims of industrial conscription. "I am not in favour of compulsion in regard to any industrial work; I see no reason, for it. As far as I am concerned, I shall absolutely resist it to the lust."

===== 0002601_19160129_art0213.txt | included=true | included =====
Labour Not Menaced,

In apt conjunction with this there is quoted Mr. Henderson's declaration upon the same date in the House of Commons: " I say that the rules and privileges of labour in this Bill aro not menaced in any way. In fact, I say the rights not only of labour but of democracy ara bound up with the victory which may depend upon this Bill." '

===== 0002601_19160129_art0214.txt | included=true | included =====
Needed For Victory.

And the case for the Bill is emphasised by the citation of Lord Kitchener's assurance to the House a Lords that "the existing system without modification is not equal to maintain- ing the Army which is needed to secure victory."

CHARACTER STUDIES. ,

No. 111. THE OLD INN-KEEPER OF HEIDELBURG.

"The Broken idol."

BY HAROLD MAYBURY.

There is a quaint old-fashioned inn lying snugly in a winding lane to the south of old Heidelburg, where. prior to the war, the stu- dents from the University used to congregate , and make merry after their laborious studies, , where maybe over their blue pewter pots of "lager beer" they toasted to "The Day" and boasted of the Fatherland, as men from this country have done throughout the ages. Just now the old inn is closed and rapidly fall- ing into a state pf dilapidation, for its habitues are all at the war; so old Franz, the innkeeper, took unto himself a second wife, and settled down to rear poultry in a small farmstead nearly opposite to his old abode, always within sight of the swinging sign- board, whereon are paintd these words, "The Inn of the Broken Idol." None around the sleepy countryside can tell you of how the inn came to be so named except perhaps old Franz, whose ancestors had resided there since first it was erected. Old Franz, how- ever, is reticent on the matter, for in spite of his three score years and ten, unlike Most old men, he can keep a still tongue in his wise old head. On the reverse side of the sign- board, faint of outline, due to the dusty roads of summer and storms of. winter, is to be seen crude drawings of the German Eagle and an amateurish sketch of the sea. Franz's figure is a familiar one in these parts; his dreamy blue eyes still observe the beauties of Nature, although his sight is failing, and his head is a mass of stiow-white locks. To look at him one would think him a musician, as indeed he is, for at the close of the day's toil he spends an hour or so with his beloved violin, _which, when not in use, hangs by red silk cords from the wall in his humble abode. At eventide, should you be passing along this winding lane, the music of haunting melodies composed by German masters, will be borne to your ears, mingling with the sound of running waters of a plashing rivulet which flows a little distance from inn and cottage.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0216.txt | included=true | included =====
" The Sun Has Set."

Lately, however, Franz has aged .terribly, the eventide of his life seemingly overbur- Tlened with a load of sorrow too great for his feeble frame to bear, and he is easily fatigued. ilia journeys to the historic city of HeidelbUrg are not so frequent as they used to be, and the present struggles of his countrymen have left him restless and much perturbed. The toll of battle and the sight of the many pale- faced women draped in black walking the streets of the city, have visibly changed him and into his eyes has come a look of hopeless- ness and despair. Ofttimes in late afternoon Ono may find him seated in the porch of his cottage reading "The Life of Bismarck" by the aid of his well-worn spectacles:' Suddenly he will close the book, look in the direction of the historic inn, and soliloquise; then mutter to himself:

"The broken idol? Only old Franz knows the story, yet even in my old age !_did not

think it: -NiTeuld come true. Poor Wilhelm, poor Louis, light-hearted Johann, all gone to their last rest, and I have outlived them all. Jail! Who. can tell?. No more women and wine, no more boisterous jests and pranks in the old inn, and nights spent in the lock-up. No more follies of youth and carnivals in beer gardens,' under the light of the moon. They have finished toasting their Emperor and The Day.' Gott in himmol, the sun has set for them. War, not peace, was their maxim. Ha, ha, old Franz knows what is best. Yes, truly broken ithils." In such . strains does the old innkeeper mutter, for lie has seen the horrors of war. To the habitues of the tavern, around the blazing pine logs, in days past, lie used to tell of the struggles of 1870, showing and finger- ing with pride the decorations he earned dur- ing that eventful period.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0217.txt | included=true | included =====
The Argument and the Sequel.

Sometime during the month of last Decem, ber along this sleepy highway there chanced to pass a luxurious motor car on its way to Heide'burg. The sight of its magnificence attracted the attention of old Franz, who at that moment was wending his way to draw water from a well close by. With some sur- prise he noticed that the car had drawn up opposite the old inn, and that one of the chauffeurs was loudly tinocking at the door. Old Franz, with some show of haste in spite of his years, hurried in the direction of the tavern. The occupants of the car had now. alighted and, with impatience, demanded to know of old Franz the reason of the hostelry being closed. "It has closed its doors these twelve months, sires," exclaimed the old man. "I was the proprietor, but owing to the terrible war trade was bad, so I was obliged to seek a, living in another sphere. Can I get you any- thing, sires, from my humble cottage?" The two men clad in military uniforms bade the old man show the way. Within the precincts of the cosy homestead old Franz was com- manded to explain the meaning of the•inn's curious sign, and in fear and trembling this is what he related:—

"For nigh two hundred years, sires, the family of Franz Soburg owned the inn with the curious sign, and I, methinks, am the last of the race. As a boy or nine both of my parents died, so I was left in the charge of my grandfather, who at that time kept the hos- telry. When I took up my abode there, it was known as The Two Spires,' and such it re- mained until I left to fulfil my military duties. One day a Prussian naval 'officer chanced to stay the night under the roof of the old inn, and as the officer was alone he asked my grandfatheeto dirie with him. 31y grandfather, who was a well-read man, during a discussion made the remark that his country could never be a great sea power owing to her geographical position, which infuriated the officer. Hasty words ensued and without more ado the officer picked up his pewter pot and aimed it straight at his host. With alacrity my grandfather dodged out of the way, and it struck a bust of the Emperor, shattering it to pieces. ' At dawn next morning the officer was discovered hanging from, the steel supports of the sign- board. When I arrived home some few months later I found the sign changed to its present name' and also learned the story, which had a double meaning. The first is that an officer of the Fatherland tarried there, , he argued over the future of Germany's sea power, and that a bust of the Emperor had been shattered, signifying a broken idol, but methinks the real reason is confined to the argument. You see, gentlemen, my grand- father asserted that our country would never ' be mistress of the seas, and that the hopes and aspirations of future generations would be as a broken idol. The one coincided with the shattered bust of the Emperor. That is the story of the inn, sires." Immediately after old Franz had related his story , the two were driven away in the direction of the old-world city. of Heidelburg. The same day old Franz received a letter coin- manding him in the name of His Imperial Majesty to refrain from r the story.epeating So the old innkeeper of Heidelburg ekes out a miserable existence, with the memory of a visit of his Imperial master, with the remem- brance of the laughter and gaiety of those stulents who have fallen for the Fatherland, the thoughts of the year 1870, and of a life fast drawing to a close.

"It is most kind of you!" . .. ~ - .

"Suppose we make a beginning with a small table and some chairs ?"

" Oh, thank you tie much!"" I don't know how to thank you." "Poor mama will be so grateful!" "And then you would like some tea. I'll aave some sent in: I know that, when ladies are ever so . miserable, it only takes the sight of a cup of tea to raise their spirits again .to the proper level." ' 'Then liaTt7e ' properly !" cried Ermyntrude merrily, as he hurried down the little garden to the gate, to make his exit in a more dignified manner than he had made his entrance. " Who is he ?" asked Rosemary, the cous in. She was five and twenty, a prim young woman who disapproved, as much as did her aunt, of chance acquaintances. And he's so good looking," added Sheila, enthusiastically. "He may not be a desirable acquaintance for all that," said her cousin.

Oh, indoors, please," said Ermyntrude, who saw -with horror that some of the youth- ful villagers, by this time released from school, were planting their dusty little faces against the railings at the bottom of the gar- den, much interested in the proceedings. "This way then, Miles." "Yes, sir.

HOMELESS FOR TMRTY-EIGHT YEARS.

Soldier's Plea When Charged With

Theft.

On the night of, Friday, the 11th inst., P.C. Joynson, of the Cheshire Lines Police, whilst On duty at the Central Station, was told thai a man was going about the station offering mili- tary boots and shoes for sale.. Ile was also told that the man had left a parcel under a seat. Accordingly, he kept watch, and when the would-be salesman turned up for his property, he put a few questions to him, with the result that on Saturday morning, James Francis North, of no fixed abode, found himself in thq dock at the Warrington Police Court, charged with stealing three pairs of Army boots, a comb and two Army shirts, to the value of £3 135., the property of the military authorities.

He pleaded guilty, and made a statement to the Bench, in which he said "It has been my misfortune to have been born in slums and to be brought up amongst drunkards. In conse- quence, when -.1 was a boy, I was not sent to school'. hau. never known what it .is to have a home for 38 years. I have served 11 years in the Army, including India and South Africa. I hope you will show me a little mercy and I Will do my utmost to be better in the future. When I took the boots I was in the military hospital tvaiting for my discharge." Inspector nodgkiss said the prisoner had been in the South Lancashire Regiment and had been before the Medical Board. He discarded his uniform and -took the boots. leaving the barracks by climbing orer the wall. North was sentenced to two months' hard labour.

SUNDAY'S DINNER BEER.

Must Not Be Taken Into Works.

On Sunday, the 16th inst., George O'Brien, of Northwich, who is engaged on war work with a well-known firm, endeavoured to tare a pint of beer On to his employer's premises to drink with his dinner, .but as a result of this action he found himself at .the Warrington Munitions Court on Monday, charged ,With,' atternpting to take intoxicating liquors on to the works. k foreman at the works said that on the 16th inst. he saw O'Brien taking the beer on to the premises. He asked him if he knew he ought not to take intoxicants into the works, and O'Brien replied "It is a 'pity it a man..-cannot have a pint of beer for his Sunday dinner." Witness told him he could not take the beer into the zworks, so O'Brien went outside and drank it.

O'Brien maintained he had never seen the notice in the works prohibiting the taking in of

intoxicants. but he was fined 2::. 6d. _ Mr. T. J. Ridgway presided over the Court, and his assessors were Messrs. Tohn H. Williams, of Crewe (employers' panel) and Joseph T. Robinson, of Prescot (workmen's panel).

THAT "LIVERISH" STATE.

Any man or woman is liable .to fall into a wretched liverish state, just through unsuit- able food, or change of weather, or through !neglecting to give the liver a little ,occasional !help in the form of a suitable tonic, such as ;Mother Seigel's Syrup.
