AN IRISH LABOURER.

3und Crouching Under A Stall In The Market.

For his suspicious conduct on Sunday night of last week in the Market, Patrick Byrne, a labourer from Ireland, was kept in custody for seven days and then released. He was before the magistrates on Monday of last week on a charge of loitering with intent to commit a felony. He was then remanded in order that in- quiries could be made. When again before the Colift on Monday, the Chief Constable said on the Sunday night in question Byrne was found by a police officer crouching under, a stall in the Market. He said he was going to sleep there, but it- was im- possible to sleep in such a position. The pre- vious day he had drawn £1 Is. as wages, so there should live been no necessity for him to sleep

The Chief Constable added that perhaps the seven days' detention the prisoner•had had to undergo would be sufficient punishment, and suggested he should be released. This course was agreed to.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0226.txt | included=true | included =====
BEATING A DONKEY.,

Having seen two youths hitting a donkey with a stick in Winwick-road, Inspector Dee, of the R.S.P.C.A., had them charged with cruelty at the Police Court on Wednesday. He said that the boys were rag gathering, and it was a continual thing for them to act in this way. The Chairman (Mr. J. W. Hayward) to the defendants: What made you do it? One of the defendants: It would not go fast

enough. Mr. Hayward: If you continue like this, you will become brutes, not men. The defendants were each fined 10s., the Chairman remarking that the magistrates were sorry that they could not give them the same punishment which the defendants had given to the donkey.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0227.txt | included=true | included =====
HID UNDER THE TABLE.

Soldier Who Is Always Running

Away.

A soldier who gives the police a great deal of trouble is Alwyn Jenks, of the Cheshire Regi- ment. He was charged at the Borough Police Court on Thursday morning with being an absentee from his regiment. Detective Turner said that in consequence of information received, he went to 13, Antrobus- street, where he found Jenks hiding under the

ble.- He had been absent from his regiment

for 17 days. The Chief Constable commented that Jenks was always away from his regiment. It was a pity that he could not hide himself somewhere wnere he could not be found. The police re- ceived communications about him nearly every week.

Jenks was remanded to be handed over to an escort.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0228.txt | included=true | included =====
HANGING ON A NAIL.

Boy Steals Watch Belonging To Neighbour.

Left in the house of a neighbour while the tenant went away, a ten-year-old boy took down a watch, valued 10s., which was hanging on a nail in the wall, and ran off with it. A day or two afterwards he was arrested by Detective Green and charged at the Police Court on Wed- nesday with the offence. The watch was the property of Mrs. Elizabeth Garrett, 12, Dial- street. Her husband is serving abroad. The detective stated that after the boy had stolen the watch he showed it to his friends, ex- plaining in one case that his father had given it to him, and in the other that it belonged to his father.

The magistrates ordered. him to receive six strokes of the birch rod.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0229.txt | included=true | included =====
IMPORTED DRUNKARDS.

Spoil Record Made By Warringlonians

When a Liverpool woman was charged at the Police Court on Wednesday with being drunk in Sankey-street, the Chief Constable said that during the last twelve months there had been an increase of 120 in the number of people who came into the town and got drunk, while the number of Warringtonians who had got drunk had decreased by over 100. "There is hardly any use of a town becoming sober when these people keep coming through," lib declared, and Mr. J. W. Hayward, in fining the prisoner 95., said we did not want people to come from Liverpool to disgrace Warrington.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0230.txt | included=true | included =====
ARRESTED AFTER THREE YEARS

Alleged Theft Of Horse And Trap.

The arm of the law is very long, as Charles Henry Barker, who was arrested last Decem- ber in Hull,. has -found. With another man, in December of 1912, he went to see Mr. Ed- ward Connor, of Sankey-street, and hired a horse and trap, saying he wanted to go into the country for some potatoes. The following day he sold the horse and trap in Liverpool. Nothitig more was heard of him in•Warrington until December last—three years after•—•-when he was arrested in Hull. Barker was brought before the boroughtmag- istrates on Friday morning,_ charged with

stealing the horse, trap and harness, valued, at £3O, and the Chief Constable asked for a week's adjournment. The prisoner asked that the case should be dealt with immediately, saying he had been in prison since Christmas on other charges. The Chief Constable's application was granted, but he intimated that if it were pos- sible, he would bring the case before the Court before the week had expired.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0231.txt | included=true | included =====
MAGISTRATES DISAGREE.

Al ife's Second Claim Against her

Husband Fails:

It is -not often the Warrington Borough mag- istrates disagree, but this occurred in a case heard on Friday morning. Mr. S. Wright was in the chair, and his col- leagues were Aldermen Smethurst and Bolton, Councillor Wilkinson, and Messrs. T. Locker, W. Furness, G. Pilling and A. J. Jolley. - The case was that in which Mrs. Emily Swift, of 60, Ilaydock-street, applied-for a sep- aration order (on the ground of desertion) from her; husband, who is now residing at 17, Scott-- street, Warrington. The previous week Mrs. Swift applied for an order on the ground that her. husband was an habitual drunkard, and the case was then dismissed. On Friday Mr. T. S. Steel represented the applicant and Councillor W. 11. J. Jenkins the defendant. Mr. Steel said that on the 16th inst. the de- fendant left his wife and had not been to see her since, nor had he sent anything for her maintenance. _ _

Councillor Jenkins maintained that on more than one occasion Mrs. Swift had ordered her husband out of the house. Instead of Swift deserting his wife, he had been absolutel driven out: - •

After hearing the solicitors' arguments and the evidence of both Mr. and Mrs. Swift, the Bench decided by a majority to dismiss the summons.

There is harmony in the life only as all its strings are tuned by pain.

RUGBY.

NORTHERN UNION.

Yorkshire Clubs Still On Top.

As a result of last Saturday's matches, Leeds and Hull still maintain their positions at the head of the League. Leeds gained a victory at the expense of Rochdale Hornets, but it was only by the odd point in eleven. Huddersfield reminded one of their old-time glory by run- ning up a score of 41 points to 10 against Halifax. .

NTERMEDIATE LEAGUE.

No Change In League Positions.

As a result of Saturday's games in the local Interniediaie League, when tavourabie con- ditions again prevailed, all tue teams remain in the same position as last week. Two matches should have taken place at Wilderspool, but St. James', who have only two victories to their credit so far, and are at the bottom of the ladder, failed\to put in an appear- ance against the League leaders, Parochial. Consequently the latter team were denied an opportunity of gaining another two points, and thus materially strengthening their position at the head of affairs- On the other hand, Heath- side. Parochial's nearest rivals, met and de- feated Bea Mont, after a good game, .by seven points to nil. This brings their points up to twenty, against Paroehial's twenty-two, but the " Heathens" have played an additional match. At Gorsey-lane, Rylands's were altogether too good for St. Mary's, who were defeated point- less. Everyone was surprised at the number of chances missed by the home team. Indeed, the score which they triumphed by, had they taken all the chances which came their way, should have represented their half-time score. At Victoria Park, Bolton accounted for Sacred Heart by five points to three, after an evenly contested game.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0234.txt | included=true | included =====
BOLTON'S NARRq,W WIN.

After a hard struggle, during which there was little to choose between the respective teams, Bolton triumphed over Sacred Heart by five points to three. The game, which was fought out at a great pace, took place on Victoria Park, in the presence of a fair number of spectators, and on a suitable ground. From the commencement Bolton attacked, but good kicking by the " Hearts " kept them out. By steady play the " Hearts " worked them- selves towards their opponents' line, which, for a time, was hotly besieged, but the Bolton lads proved themselves able defenders. Play was finally removed to midfield, where, from a scrimmage, Bolton broke away, but a fine lusty kick by one of the defenders relieved the situation. From now onwards give and take play was the order, and when the final whistle sounded Bolton were victorious, as stated above, by one goal, one try, five points; to one try, three poinss.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0235.txt | included=true | included =====
ANOTHER HOME WIN FOR RYLANDS'S.

A fair crowd assembled at Rylands's to wit- ness the match between the home team and St. Mary's. The ground was rather on the soft side, and two features of the game—especially during the first half—were the large number of free-kicks awarded to each side, and the numer- ous chances which Rylands's allowed to let slip by. The home team won the toss, and elected to play with 'a strong breeze at- their backs. The opening kick being returned, play, for a shorx period, settled down in midfield. By good for- ward work, Rylands's worked their way to their oppontnts line, and, had a little more steadiness been displayed, the St. Mary's citadel must have, fallen. Once indeed, a home player went over the line with the ball, but, to the dismay of the home supporters, he allowed the sphere to roll from his grasp. Rylands:s were gradually driven back, and the visitors had a spell of attacking, but in their" case also, the finishing movements were weak. The home team came again, but displayed another weakness—that of selfishness. The right-centre often clung to the ball when his wing man was lying unmarked. A good pass- ing movement was now commenced, and Littlewood passing to Britton, that player made a nice opening for Hill, who dashed over a4l opened the score for the home team. Dumbell failed to convert, and the score at half-time stood at a try to nil. Shortly after the resumption, St. Mary's were awarded a free-kick, but nothing came shr it. Rylands's now again took up the running, an.l, after a good forward movement, Britton score 1 a try, but Archer failed in his attempt to :1•Id the extra points. From now onw:ir I- Rylands's had almost all the play, tries being scored by Mulvey, Britton, Littlewood and Latimer: Dumbell and Mulvey goaling one each. The final score read: Rylands's, two goals, five tries, i 9 points; St. Mary's, nil. The forwards on each side played a good 'game.

ASSOCIATION.

NORTHERN UNION

Causes New Club To Alter. Its

Name.

TO BE KNOWN AS WARRINGTON

TON\ \.

g7r he new Association football team having called themselves Warrington has been consid- ered an infringement of copyright bek the Northern Union, who have written to the Football Association and laid .a complaint. The point has been carefully considered, and, so as to avoid confusion in the respective Rugby and Association teams, of which we may be proud, the new team will henceforth be known as Warrington Town—though it does I play in the district. To-day (Saturday) they meet South Liver- pool at lloodiane, Sankey, and the team will be selected from the following: Messrs. Kite, Leah, Cookson (late of Tranmere Rovers), Millward, Ernest Brookes (International), Ditchfield, George Whitfield, Bullen, Aspinall, T. Whitfield, Foster, Illidge, Banner and Connor. Seeing that South Liverpool defeated War- rington Town last week by the only goal of the I match a thrilling game may be expected.

Warrington's Good Show Against The Leaders.

On Saturday the Warrington Association team paid their first visit to Dingle Park, the home of the South Liverpool team, who are the present leaders of the Lancashire Combination and, owing to the splendid form shown in the two games played against Tranmere Rovers, there was a good crowd present to give them a wel-

===== 0002601_19160129_art0240.txt | included=true | included =====
come

The game was:very even and keenly contested throughout. It was in the first half that the Warringtonians missed their best chances of scoring, Aspinall missing a very easy chance when a few yards in front of the goal, with only the custodian to beat. Following that, Bullen was entrusted with a penalty-kick, from which he failed to score, and at the interval no goals had been registered.

The game had only been in progress a few minutes when the home team scored the onlY, goal of the match, Rigby doing the needful.

The Warrington forwards made many strong attempts, to get on equal terms, but the home defence succeeded in holding the fort intact. To come away from the Dingl;defeated by the only goal scored in the game should give no cause to be downhearted; but it should act as a stimulus to reverse the result in to-day's return engage- tuent at Hood-lane when, given fine weather, there will be a great crowd to encourage the home team to try and secure their first victory in the competition at the expense of the cham- pions.

The Warringcn Central had arranged to play what might have been termed a test match with the Sankey Athletic. These two teams have met twice before this season and played drawn games. On both occasions the Central were not able to place their best team in the field, and as the Athletic have not been defeated yet, the Central arranged to play on tlieir, ground and bring a full team. The Secretary of the Central Club received note by post on the Wednesday asking for confirmation, and this was done, and on the Friday he received another note cancelling the match, much to the dis- appointment of the Central team.

The Emmanuel Institute also had their fixture with the ,Walton Club cancelled owing to the latter being unable to etag team together, their players having enlisted during the last two months.

'These two clubs were able to arrange a match I on the Saturday morning, and the game was ! played in Victoria Park. The Institute had three of their regular players away, but good substitutes were found in Catlow, Warburton and Wilson. There was a strong wind blowing straight up the playing piece, which was of great assistance to the Central in the first half. T. Knight, in goal. handled the ball only once. In the second half the wind abated a lot, and the. Central :proved the stronger team and won: by five goals against 'none. This is only the second defeat of the Emmanuel Institute this

Warrington Lose To South Liverpool

Warrington were away to South Liverpool on Saturday, their team being slightly altered to the one which did duty the previous week. Ditehneld took the place .of Illidge at outside-

WARRINGTON AND DISTRICT JUNIOR LEAGUE.

TABLE TO DATE.

Goals P. W. L. D. F. A. P. St. Elphin's Fairfield United Res. 8 4 3 1 24 12 9 Stockton Heath Scouts 9 4 4 1 19 17 9 Orford Juniors Howley United Bewsey A.F.C. Penketh Juniors Mersey Juniors * Penketh Juniors have had two points de- ducted for playing ineligible players

On Saturday one of the most interesting fix- tures for the day, Howley United v. Penketh, had to be cancelled owing to the ground upon which the Howley club play their.home fixtures being not available.

On the Arpley ground the leaders of the League got a fright in their engagement with the lads from Bewsey. With the wind in their favour the visitors held the lead at the interval by -the only point scored. The second portion was strenuously contested by the "Saints," and they had the greater share of the play, but owing to the good defence of the visitors it was only minutes from the call of 'time when the " Saints " gained the winning point,' which was a very lucky point for them. It was gained by a long kick from midfield, and owing to the strong wind the ball curled towards the goal, and one of the Bewsey defenders, in trying to head the ball away, unfortunately put through his own goal. It was in this manner Chat the "Saints" luckily escaped losing their first point.

The Fairfield United Reserves got a shock on Saturday, when they had Orford Juniors as visitors to their ground. Although the home

Lthlb was represmited by their bes-t team, they fully expected to gain both points, but the lads

from Orford had had their calculations upset the week previous, and were determined to make strong efforts to succeed in this match, and they were rewarded, much to the satisfaction of their supporters.

This was the best display of the Oriord Juniors this season, and Doughty, Povey 'rand Clare were excellent for the winners. The home team was best served by Kidd, Lygo, Boyle and Walker.

I am informed that during the progress of the game, on several occasions, when the Fair- field goal was :under attack, someone amongst .the spectators blew a whistle, which caused much annoyance to the players. This is not a very sportsmanlike action; and it is not the first time the same complaint has been made.

Some of the clubs in the League are very anxious to.have a knock-out competition bei(:re the season closes.

ATHLETIC.

At Widnes on Saturday.. The Unitarians were more fortunate in their match with the Athletic than the previous week against Emmanuel, as they were able to play their strongest team. The home team have only been beaten twice this season, so, the Unitarians had a stiff task on hand. Hallam won the toss for the visitors, and gave them the advantage of the wind and slope. STORIES FROM THE TRENCHES.

AN AWFUL PLACE.

IN atelling Taubes In Action Every' . Morning.

Mr. T. Gleave, 14, Hawthorn-road, Stockton Heath, has received a letter from a friend at the Front. He says : " I am in an awful place at present. No town of any size for miles, • and the most awful roads you ever heard of. The mud is often ankle-deep in the middle of the road, so you can guess what it is like when you have to step on one side to allow a transport to pass. It is rather exciting here in the daytime. We usually have the pleasure of watching a Taube getting shelled in the morning, and now and again we get a bomb from one. Again, the German artillery occasionally find us out and I can tell you that on such occasions we lie very low indeed, and make a noise like a rabbit. Our grub is not bad considering, being better in quality than that at the base. The only thing is that sometimes we have to do without butter. We are all keeping pretty well in health, despite plenty of hard work. Handling spades and picks is a bit more exhausting than pen-pushing. I have been told that this is the worst part of the line for fighting, and I can well believe it."

===== 0002601_19160129_art0257.txt | included=true | included =====
"011! THOSE SLACKERS !''

Sapper Daniel's Interesting Letter

From Salonika.

Mr. and Mrs. G. Daniel, 13, Forster-street, have received the following interesting letter from their son, Bert, a Sapper in the R.E. Mediterranean Expeditionary Force :--" I am still at . I am sending a Greek-made handkerchief, which I think will serve as a souvenir. I have also got a Greek bullet and a Turkish bullet, and a piece of cast iron, which fell about ten inches from me just before we left Suvla Bay. I have had a few narrow escapes, and I' must thank God for being alive at present. We had a pretty decent Christmas, only a Taube gave us a visit. I received the newspaper and I saw that Gilbert Roughley had been killed. When I read about him sbeing missing, I gave it up for a bad job, because he had both a daring and dangerous duty to per- form. He must have died a hero. Oh! If only some of the slackers could only come over here and see for themselves what we have gone through there would be no peed for conscrip- tion."

Sapper Daniel, who is 22 years of.age, enlisted in April, having formerly been employed in the Warrington Electricity Department. lie was educated at the Blue Coat School. An elder son was lost in the sinking of the Monmouth.

THE BREAKING-UP OF MEETINGS.

The Warrington Scene Alentioned In

Parliament

In the House of Commons on Wednesday, Sir W. P. Byles, Oh behalf of Mr. Snowden, asked the Prime Minister what action he proposed to take to prevent the breaking pp of public meet- ings by soldiers in uniform, in view of the fact that during the last few days meetings had been so attacked at Southgate, Lambeth, Newcastle, and Warrington; and if he was prepared to assist the carrying out of the Home Secretary's promise that no Government interference would be offered to the reasonable public discussion of the question of conscription. Mr. Asquith (Prime Minister): Soldiers are in the same position as civilian's as regards their liability to be proceeded against by the civil ,authorities in respect of any breach of the civil law. In-view of tbe fact that so large a. pro- portion of the population is at present in the Army, it is not consicistred „desirable expressly to forbid soldiers—who are now always in uniform—from attending during their leisure hours meetings which are held for the discus- sion of subjects of national importance when the discussion is unobjectionable from a military point of view. (Cheers.)

Circumstantial Evidence.

SHIPS THAT CARRY STRANGE CARGOES.

"King Solomon drew merchantmen, Because of his desire For peacocks, apes and ivory, Froxn Tarshish unto Tyre:'•

From this .we may take it that, from the days when the trading galleys of Sidon put to sea—passing along with the procession of Roman tin-seekers, Viking plunderers and , Spanish treasure-shizs, up to the cargo- tramps of the Britisd Mercantile Marine of to-day—merchants have never hesitated to pack all 'manner of wares into the holds of ships. And what a tempting theme, that of the cargoes of the dead centuries! To surmise the homeward-bound merchandise of the ves- sels which the Elizabethan navigators steered into Plynrouth, or the rich East Indian stores which the round-bellied Dutch traders brought back to Rotterdam! The wziter perused recently the prospectus of a wreck-raising company, and saw men- tion of a long-lost wine ship buried in the sands: that cargb, if obtained, would fetch fabulous prices. But, as a fact, the merchan- dise which travels over the seas to-day is quite as strange as that which lies "all sunk beneath the wave" off the Texel, or the west coast of Scotland or along the eastern shores of Africa—all favourite spots for those who would bring up buried treasure.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0263.txt | included=true | included =====
WILD BEASTS AS FREIGHT.

The proprietors of travelling menageries often came much misgiving to the ship-mas- ter by the cages of wild beasts which they send aboard, and should bad weather pre- vail, them may arise trying contingencies to be faced if the occupants of these cages get loose. The question of food supplies, too, is not easy to face. I fancy that some skippers who have done' business for Mr. Hagenbeck, in bringing home for him wild creatures from East Africa for his famous open-air menagerie could tell some queer tales of the difficult& they have had to meet. Vesseis arriving in Liverpool from West Africa often bring hundreds of monkeys and parrakeets, and some of the queerest cargoes ever bound for London River, have been those consigned to Jamrach's„ _

If you v7ant to catch a glimpse of a wild beast at sea, look .at one of Kipling's short stories entitled "Bertran and Bimi;" he tells of an orang-outang caught somewhere in the Malayan Peninsula, and going to Eng- land. "For four days he had struggled, yelled and wrenched at the heavy bars of his cage without ceasing, and had nearly slain a lascar, incautious_ enough to come within reach of the great hairy paw." Major F. G. Jackson, leader of the Jack- son-Harmswarth Poiar Expedition, tells of the struggles of a huge polar bear which had been captured alive and brought on board. The animal in its fury burst open the bars at the top of. the cage and almost succeeded in taking charge of the ship. An opportune blow on the head with a hammer, however, caused him to drop back into the age, and he was kept in check until spars were lashed over the aperture he had made by crumpling up the bars.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0264.txt | included=true | included =====
DEADLY CARGOES.

In the event of fire breaking out on board ship, gunpowder and petroleum are awkward items to figure among the cargo. The writer went out to Malta on a steamer carrying ten into the of powder. To load this, she went the lower reaches of the Thames and the stuff was brought alongside in early morn- ing; powder must be put aboard in daylight, and both steamer and barge were flying the red danger flag. The galley fire was put out, the engine-room fires were banked, two in- spectors watched over the stowage in a speci- ally boarded-off partition in the hold; the ponder was in green-painted wooden boxes, and"when it .v/f3 discharged on arrival, simi- lar precautions were taken.

Cciffee sounds an innocent substance to carry on board ship, but Clark Russell calls green coffee berries "fifty thousand times worse than a freight of gunpowder mixed with infernal machines." This is on account of the deadly vapour given off, and his story tells of a Dutch brig, thus loaded, in which practically all her crew went stark raviñg. mad and died before the two survivors were picked up. Danger may arise, if the seas are rough, from -bulky cargo breaking adrift, and I have noticed heavy guns, big teak baulks and huge boilers, figuring as cargo, were always lashed or chained with very great care.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0265.txt | included=true | included =====
WOOL, RABBITS, FROZEN MUTTON.

In the stowage of wool, tight packing is necessary, and anyone who has been in Aus- tralian ports, will have noticed the " wool- screws " and their use. The bales are placed loosely in position, and then a screw caused to work outwards at both ends by means of a handle, forcv suff4cient space for a larger one to be inserted; this process is repeated till there is room for another bale, and so the soft load becomes pressed firm. In the old wooden "clippers," cases are known in which the pressure has been so great that the timbers of the hull have been burst open. Steamers coming from New Zealand will sometimes contain as many as ten thousand carcases of sheep and a hundred thousand rabbits. Needless to say there must be a re- frigerating plant on board to keep cold the space under hatches where the mutton car- cases are suspended. I believe such ships carry a lad known as the "snow boy" or "Snowy," whose busi- ness it is to keep the white flakes caused by the refrigerator appliances from collecting below.

The writer has seen at Hobart, Tasmania, cases of apples lying waiting on the wharf by the thousand, and he once sailed from Hamburg in one of the Great Central steam- ers which was full up of baskets of plums, even though there were about a hundred thousand baskets still waiting to be loaded. CURIOUS HUMAN FREIGHTS.

Ships have, sometimes to carry extraordin- ary collections of people as passengers. Some years ago, the Salvation Army decided to i open up a matrimonial bureau, n Canada, I think, and sent out a cargo consisting of waitresses, factory girls, domestic servants, and farmers' daughters! I travelled once from Malta to Alexandria with an Arab circus troupe numbering over eighty, of whom the rank and file camped in tents on deck; they kept themselves in form by turning cartwheels and walking on their hands between the hatches, and this even though there might be some sea running. When Barnum brought his troupe to Europe, he chartered a special steamer, and the oar- 'go was, therefore, composed of as weird a company of human freaks as ever sailed the seas.

One remembers Captain Kettle's difficulties with his pilgrims in the Red Sea. The writer met in -Quebec, a port doctor, who had had to go aboard a ship which. brought to Cana- da some hundreds of Russian Dukobhers, and his account of its verminous state was simply appalling. Much might be written of the cargoes of bullion brought across the seas; not long since, the papers recorded the arrival in an English port of a liner which bore a hundred and sixty tons of Mexican dollars packed in kegs, as well as silver bars valued at seven hundred thousand pounds. A good deal, too, might be written of those curious products, so little known to the pub; lie, and yet which show on a ship's manifest, Cryolite, pimento, trepang, annatto, beehe-de- mer, esparto, and so forth. There are, I be- lieve, some vessels which do little else than carry cargoes of pitch from the "Asphalt Lakes" of Trinidad.

A taste for chicken is not the only appetite a preacher needs.

Except.. Money will buy anything—except happiness; it will enable you to go anywhere except to heaven.

A 1910 Wonder.

In France experiments are now being carried out using the aeroplane as an offensive weapon. In one such recent experiment the aviator flew to a height of 375 yards with a gun mounted on the aeroplane.—From an American paper dated 1910.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0267.txt | included=true | included =====
BANKERS OPTI

Expert Opinion That Germany Is Irretrievably Beaten.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0268.txt | included=true | included =====
OUR VICTORY FORETOLD.

Several of the leading banks of the' 'country have held their annual meetings this week, and a uniformly optimistic' note has been sounded.

While our trade is flourishing, that of the enemy has been throttled mainly by our sea power; and the Germans are held not only to be beaten, but to know that they are beaten.

The bankers warn us that we have trying times still before us; but they prophesy that, if we do our utmost in every walk of life, victory is assured.

other of the larger banks, our statement on this occasion contains an unusual entry, on both sides, of a liability undertaken under Treasury Minute of November 17th, 1915. This item de- notes our share, for which under Government guarantee we are responsible, of a total credit for £10,000,000 arranged in New York by a com- mittee of bankers on either side of the water'. with the view of rectifying and steadyirg the exchange between the two countries. Trade Prosperity. With regard to the trade of the country, from all the centres in which we are interested come very similar reports. The various industries that minister to war needs are, of course, doing exceptionally well, while others are doing a profitable business owing to absence of competi- tion from the Continent. A few trades like the building trade and others connected with public utilities are nearly stagnant, but even in the case of some industries which are chiefly con- cerned in the production of luxuries, such as the silk and lace trades, we have satisfactory reports. (Hear, hear.) But of these again their present activity is due directly or indirectly in great measure to the war. Lace manufacturers have been called upon to supply various war accessories, such as mosquito curtains, res- pirators, khaki webbing bandages, while the silk trade has since last autumn experienced a brisk demand from America. 'Prices have gone up 30, 50, and even 100 per. cent., the demand being due to the rapidly growing riches of the United States. While we may congratulate Macclesfield and Leek and Tiverton on their good fortune, we must regret that they are benefiting largely at the expense of our ally France, whose silk industries are more or less at a standstill (Hear, hear.) The cotton trade has been quiet during the year, but mills for the most part have been kept working. Nor does the great rise in price'from 41d. in December, 1914, to M. at the present time, appEar to have cherhe.l demand for goods for the Far East, though, of course, trade in the Near East is altogether dis- organised. Shipowners, as everyone is aware, have had an exceedingly prosperous time. Lastly, farmers all over the country, though hampered, perhaps, by shortage of labour, must have had an unusually good year, having ob- tained high prices for every kind of farm pro- duce. (Hear, hear.) Dividend Of 18 Per Cent.

Having referred to the fact that 75 per cent. of the eligible men of the staff of the bank had enlisted or attested, a remark which Was loudly applauded, Mr. Parr reverted to financial mat- ters and continued :

In preparation for any eventualities, instead of putting any sums away, as is our usual prac- tice, to reserve, or premises, or to other so-to- speak earmarked 'accounts, we are carrying for- ward the unusually large sum of £293,899 10s. 6d. in what I may term the most get-at-able form. Possibly some of our 13,000 odd share- holders who are curious enough to Compare other bank statements with our own, may be puzzled that, whereas most other kindred insti- tutions have shown larger profits this year than last, we have shown a smaller one. It is merely a question as to how you state your case. We deduct the amount assigned for depreciation of investments before reckoning our profits. The other banks first state what they call their prutit and then proceed to deduct therefrom the amount of their depreciation. Now I co afess think ours is the most commonsense method, I cannot see how it is possible to quote net profit until all necessary deductions, of which surely depreciation is one, have been made. (Hear, hear.)

The Chairman concluded by moving: "That the report now presented be received and adopted, and that a dividend at the rate of 18 per cent, per annum, subject to income tax, be now declared for the half-year ended 31st Decem- ber, 1915, payable on and after sth February, 1916."

Mr. Whalley (deputy-chairman) seconded, and the motion was carried.

RE-ELECTI ONS.

They had, however, produced a very satisfactory report. (Hear, hear.) He was glad their old friend, Mr. Whalley, remained a director; it was to be hoped he would continue to do so for many years. .(Hear, hear.)

PARR'S BANK LIMITED.

Mr. Cecil Fe Parr On Exhaustion

In Essentials.

Mr. Cecil P. Parr presided over the fifty-first ordinary general meeting of Parr's Bank Limited, held on Thursday at the Cannon-street note', London. The Chairman, in moving the adoption of the report and balance sheet, which have already appeared in "The Examiner," said the war might provide even still more serious phases for the Allies; but he had no doubt we should come through the conflict with success. • Germany Ruined. . .

Germany, he continued, though not yet beaten in the field, must already be more or less ruined commercially, even were the war to stop to-morrow. With so many of her home indus- tries, crippled, her external trade lost, her mercantile fnarine• dispersed or destroyed, her people crushed beneath the burden of enormous debts, it will be difficult for her to right herself financially. It is by exhaustion in essentials that we shall win this war, and if that process had been applied more drastically, many months ago, we might have been now within a measure- able distance of finishing the war, and have saed many a gallant life. (Hear, hear.) lam afraid that not even yet the vast majority,lsf the nation has taken to heart, or certainly has not wit in practice, the lessons on thrift and economy so persisently inculcated by a portion of the Press. 'Ministerial utterances have been few and have fallen on deaf ears, yet the need is urgent. It is long since Mr. Lloyd George talked of silver bullets and Mr. McKenna of the last million. (Hear, hear.) Our Own Vital Need.

It is vital to us, vital to our power of outlast- ing our enemies in material rescoarces, vital to London continuing to be the hub and centre of the world's monetary system, that we should in every possible way curtail our imports and save our money here at home, and the only method to bring this about effectively, is to exclude rigorously by heavy duties all imports, with the exception of food, clothing and munitions— everything, in short, which is not essential either to living or fighting—for it is useless to expect the nation to perform voluntary acts of abnegation, for which the vast majority will neither understand the reason, nor believe in the efficacy of the result. This further material advantage would, ipso facto, result from the stoppage of unnecessary imports. It would tend to cheapen our necessary imports. For the price of these last is obviously considerably en- ha nc ed by the excessive,. freights now ruling, owing to the dearth of ships. This dearth is in turn due to a large extent to the commandeering of so many vessels by the Government for trans- port. In this latter respect, too, we venture to think that with better expert organisation of the shipping on the part of the, Government, a greater economy of our carrying power could be effected, with a corresponding reduction in price of freights. Such expedients as the Anglo- French Loan, the sale of American securities, the establishing of the bankers' credit in New York,, are all merely palliative measures. They relieve a tension, which would otherwise be ex- treme. They reduce the high temperature for a time, but leave the disease itself untouched. Only in one way, and that is by stopping all un- necessary imports, can we so starve the disease that the temperature will fail to normal. To drop the metaphor, only then will exchange with America and other countries recover to ante- war rates.

===== 0002601_19160129_art0270.txt | included=true | included =====
Gigantic Figures.

I do not to-day propose to trouble you with any detailed enumeration of the gigantic figures which the nation has almost grown accustomed to deal with, and to talk of. Literally, instead :of thinking in 'millions, as Mr. Chamberlain 'once told us to do, we have now learnt to think land to deal in hundreds of millions. 1 may just recall in ,a few words how that before the war was many months old, successive. Chancellors of the Exchequer, Mr. Lloyd George and Mr. McKenna, had, by additional 'taxation, raised our normal *revenue of £200,000,000 to £370,000,000, how we raised our first loan of £350,000,000 at 3-1 per cent. and a second one. in July of £600,000,000 at 41 per cent.; how further a loan,in America of 100 millions was raised in the autumn for the joint benefit of France and (*selves; how Treasury Bills and Exchequer Bonds. for C 400,000,000 have been 'issued, and 'how a credit of a further ten millions was arranged :in New York between the bankers there awl London bankers: November saw the inemtion of a scheme, which has since, been perfected, for still further rectifying and supporting the exchange with America. Under this scheme the ' Treasury obtains control, by purchase, or loan with the right to purchase, of a mass of 'American securities contributed .at the invitation of ,Government by companies, firms, and indi- viduals,- throughout the country. The good results of this measure are already apparent. —. Bank Changes.

They had, proceeded Mr. Parr, purchased the old-established private bank of Messrs. Barnard and Co., Bedford. Lord Farquahar had re- signed his directorship on accepting office ,under the 'Crown. Mr. Whalley had resigned as ,general manager, but they retained the use of his great experience and good counsel as deputy chairman, in which capacity he succeeds Mr: Fraser, and the latter still as a director, gave the bank the . advantage of his ripe ex- perience of the City. They were fortunate in 'having Mr. Stewart and Mr. Rae to succeed Mr. Whalley as joint general managers. (Ap- plause.) Liberal Help For The Nation.

Our patriotism, he continued, in subscribing largely for the War Loans has, necessarily altered the proportions usually• observed be- tween the various items in the bank's balance sheet. .(Hear, hear.) In normal times a pru- dent banker will divide his funds, in well-recog- nised proportions,- beiween actual cash and what is termed short money, bills, loans, advances and investments. But these usual proportions were of necessity •upset by the requirements of the war. We subscribed liberally both to • the per cent., Loan -issued in 1914 .and to ihe 4; per cent. ,Loan issued last year. (Hear, hear.) The 31- per cent.' we subsequently conyeriv-1, Os well as our Consols, into the 41 -per • col-. Ic..an, which is the only War Loar we nov hold, but these large subscriptions inevitably swelled .the narmal proportion of our inv6tments, indeed practically doubling them during the year. Thep, too, owing to the patriotism of our de- positors,, Who also subscribed, our deposits were temporarily, but sensibly diminished. At the smile time we were :continuing to. assist our customers to finance their share in the ordinary trade of the country, as well as to finance those particular industries engaged in the manufac- tute of war needs. Our depoSits, after suffering, considerable depletion, occasioned as I have said by. Wai Loan operations on account of our customers show an aggregate of over v,54.000,060—the •highest figure attained, so far. in any of our annual balance the.ets—though it may be of in- terest to you to *now that in the 'course of the year under review still higher figures have been reached: (Applause.)' In common with the balance sheets of many

LANCASHIRE & YORKSHIRE

Mr. Thos. B. Moxon, presiding at the annual meeting of the shareholders of the Lancashire and Yorkshiro Bank, Ltd.; yesterday, said it was something; that at the end of the year they were able to say, .with absolute truth, that thq. position of the bank gave them every satisfac- tion, and that the number of its customers had again increased.

"During the past year," he said, "we have made liberal subscriptions to •the War Loan, besides giving substantial assistance to various schemes for improving the American exchange. This we have done cheerfully, and to a larger extent that the public are aware. you may be surprised to hear that the banks of the kingdom have all together subscribed fully one-third of each of the War Loans that has been brought out, in addition to the large amount of Treasury Bills they have taken. This is quite apart from the amounts taken up by their customers, so that the banks have certainly done their share in providing means for the war, and the confi- dence the public has placed in them has been fully justified. "As you will see from the report, the net profits of the bank for the ; year have been £153,415 18s. Id., plus £21,048 3s. 9d. brought for- ward from last year. 'This enables us to con- tinue our dividend of 16s. a share, less income tax, to add £5,000 to our reserve fund, and to increase the amount of undivided profit carried forward by the sum of £10,400." A fuller report will appear in our next iesue.

CHURCH CLOCKS.

Registers Of Time That Are Indifferent To Punctuality.

The following article was written last week; and the references to times should be read accordingly. "What. is the time?" is a question asked frequently every day, but •Warringtonians will have a difficulty in answering it if they take the church -clocks as their guide. There are' various states of time. English time—apart from Warrington time—is indi- cated from Greenwich. The Frenchmen set their watches from Paris, whirst a German will swear by the time of Berlin. If any clock in Warrington is right, it must be the. Town Clock at Market Gate, and loyal inhabitants must adopt this as their stand-by: The car conductors do,- and those who have cause to complain occasionally about missing trains, do not know whether to blame the Town Clock or its understudies—the conductors' watches. The conductor playing for safety blames the excessive number of passengers for whom he has to wait in a series of false starts. The Parish -Church clock is also one that can be followed—by the trustful; it gallantly tries! to do its duty, and generally—like the car con- ductors' watches—agrees with the Town Clock. What about the other clocks, though? St. Paul's, in Bewsey-road, is by far the worst offender. It actually has four faces, but these, instead of -registering the exact time, seem to do their best to kill time. They seem to be eager to get on too quickly in the world—a very wrong, ambition for a church clock—and to leave others behind. When the Town Clock indicated mid day on Monday, the clock on the east side .of St. Paul's declared that it was sixteen minutes past.- On the west side it wasl really twenty minutes past; on the north 'side it was twenty-seven minutes after noon! The fingers .on the south side do try to control themselves sontewhat better and trot along at a nice steady pace, being only seven minutes in front of the Town Clock.

Bad as St. Paul's is, however, the clock on St. James' Church, Latchford, is, for the time being at all events, worse. It will not get a move. on. For weeks past the hands have been standing at mid-day, and one is reminded of the Biblical text which states: "So the sun stood still in the midst of heaven and hosted not to go down about a whole day." St. Thomas', Stockton Heath, is a fairly reli- able clock; you can generally• trust it not to be More than two minutes behind Warrington. Other churches frequented regularly .by wor- shippers are not .adorned with. timekeeper& But that at the Cemetery has' one. Like poets who die young, it is before its time—by three in in utes.

We do not wish to be unfair, and so we admit at once that these clocks are very much out in the cold—whicli'may account for many of their PI) or tc;o m ings.

LOCAL AMUSEMENTS.

"6000 Y TWO- SHOES."

Splendid Pantomime At The Royal

Court.

Messrs. Russell and Levaine this week present a pantomime of good quality. The show goes with a fine swing, although one might remark that the vocal efforts are not so good, possibly owing to the fact that several of the company have been suffering from colds. However, the element of humour more than amplifies the slight defect of the former, and has delighted large audiences during the week. The title part is admirably portrayed by Miss Rita -Moya, who, in conjunttion with Miss Flo Curran, as Robin Goodfellow, constitute worthy principal boy and girl. Harry Levaine, as Mother Hubbard, is the heart and soul of the production, one item of especial note being his historical burlesque entitled "Josephine." As the ill-fated Napoleon's wife, he is especially funny, and the introduction is a novel one. Several speciality acts are included in the show, which include Talbert° and Douglas, who earn unstinted applause in a knockabout and.equili- brist act. The bevy of dancers named The Regina Troupe also evoke spontaneous applause. Special scenery and stage effects gb to make up a splendid two hours' entertainment. A Repertory Week.

Mr. Baynor's company will pay a welcome return to Warrington next week, and will pro- duce three famous plays, i.e., "Moths," "A Woman of No Importance" and "A Bunch of Violets "—all of which have been written by writers whose names speak for the quality of the productions. Four performances will be given of "Moths," the days being Monday (afternoon and evening), Tuesday and Saturday. This play is adapted from .Ouida's novel of the same name, and has been played by Mr. Ral- nor's company over 3,000 times. On Wednesday and Thursday evenings "A Woman of No Im- portance," one of Oscar Wilde's wittiest plays, will be staged. Friday's play, "A Bunch of Violets," is by the late Sydney Grundy, and it is one that appeals especially to women. But the other sex will find it as interesting as Mr. Raynor's other two productions.

THE HIPPODRONIE.

"The Di% int] " Artistic Aquatic

Feats.

The management of the theatre of varieties have this week secured some attractions which have caused crowded houses at each perform- ance. Top of the bill are the Diving Belles in their exhibition of unique water tricks and acro- batic -diving. The stage effects are splendid, particularly the opening tableau, which depicts these three charming young ladies as mermaids in monster shells. Their entertainment is both pleasing and refined. They are remarkably ex- pert swimmers, and perform artistically. Gillin, the popular and acclaimed first-rate ventrilo- quist, has again been delighting Warrington audiences, his act being as fresh as ever. Jim Kenny and Emmie Aston have merit as a coloured humorist and pianist respectively. The Bell Bros. in a gymnastic act, Royce, Babs and Royce in a novel singing and dancing turn, and Patti Loftus, Irish and Scotch, comedienne, are all items of note. Frank Dickinson, the power- ful Yorkshire basso profundo, rendered two capital songs, the one entitled "Drinking" being especially well given. A particular feature of the performances has been the spontaneous ap- plause accorded to the orchestra, under Mr. 'l'. H. Fiulley, for their splendid rendering of the "Princess Caprice" valse.

THE STAR KINEMA.

An excellent programme was presented at the Star Kinema House during the first half of the week. The leading film, entitled "Stolen Goods," and featuring Blanche Sweet, was, in- deed, a capital picture, the views 9f the burn- ing hospitals, &c., in France, after the visits of hostile aircraft, being remarkably 'vivid. "The Hour of Three," another thrilling episode of the "Exploits of Elaine," in which Craig Kennedy, the noted detective, is opposed to the master criminal, "The Clutching Hand" proved to be very exciting. The views of the anti-aircraft guns in Lorraine, were very interesting and in- forming, as also were the war scenes depicted in " Pathe's Gazette." There was first-class comedy in "The Fighting Kid." • " Infelice," the title of the leading week-end film, has proved a great draw. "The Rosary."

The principal film next week will be "The Rosary," showftig the life of Father Kelly and featuring Miss Kathlyn Williains. It is a most interesting affair, and will be shown throughout the week. Madam J. Armstrong will sing dur- ing each performance.

THE GEM PICTURE HOUSE.

Excellent as was the picture entitled ," Vaga- bond Soldier," which headed the bill at the Gem Picture House during.the first part of last .week, it was surpassed by the leading picture displayed during the first three days of this week. "The Young Ironmaster," for such was the title of the film, was brimful of exciting incidents throughout its three parts, and it was consoling to see the heroine receive her just rewards in the end, after being badly treated and falsely convicted at the instance of a band of thieves, in the early stages of the picture. " Comrades Three" was another very, good pic- ture, while "filly Bow-wow" and "The Counterfeit Cowboy" were the titles of two amusing skits. YOU LIKE " TIIE EXAMINER"?

Of course you do.

But do you get it regularly?

Do you advise your friends to see the good things it contains?

Do you send it to your soldier and sailor boys who are longing for the latest and best news from home?

If you go away visiting for a week or a month do you have it posted to your holiday address? Your newsagent is anxious to have your instructions to send it on to you.

Or we will be glad to post the paper direct from "The Examiner" Office if you will send us your name and address and the necessary cash.

War Letters and Photographs.

We invite our readers to submit to us letters received from their soldier friends. We shall also be glad to receive photo- graphs of soldiers who have been killed, wounded, mentioned in dispatches or other- wise become a source of interest to the public.

The letters will be carefully edited so that nothing will appelw in print that ought not to appear.

NO MORE LAID CR4S There is no surplus labour to deal with laid crops, and every Farmer should rely on standing crops by sowing only Stiff. . /Ai Gartons Sttrawed tS. which include our NEW REGENERATED BREEDS for 1916 of The ecord, The Yielder, Tartar King, Supreme, etc. Thousands of pounds have been lost in past years through laid crops. Then, labour was plentiful and laid crops .were harvested by hand without great trouble or inconvenience. Now, however, it is an impossibility to deal with laid crops, and every farmer must see that he assures for himself a straight- forward harvest by sowing only those varieties which can be relied upon to , stand up to the time of cutting. Send for New Illustrated Catalogue, with full particulars, to GARTON S SEED (Ltd.), EXPERTS, Warrington

the President of the Board of Agriculture and Fisheries and the President of the Board •of Trade, to suspend for the present the issue of licences for the export of sulphate of ammonia. Udder normal ceitiditions it is well known that the production of sulphate of ammonia consider- ably exceeds home requirements, hut Lord Set- borne confidently hopes that farmers will this year greatly increase their demands for fer- tilisers of all descriptions, so as to stimulate so far as practicable the production from the land, and thus reduce the importation of foodstuffs. This stimulus is the more necessary to counter- balance in some degree the hindrances to pro- duction arising from shortage of labour and other causes. Lord Selborne therefore appeals to farmers generally to justify his belief in their enterprise and patriotism by availing themselves of the opportunity now provided to secure plen- tiful supplies of fertilisers. Farmers are especially urged to place their orders for sulphate of ammonia at once, so as to enable the expected increased demand to be met without undue delay or difficulty.

DEITH OF MR. R. CORBETT. Wesleyanism Loses Prominent And Energetic Worker. We regret to announce that Mr. Reginald Corbett died at his residence, 17, Wilson-Patten- street, on Tuesday, after a long and painful illness, patiently borne. He underwent an operation and treatment in a nursing home in June of last year, and for a time he .enjoyed better health, but his malady returned. The deceased gentleman, whose age was 43 years, for a considerable period was engaged as commercial traveller for the firm of Messrs. Edwin Richardson and Son, grocers, Bridge-

ON HONOUR'S SCROLL. PRIVATE R. FELLOWS. —7-- Buried In Trench, But Progressing Favourably. News has been received by Mr. and Mrs. C. F. Fellows, 18, Pickmere-street, that their son, Ralph, of the Public Schools' Battalica of the Royal Fusiliers, has been buried by a falling trench at the Front, and is now suffering from shock. The following communication from Private Fellows conveyed the news:— " You will be surprised to receive this card. but don't be more than surprised. While sit- ting in the trench on Sunday afternoon, writing a letter, a shell came and hit the trench, knockeck it in, and buried me. Luckily, when dug out, I was only suffering from shock and bruises. ,My right leg caught it the worst. My

RIFLEMAN T. WARING.

must have been a mistake about our doings of the 14th. We were carrying rations along a road, when a bullet came and hit my rifle, then skidded off and went through another man's hand. It was a good job for me that the bullet hit the rifle, for, had it not done so, it would have hit my side. All the boys said I was lucky." Rifleman Waring, who .is 26 years of age, has had had an adventurous career while at the Front. He has been wounded tWi1245," and this is the second time that his rifle has been hit with a bullet. He enlisted shortly after the outbreak of war, having previously been em- ployed in the galvanising department at Messrs. Rylands's Works. LATEST CASUALTIES. SOUTH LANCASHIRE REGIMENT, 2nd Battalion. Previously missing, now reported killed o) died.—Pte. W." J. Viggor, 8811. THE SENTRY. I saw a sentry on the hill. His bayonet fixed, his posture still; A silhouette against the sky. " A bit of England 'tis," said I; And carelessly I passed him by. A raging storm came rolling on And all the world was woe-begone. Green shelering trees were rooted nigh, But still he stood against-the sky. "A bit of England list" said I, t" Firm as a rock 'mid tempests high." I passed the hill in early morn Amid the mounting flush of dawn. Fair was the landscape to the eye. The sentry stobd against the sky: f‘A bit of England 'Us," said I.

PROMOTIONS AND DISTINCTIONS. A IVARRIN6TON D.C.M. The official details of how Lance-Corporal Wm. Keating, of the 11th Lancashire Fusiliers, and of 68, Catherine-street, won the Distin- guished Conduct Medal, appeared in the "Lon- don Gazette" on Saturday. The "Gazette" states that the D.C.M. was awarded For conspicuous gallantry on the night of December 9-10, 1915, north of Warneton rail- way. Lance-Corporals Keating and Bed- dews remained out for three hours and 20 minutes cutting wire in front of and on the enemy's parapet in preparation for an assault. They were subjected to heavy machine gun and rifle fire. BATTERY SERGEANT-MAJOR W. J. PRICE, D.C.M.
